- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C11-1|MT038-C11-1]] — Swapping carry for trend's relative value preserves defensiveness only if the tilt is market neutral in volatility space.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C12-2|MT038-C12-2]] — In practice one can keep native relative-value trend and overlay carry or resize it; the paper's replacement was illustrative.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C12-3|MT038-C12-3]] — Trend portfolios typically run about 3.5-4x risk leverage, and adding more relative-value could increase leverage and trading costs.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C12-4|MT038-C12-4]] — Tilting within asset classes reduces diversification, which could lower the leverage needed, so the net effect is ambiguous.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C13-1|MT038-C13-1]] — Carry built long/short with equal volatility within an asset class avoids the usual poor crisis performance.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C13-4|MT038-C13-4]] — Investors like layering another strategy on top of 100% trend (portable alpha) because it gives more risk for fees, especially when they cannot lever.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C14-1|MT038-C14-1]] — In the March crisis execution reduced longs and shorts separately, when the right action was to cut the dominant duration factor immediately.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C14-2|MT038-C14-2]] — Execution trades off passivity (lower spread cost) against accumulated risk of holding the order.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C14-4|MT038-C14-4]] — CTAs typically start passive and become aggressive as the deadline nears, leaving risk when the order is largest.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C14-5|MT038-C14-5]] — Almgren's finding: be aggressive first and passive later, since risk is highest at the start.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C15-1|MT038-C15-1]] — In pair orders, low-correlation legs (soybean vs oil) can be executed independently, but highly correlated legs (2y/10y) must be coordinated to avoid beta risk.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C15-2|MT038-C15-2]] — Different trading hours (Asia vs US bonds) create hedge mismatch in pair orders.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C15-3|MT038-C15-3]] — Because trend's information coefficient is low, routine rebalances should not be traded aggressively.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C15-4|MT038-C15-4]] — Crisis risk reduction should be handled separately from alpha execution.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C16-1|MT038-C16-1]] — When one market is closed and correlation is high, hedge with the cheapest available asset then rebalance.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C16-2|MT038-C16-2]] — In alternative markets trade passively over multiple days.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C16-3|MT038-C16-3]] — OTC and commodity markets (freight) need broker execution that cannot be fully automated.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C16-5|MT038-C16-5]] — A static correlation assumption is a flaw in execution models, since correlations change in crises.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C16-6|MT038-C16-6]] — Discretionary macro traders in crises first cut risk in the most liquid assets (S&P, crude, Treasuries), then refine by view.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C17-1|MT038-C17-1]] — The correlation estimate used to execute may be stale exactly when it is needed in a crisis.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C17-2|MT038-C17-2]] — Transaction costs rise sharply when volatility spikes, complicating crisis execution.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C17-3|MT038-C17-3]] — Intraday correlation spikes (Shephard) signal risk-off because markets are becoming dysfunctional.

## [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve]]
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C02-1|MT039-C02-1]] — A rules-based approach lets investors cope with information overflow in volatile news-driven markets, which discretionary investors cannot process.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C06-2|MT039-C06-2]] — Liquid QIS strategies can act as a liquidity reserve to fund private-equity capital calls.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C06-3|MT039-C06-3]] — Risk (volatility) can be measured and managed; uncertainty (the unknown) cannot, which is the challenge investors face.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C12-2|MT039-C12-2]] — Switching between trend managers loses loss carry-forward (high-water marks) and fees, hurting performance.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C14-2|MT039-C14-2]] — Observed paper returns are not always capturable because of transaction costs (limits to arbitrage).
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C15-3|MT039-C15-3]] — Sizing beyond risk (such as growth level) is the hard part of economic trend.

## [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4]]
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C06-2|MT040-C06-2]] — Short-term trend is kept for fast reactivity and convexity going into risk-off periods.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C07-1|MT040-C07-1]] — Longer-term trend managers can cut exposure quickly (e.g. in the yen after intervention), so positioning narratives are unreliable.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C12-X1|MT040-C12-X1]] — Even trend's strong decade (2020-2026) included a period in which many managers came close to their largest drawdown, so good aggregate returns came with deep interim losses.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C16-X1|MT040-C16-X1]] — Unlike 60/40 portfolios, which had about seven distinct 'lost' periods averaging roughly 11 years in GMO's chart, long-standing trend managers have rarely if ever had a 10-year period with no return.

## [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner]]
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C11-X1|MT041-C11-X1]] — When a new tariff is announced, a public-equity investor's first step is to map which holdings source from or sell into the targeted country, since the short-term hit falls on those supply chains.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C18-4|MT041-C18-4]] — Under downside risk a manager should concentrate on the best ideas and understand them rather than add marginal positions.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C18-X1|MT041-C18-X1]] — A long-horizon investor may stay fully invested through policy noise on the AI super-cycle thesis, unlike a hedge-fund risk manager who cuts risk; the right response depends on horizon.

## [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385]]
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C03-1|MT042-C03-1]] — Trend followers' returns differ mainly by how much risk they allocate to the few big trends, which depends on their market set.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C04-2|MT042-C04-2]] — Volatility-targeted CTAs cut positions when asset volatility rises, giving profit taking and spreading risk across metals.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C04-X1|MT042-C04-X1]] — Trend followers that do not volatility-adjust keep full size as volatility explodes in a trending market (metals, January 2026), so they earn far more than vol-targeted CTAs in such a month, at the cost of larger risk.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C05-1|MT042-C05-1]] — Trend following does best and is least exposed to reversal when profits come from many sectors at once, as in January 2026.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C05-3|MT042-C05-3]] — On equity down days other trend positions (dollar, metals) offset, showing trend portfolios are not just equity exposure.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C12-2|MT042-C12-2]] — Volatility estimation sizes positions as risk, not alpha, but changes the return path depending on how fast it adjusts.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C12-3|MT042-C12-3]] — CTAs treat each asset as a unit of risk and size by volatility and signal strength, not by notional value.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C12-4|MT042-C12-4]] — In 2022 bond volatility doubled (about 4 to 8); slow vol estimates kept larger positions in the bond downtrend and captured more of it, while fast estimates cut risk early.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C12-5|MT042-C12-5]] — In 2025 slow vol estimates were better in H1 and fast better in H2, so speed of vol adjustment helps or hurts depending on path.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C13-1|MT042-C13-1]] — Short vol windows cut risk quickly in events like COVID, SVB, Liberation Day.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C13-2|MT042-C13-2]] — Cutting risk fast on a shock and restoring it fast later captures more; downsizing and resizing speeds need not be equal.

## [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20]]
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C03-3|MT043-C03-3]] — Sectors are traded only when a supply-demand story gives an attractive risk-reward, and can sit idle for years.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C04-5|MT043-C04-5]] — Passive commodity index funds and ETFs lose money; USO and UNG with a static roll lost everything, USO especially when WTI went negative.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C06-2|MT043-C06-2]] — Portfolio holds 5-20 strategies in 2-4 themes with limits per theme, concentrated but not all-in on one.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C06-3|MT043-C06-3]] — Risk is held back from sectors without a supply-demand story, such as grains in 2017-2019, when they were well supplied at full carry.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C06-4|MT043-C06-4]] — Positions are cut in stages as the risk budget is drawn down, then closed and sidelined for a period.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C06-5|MT043-C06-5]] — The fund targets about 21% annualised volatility and runs at no more than half of that when out of step with markets after drawdown.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C07-1|MT043-C07-1]] — Cutting the volatility target after a loss slows recovery, so losses are especially costly.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C07-2|MT043-C07-2]] — Under volatility targeting, spikes (cocoa, coffee, WTI negative) force position cuts so the fund cannot hold full size on big moves.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C09-2|MT043-C09-2]] — Fund cut the oil short when ISIS risk emerged in Iraq, and re-entered once the thesis held.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C09-3|MT043-C09-3]] — After the Iraq shock, adding back to the short was right: global growth disappointed and surpluses built.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C09-6|MT043-C09-6]] — In the LME nickel squeeze the exchange cancelled trades after the market opened, so exchange intervention risk exists in extreme squeezes.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C09-R1|MT043-C09-R1]] — In the LME nickel squeeze the fund sold nearly everything after the price had doubled in a morning, then the exchange cancelled trades it did not honour.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C10-2|MT043-C10-2]] — Physical trading in 2014-17 had poor margins and high counterparty credit risk, which makes it less attractive than futures.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C12-1|MT043-C12-1]] — Global risk-off days (Liberation Day) force de-risking even when the commodity story is strong, repeatedly cutting and rebuilding positions.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C12-X1|MT043-C12-X1]] — Multi-manager platforms run commodity PMs on very tight drawdown leashes (out after a few failures within months), unlike a single-team fund that can sit through noise around a long-term supply-demand theme.

## [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor]]
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C03-4|MT044-C03-4]] — Rob's trend signals were weak in mid-2025 (long equities, a little short bonds, flat energies), so the system took low risk instead of piling on positions.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C04-1|MT044-C04-1]] — Rob's energy position is net flat (short gas, long crude), with no big risk coming on.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C04-4|MT044-C04-4]] — A one standard deviation drawdown for trend is small compared with equity bear markets of about four standard deviations.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C05-1|MT044-C05-1]] — A strategy can be worth holding through a flat spell if it delivers uncorrelated returns in the long run.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C09-1|MT044-C09-1]] — Full Kelly says run annualised volatility equal to Sharpe ratio to maximise CAGR, assuming Gaussian returns, known Sharpe and infinite leverage; at Sharpe 0.5 that is 50% vol.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C09-2|MT044-C09-2]] — Practical volatility targets (Rob's is under half of Kelly) are well below full Kelly because the Sharpe is uncertain.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C09-3|MT044-C09-3]] — Negative skew requires a lower Kelly-type volatility target and positive skew a higher one.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C09-4|MT044-C09-4]] — Positive skew raises the optimal volatility target by less than people think, so it cannot be converted into higher returns easily.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C10-1|MT044-C10-1]] — Position sizing studies (Saratini and Quantica) find that both volatility-adjusting positions during a trade and scaling with forecast strength help in a long backtest, with small differences.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C10-2|MT044-C10-2]] — Higher Sharpe gives higher CAGR with leverage unless it costs too much positive skew.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C10-3|MT044-C10-3]] — Fixed-stop sizing that ignores changing volatility adds positive skew but loses too much Sharpe, giving lower CAGR.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C11-1|MT044-C11-1]] — Concentration lifts portfolio volatility above the intended target, so the manager is over-risked at peaks.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C11-2|MT044-C11-2]] — A well-known trend manager with static sizing reached about 90% volatility, which implies a belief in a Sharpe of 0.9 or more, about twice a realistic 0.5.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C11-3|MT044-C11-3]] — Nobody can forecast near-term Sharpe accurately enough to justify doubling the risk target.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C14-3|MT044-C14-3]] — Cost-aware portfolio optimisation across many illiquid markets separates the profitable from less profitable managers.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C14-4|MT044-C14-4]] — Rob's mistakes were trading too quickly and over-concentrating, and he stresses avoiding errors.

## [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356]]
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C03-3|MT045-C03-3]] — The commodity sector provides diversification that most investors do not hold directly, which CTAs supply.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C06-2|MT045-C06-2]] — A blend of trend following and the S&P 500 gives nearly the sum of both returns (portable alpha), explaining the stream of new overlay products.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C08-5|MT045-C08-5]] — CTA index drawdowns of about 20% are small next to a 55% S&P 500 drawdown.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C10-2|MT045-C10-2]] — Institutions build layered defensive portfolios of different diversifiers, trend as one layer.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C10-3|MT045-C10-3]] — Evaluate trend by its portfolio effect, not stand-alone Sharpe.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C16-3|MT045-C16-3]] — Replication can be a separate strategy combined with CTAs to reduce dispersion.

## [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio]]
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C04-X1|MT046-C04-X1]] — He treats a macro forecast as a probability weighting that he acts on through his process but abandons when evidence proves it wrong, citing his mistaken post-1987 depression call.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C05-4|MT046-C05-4]] — Shorts have asymmetric downside (losses can exceed 100% of capital), and he is not sure he has ever made money on shorts in 40 years, so shorting is a game for professionals with caution on sizing.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C07-2|MT046-C07-2]] — Success depends on knowing when not to bet; multi-asset flexibility lets him move to bonds and currencies when equities are uncertain, as volatility in them rises when the world is blowing up.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C08-2|MT046-C08-2]] — Avoid making big bets while down and uncertain; preserve capital until conviction is high, since bad drawdown states lead to poor decisions.

## [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340]]
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C04-1|MT047-C04-1]] — A leveraged single-stock ETF (2x Tesla, short Ford) lost two-thirds in five weeks, showing the risk of financially engineered ETF products.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C10-1|MT047-C10-1]] — Equity drawdowns can be deep and long: the Depression fell 79% in real terms and took 15.5 years to recover, so buy-and-hold has a worst case that matters near retirement.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-1|MT047-C11-1]] — In the long histogram of annual returns, 2022 bonds were down over 30% (real), a standout loss, while equity annual returns are skewed with 10-20% most frequent.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-2|MT047-C11-2]] — Equity drawdowns were 56% in 1973-74, similar in the tech bubble, and 52% in the GFC (5.3 years to recover); the worst case wait for equities is 16 years in real terms.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-3|MT047-C11-3]] — Equity drawdowns last longer than typical CTA drawdowns, countering the common criticism that trend following drawdowns are long.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-4|MT047-C11-4]] — Worst-case recovery from a drawdown in real terms was 16 years for US equities but 80 years for bonds.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-X1|MT047-C11-X1]] — Recovery time after the trough understates the full pain of an equity drawdown, because the slide into the trough also takes years; recent quick recoveries (GFC 5.3 years) may lull investors.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C15-2|MT047-C15-2]] — For a $3-4bn trend program with about $1bn in commodities, capacity limits reduce the Sharpe (the transcript says 177%, likely 17.7%, about 0.16), so the sweet spot may be below $3-4bn or even below $1bn.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C15-4|MT047-C15-4]] — Low-fee replication ETFs must scale to be profitable, which forces fewer, more liquid markets and hurts diversification, so size has a performance cost.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C16-1|MT047-C16-1]] — Size limits commodity diversification, which may also reduce crisis alpha when clients need it most.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C17-3|MT047-C17-3]] — Programs trading hundreds of markets with billions in AUM deviate from equal-risk allocation, and many markets add more to marketing narrative than to risk or performance; Niels has never seen data showing hundreds of markets beat 70.

## [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge]]
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C01-3|MT048-C01-3]] — A public-markets manager rechecks risk-reward daily, as price rises can come with deteriorating conditions, even while holding a longer horizon in venture.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C02-1|MT048-C02-1]] — Size the net market exposure (units of risk) by the skew of expected outcomes, cutting it when the payoff skew worsens, like bet-sizing in poker.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C03-1|MT048-C03-1]] — Cutting from 9 to 3 units of risk is done by selling longs, adding hedges or adding shorts, not by exiting the market.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C03-2|MT048-C03-2]] — Long holding periods (about 3 years public, 10 years venture) are combined with continuous monitoring and resizing when risk-reward changes.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C09-4|MT048-C09-4]] — Microsoft and Meta CFOs said they can flex data-centre shell build-outs (15-year assets) if revenue lags, reducing capex downside.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C14-1|MT048-C14-1]] — At all-time highs with limited upside, tail risks (assassination attempt, geopolitics) justify cutting exposure because rare shocks can hit anytime.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C15-1|MT048-C15-1]] — When prices are high, reduce units of risk rather than exit; the recession risk is the most important of the risks.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C21-1|MT048-C21-1]] — Gurley's venture firm declined to underwrite Character AI because the likely outcome was a sale to Google or Meta, since breakout was very hard.

## [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C04-1|MT049-C04-1]] — Treating a bank balance sheet as a set of asset and liability portfolios with different speeds (repricing/duration) lets you manage net interest margin as a portfolio problem.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C06-1|MT049-C06-1]] — Companies carry hidden market exposures (e.g. a jeweler is long gold), so profits blend operating performance and market bets; risk-management plans separate and hedge the latter.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C06-4|MT049-C06-4]] — Moving from a single-market exposure to a global long/short mandate lets you exploit any market and avoid being a sitting duck to one price.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C06-X1|MT049-C06-X1]] — A company can hedge its embedded market exposures on the liability side by structuring its debt (currency, maturity, rate type) to offset what its business revenues are exposed to, as Bridgewater did for BC Hydro.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C07-1|MT049-C07-1]] — Kodak's CIO worried that a diversified portfolio would fall together in a deflationary depression; a small allocation of long-duration (25-year) bonds could deliver the price action of five times the capital as a hedge.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C07-2|MT049-C07-2]] — The first alpha-overlay mandate was implemented with futures: 25-year target duration, 2-2.5x leverage, duration flexible from 40 years to zero, currency exposure plus or minus 100% and any country, combining liability hedge and alpha.

## [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting]]
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C03-1|MT050-C03-1]] — Zulauf concentrates and moves to extreme positioning when he sees big risks or opportunities, rather than staying near benchmark constraints.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C03-4|MT050-C03-4]] — Zulauf's hedge funds ran long/short with only about 30% leverage but could be net 100% short, so exposure was flexible in both directions.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C05-1|MT050-C05-1]] — He trades only when odds turn in his favour and otherwise stays out, so selectivity and waiting matter more than being always positioned.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C07-2|MT050-C07-2]] — Always keep a failsafe stop-loss and stay humble: a large loss unbalances decision-making and is hard to recover.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C07-3|MT050-C07-3]] — Large losses are hard to recover (needed gains grow nonlinearly), which matters even more in a performance game.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C07-4|MT050-C07-4]] — His biggest loss was a position held without a stop that was finally closed down 50% when he was about 30.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C07-R1|MT050-C07-R1]] — He is in a performance game only for his subscribers, not for the portfolios he manages, which hold his own money.

## [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management]]
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C03-5|MT051-C03-5]] — Risk should be judged as how risky and what expected return versus compensation, not as good or bad; intelligent risk-taking needs quantification, diversification and adequate payment.
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C05-1|MT051-C05-1]] — A superior investor knows the distribution of outcomes and when the odds favour playing (e.g. 75% good outcomes vs 25% bad) and judges that upside more than compensates for downside.

## [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C01-1|MT053-C01-1]] — A true hedge fund aims for returns uncorrelated with the market, which helps a portfolio because the stream rises when other assets fall; many so-called hedge funds are simply long-only.

## [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund]]
- [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund#^MT054-C03-1|MT054-C03-1]] — Being right on the thesis does not help if timing is wrong: his shorts on companies that later went bankrupt first soared in a rising market and wiped him out, so the market is harder to read than the analysis and there is more emotion than expected.
- [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund#^MT054-C06-R3|MT054-C06-R3]] — Timing is part of investing; knowing facts such as the price of copper is necessary but not sufficient.
- [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund#^MT054-C08-1|MT054-C08-1]] — He has no rule for cutting losses: some use stop-losses (e.g. out if it falls 12%), he holds through the pain until he sees he made a mistake.

## [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395]]
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C05-1|MT056-C05-1]] — On a day when markets moved against CTA positioning, CTA returns were only slightly negative, which Niels attributes to risk management discipline.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C05-4|MT056-C05-4]] — The replication model had no energy exposure at end-2025, added it from January as trends emerged, took a hit in March, and gradually de-risked positions that stopped working, including equity risk.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C06-1|MT056-C06-1]] — Rather than full crash-protection mode, the manager partially de-risks (shin guards and pads) when positions reverse in an unstable market.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C06-X1|MT056-C06-X1]] — In the March 2026 environment the trend book held offsetting pairs (long crude with long gold, long international stocks against short S&P), so headline swings partly netted out across positions.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C09-4|MT056-C09-4]] — Use a complex model to detect the signal but a second decision (e.g. PCA/clustering) to implement it in fewer, more liquid contracts, such as one contract for global equity exposure.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C10-2|MT056-C10-2]] — Intraday or high-frequency trend models may show very high Sharpe but only at tiny capacity, so signal quality must be weighed against implementation capacity.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C10-3|MT056-C10-3]] — Gross excess return is worthless if the cost to implement it, including conditional liquidity, equals the gain; signal and implementation must be evaluated together.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C11-2|MT056-C11-2]] — True implementation costs (bid-ask, market impact in thin markets such as Chinese power or South African soybeans) are the unspoken problem of CTAs; quants are the dumb money that physical traders like Louis Dreyfus and Glencore pick off.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C11-4|MT056-C11-4]] — Implementation costs rise geometrically with the number of positions, so by position 180 or 350 the marginal market is a coin toss on whether it trends enough to pay for its costs.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C18-2|MT056-C18-2]] — Reported fee comparisons ignore swap and trading costs; DBMF at 85 bp with near-zero trading cost competes against 170 bp mutual funds with about 200 bp trading costs.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C19-1|MT056-C19-1]] — UCITS fund transaction-cost calculations measure execution versus settlement, so pushing the price up while buying shows a negative cost, and some funds report zero costs when real costs are a couple of hundred bp.

## [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300]]
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C03-3|MT057-C03-3]] — CFD trading carries broker spreads, slippage and holding (swap) costs, which are larger than futures costs and depend on broker margin-making rather than centralized exchanges.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C03-5|MT057-C03-5]] — Trends in assets with negative swap make long holds expensive, so swap costs can materially reduce performance and should be monitored on a dashboard.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C03-X1|MT057-C03-X1]] — For long-holding trend systems, cash CFDs give access to more markets than dated CFDs but carry daily overnight financing, so the choice depends on monitoring that holding cost against the wider market set.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C04-2|MT057-C04-2]] — When prices and ATR rise several-fold over decades, keep a constant dollar risk per trade (stop = multiple of 25-day ATR) and let contract counts change; large early position counts are expected, not a bug.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C11-2|MT057-C11-2]] — Niels speculates the same methods could extend to optimal position sizing, and says his firm already uses dynamic parameter selection.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C13-1|MT057-C13-1]] — Short-term high-alpha models have capacity limits from market impact; the scalable external equity fund used medium-to-long-term trend models and performed only marginally.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C13-3|MT057-C13-3]] — Trend following works only when diversified across many markets and sectors; single-market trend following does not work.

## [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C04-2|MT058-C04-2]] — Combining good, uncorrelated bets can cut portfolio risk by up to 80% without lowering expected return.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C10-2|MT058-C10-2]] — Avoid places with extreme left-right internal conflict, where history shows losses beyond price moves, including confiscation.

## [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming]]
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C01-2|MT059-C01-2]] — Power P&L is chunky and volatile with five to ten trades making most of the year, so no monthly profit target is sensible.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C03-4|MT059-C03-4]] — Two traders trading the same product with the same data reduces missed information and forces discussion of what each sees.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C07-1|MT059-C07-1]] — Give traders a platform of grid fundamentals so that losses can be explained after the fact and ideas nudged back on track.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C07-2|MT059-C07-2]] — When moving from a firm to own capital, keep identical information and screens so the only change is risk, not reduced information.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C08-2|MT059-C08-2]] — Power P&L can swing within five minutes because prices are set in real time, creating a stressful position-sizing environment.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C09-1|MT059-C09-1]] — Most trades are for learning and staying in the market; the goal is to preserve capital and knowledge to size up on the rare big setup.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C09-2|MT059-C09-2]] — Lean years occur even with good trading because setups are absent; forcing trades without a setup is the worst mistake.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C09-X1|MT059-C09-X1]] — Traders who chase maximum variance because their bonus has no downside are not rewarded by the market over the long run, so screening for that mindset matters as much as pay design.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C10-1|MT059-C10-1]] — The firm adds a put-option-like loss feature to trader compensation to limit blowups, imperfectly, and removes uncertainty about getting paid.

## [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit]]
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C02-3|MT061-C02-3]] — Leverage losses at hedge funds are not systemic; the question that matters is what the same assets do to heavily levered banks (Lehman 36x, Bear 29x).
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C03-1|MT061-C03-1]] — A 30x levered balance sheet holding assets that fall 40% is wiped out arithmetically; the housing bust was a math problem for banks.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C04-3|MT061-C04-3]] — He structured the Japan view as two-thirds in JGB options (bet on the bond market breaking) and one-third in a weaker-yen bet.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C04-4|MT061-C04-4]] — Abenomics took the yen from 85 to 120; the option bet lost its premium but the yen leg returned many multiples, giving the fund about 250% overall: convex payoff when the fork resolved one way.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C07-4|MT061-C07-4]] — Rare-earth magnet shortages hit firms such as Ford because they ignored a long visible risk of dependence on China.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C11-2|MT061-C11-2]] — Conflict risk means US investors should not own Chinese assets; investing in communism has not worked long term.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C15-2|MT061-C15-2]] — He avoids short-term trading in structurally risky markets and prefers long-term investments in companies that can succeed.

## [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin]]
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C02-1|MT062-C02-1]] — Forecasting which hedge fund strategy will do best is too hard, so the allocator diversifies across a breadth of strategies.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C02-7|MT062-C02-7]] — In a volatile policy environment allocators should lean into positively convex, volatility-friendly strategies.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C04-2|MT062-C04-2]] — Discretionary macro is concentrated: usually a few ideas per year produce most of the P&L.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C04-5|MT062-C04-5]] — CPPIB uses hedge fund macro to diversify away from security selection into asset selection through relative value and directional trend capture.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C05-1|MT062-C05-1]] — Discretionary macro convexity arrives from one or two big ideas a year, so timing and conviction matter.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C05-X1|MT062-C05-X1]] — The allocator holds discretionary and systematic macro together to diversify along three axes at once: asset classes, styles and timing horizons.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-1|MT062-C06-1]] — Hold more than two but fewer than ten systematic managers: too many dilutes returns, a single one gives an idiosyncratic experience (a 30-day commodity manager vs others); concentrate in the best over time.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-7|MT062-C06-7]] — CFM uses a committee for non-model risks (regulation, elections, DeepSeek), since markets are nonstationary and nonlinear.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-8|MT062-C06-8]] — Never override signals, but occasionally constrain risk models and later codify the logic into the system.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-R1|MT062-C06-R1]] — Even a high-frequency systematic firm retains a metaphorical button operated by a human, because markets are nonstationary and nonlinear.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-2|MT062-C07-2]] — Quant firms run a daily risk meeting to understand system positions; interventions in disruptions are risk responses, never alpha.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-R1|MT062-C07-R1]] — Markets are highly nonstationary, so humans must watch systems even when the process is not overridden.

## [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324]]
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C03-1|MT063-C03-1]] — Trend following is a core allocation that should be held regardless of equity performance, especially when equities are strong and could turn.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C04-2|MT063-C04-2]] — Before binary events such as elections or Brexit, trend followers cannot know which side they will be on; in November 2024 they happened to be on the right side.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C04-6|MT063-C04-6]] — A difficult year (strong start, weak summer, rally late) showed the value of rules and full diversification across markets.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C07-2|MT063-C07-2]] — Converging ensembles justify focused resource allocation, while divergent ensembles mean high uncertainty and call for wider distribution of resources.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C08-1|MT063-C08-1]] — An ensemble of short, medium and long trend models is more probabilistic and less prescriptive, capturing more of a trend even if some models fail.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C08-3|MT063-C08-3]] — Differences among trend followers come mostly from exits and in-trade risk management rather than entries.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C09-6|MT063-C09-6]] — Druckenmiller attributes success to concentrated bets, and willingness to embrace concentration and volatility pays off even though investors fear portfolios converging on one theme.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C09-X1|MT063-C09-X1]] — A former Man colleague argued that trading more markets does not necessarily bring more diversification and can bring the opposite, because positions in many markets can load on the same theme.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C10-1|MT063-C10-1]] — Ensemble trend following adds models as a trend matures, concentrating on big winners in a rules-based way rather than by hindsight; it needs concentrated risk on winners.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C10-2|MT063-C10-2]] — Market diversification is a watch list: take a small allocation when an outlier emerges and add as it endures, without holding positions in all markets.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C10-4|MT063-C10-4]] — VaR, Sharpe ratios and position sizing such as Kelly use the past to allocate risk, which fosters false security and misses forward-looking vulnerabilities.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C10-5|MT063-C10-5]] — Risk should be defined as vulnerability (what could happen) instead of historical variance of returns.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C11-2|MT063-C11-2]] — Positive convexity (disproportionate gain in favorable conditions, limited loss in downturns) is the antidote to fragility.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C11-3|MT063-C11-3]] — Standard deviation and Sharpe did not reveal warehoused risk in funds that later collapsed, which looked low-risk historically.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C11-4|MT063-C11-4]] — Strong downside protection (brakes) lets a portfolio take more risk when conditions are favorable, a dual barbell approach.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C12-1|MT063-C12-1]] — Backward-looking risk metrics underweight unprecedented events such as the 2020 pandemic.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C12-5|MT063-C12-5]] — A barbell (protect against losses on one side, stay open to unlimited upside on the other) balances conservative risk management with aggressive opportunity pursuit.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C12-6|MT063-C12-6]] — Volatility targeting based on historical measures is shortsighted compared with convex portfolio management.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C12-X1|MT063-C12-X1]] — Traditional risk models prioritise precision over preparedness and aim at volatility reduction rather than resilience, so risk work should shift to understanding vulnerabilities and building portfolios that do well under uncertainty.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C12-X2|MT063-C12-X2]] — Managers use two risk frameworks, a portfolio-level VaR approach and a market-by-market stop-loss approach, and his observation from manager conversations is that larger managers have evolved toward VaR-type risk control rather than stops.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C13-2|MT063-C13-2]] — Trend followers say volatility is not risk, while other strategies rely on volatility measures.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C13-3|MT063-C13-3]] — History is a small sample of possible outcomes, so future risks can exceed anything in the record.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C13-5|MT063-C13-5]] — Large managers' rolling 12-month volatility is not stable, so they target risk rather than volatility.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C14-2|MT063-C14-2]] — Risk is quantifiable but uncertainty is not, and it appears at inopportune times, usually when things look stable.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C16-4|MT063-C16-4]] — Plans and solutions should be adaptive rather than static because conditions change.

## [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD]]
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C05-2|MT064-C05-2]] — Leveraged ETFs are dangerous for long holding because volatility drag makes down-then-up moves end below the start, worse with leverage.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C06-1|MT064-C06-1]] — A leveraged ETF on a very volatile underlying such as Strategy (a levered bitcoin proxy) would be dragged toward zero over time.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C06-2|MT064-C06-2]] — Appropriate leverage depends on the Kelly criterion, which uses expected performance of the asset or strategy.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C06-3|MT064-C06-3]] — Rule of thumb: risk above about 20-30% volatility is probably more than justified by expected performance, so 2x S&P ETFs (30-40% vol) are too much while 1.5x 60/40 (about 12-15% vol) is fine.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C07-1|MT064-C07-1]] — Adding markets gives more diversification than adding similar trend systems because of correlation.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C07-2|MT064-C07-2]] — A new market has correlation about 0.4-0.5 to existing ones, whereas another trend system has about 0.8-0.9.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C07-3|MT064-C07-3]] — Once a portfolio has many markets, a different style (e.g. carry, correlation about 0.5) adds more than further markets.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C07-4|MT064-C07-4]] — Adding systems needs no extra capital if automated, adding markets does, though complexity rises.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-1|MT064-C11-1]] — Execution matters more as assets grow; a big fund trading billions notional should invest in execution.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-5|MT064-C11-5]] — Risk management and risk forecasting offer the most room for evolution in trend following.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-6|MT064-C11-6]] — Trend followers rarely blow up unless they run highly leveraged versions.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-X1|MT064-C11-X1]] — A small trader can get decent execution from a simple algorithm only a few lines long, whereas execution becomes a major research area only for funds trading hundreds of millions or billions of notional a day.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C12-1|MT064-C12-1]] — AHL says 35-40% of research budget goes to execution to avoid losing out as inflows grow, raising strategy capacity.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C16-1|MT064-C16-1]] — Three position-sizing methods: fixed contracts, fixed notional, and fixed risk (volatility-scaled); Quantica finds volatility adjustment has the highest Sharpe.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C16-2|MT064-C16-2]] — Volatility-adjusted sizing has the highest Sharpe ratio (cocoa 2024 example).
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C16-3|MT064-C16-3]] — Fixed-contract or fixed-notional sizing produces more positive skew (explosive moves like cocoa hit an unscaled position), as losers are closed.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C17-5|MT064-C17-5]] — The lower Sharpe from constant-contract or constant-notional sizing cannot be justified by skew improvement.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C18-2|MT064-C18-2]] — Be cautious of high-Sharpe strategies that need much leverage or are opaque, since the back test may hide negative skew risk (Madoff).
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C19-4|MT064-C19-4]] — Trading at 5-minute mean reversion horizons needs large engineering and testing effort.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C20-4|MT064-C20-4]] — Trend following at short frequencies is hard because trading costs are hard to overcome.

## [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar]]
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C02-1|MT065-C02-1]] — Healthy private-sector balance sheets (low corporate debt, strong banks after tougher regulation, decent household savings) mean the downturn may not trigger negative spirals as in past recessions.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C05-5|MT065-C05-5]] — The 2022 bear market is cyclical rather than structural, not deeper than usual because no broad bubbles and banks have low leverage.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C07-2|MT065-C07-2]] — Goldman's 2022 recommended mix: quality balance sheets, defensive growth and low-valued commodity-related stocks.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C07-3|MT065-C07-3]] — Going forward be agnostic to growth versus value and diversify across geography, sector and style.

## [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It]]
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C02-2|MT066-C02-2]] — Macro funds delivered positive returns even in the weak decade and diversification when needed in 2022, so allocating to them was not costly.
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C04-4|MT066-C04-4]] — Risk approach: trade mostly very liquid markets, with daily risk committee, so positions can be exited cheaply and early rather than late at greater expense.
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C05-3|MT066-C05-3]] — Two-layer structure: quant systems hold positions weeks to months while discretionary traders flip within days, staying open-minded to price information.
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C05-5|MT066-C05-5]] — Discipline: keep crypto positions very small and avoid markets where the firm has no edge or process.

## [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel]]
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C03-6|MT067-C03-6]] — In a regime where economists and traders are on equal footing with no precedent, premium is on common sense, imagination and risk management.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C05-1|MT067-C05-1]] — Macro value comes from scaling positions to conviction, which creates risk-management challenges.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C05-2|MT067-C05-2]] — When themes are clear, PM returns correlate more and information ratio is higher, but volatility is higher so position sizes shrink, offsetting risk.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C05-3|MT067-C05-3]] — Low-vol periods give low VaR but larger tail risk because positions are sized up; in high-vol 2022 VaR is high but tails smaller.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C05-4|MT067-C05-4]] — With unstable correlations VaR loses value; stress tests and gap-risk analysis matter more.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C05-X1|MT067-C05-X1]] — Capital is allocated across portfolio managers monthly using their pairwise return correlations, and PMs are hired for what they add that is different, so the book is not concentrated in one style.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C06-3|MT067-C06-3]] — If inflation unexpectedly cooled, easing cycles would also be an opportunity; the team holds both scenarios.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C08-1|MT067-C08-1]] — Risk management structure: frontline PM sizing, a daily risk committee (4000 meetings since GFC), risk team running Greeks, hundreds of shocks and dozens of stress tests, plus qualitative 'why' from the trading floor.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C08-2|MT067-C08-2]] — Risk team advises and monitors; PMs own risk, with committee as cold-eyes review.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C08-3|MT067-C08-3]] — Size positions in volatility units so higher volatility means smaller positions; prepare for tails and gap risk.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C09-3|MT067-C09-3]] — Across asset classes dollar, rates and equities become one trade into central bank meetings, so book size is shrunk to avoid a triple-sized bet.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C09-5|MT067-C09-5]] — Daily team risk meeting asks which positions are too big or too small.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C09-X1|MT067-C09-X1]] — Portfolio construction method: generate trade ideas bottom-up, then check the combined book's overall risk orientation, using the same process in quiet (2018) and busy (2022) years.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C09-X2|MT067-C09-X2]] — Sizing rule: a position should be big enough to matter at the portfolio level if right, but small enough that ordinary noise does not force you out; getting the theme right matters more than structure.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C10-1|MT067-C10-1]] — With no edge on any given event, scale down into events and react afterwards, using liquidity to rescale (unlike long-only or illiquid books).

## [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem]]
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C09-2|MT068-C09-2]] — Philosophy: survive across a wide range of worlds because compounding depends on avoiding bad outcomes.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C15-1|MT068-C15-1]] — Survival over 50 years came mainly from risk control, accepting that all are flawed.

## [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate]]
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C02-4|MT069-C02-4]] — Soros lesson: sizing matters more than hit rate; pay-off asymmetry decides returns.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C03-6|MT069-C03-6]] — Short bonds paired with long risk assets: in disinflationary growth it breaks even, if growth creates inflation bonds profit; the 'matrix' hedges both outcomes.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C03-7|MT069-C03-7]] — Typical horizon is 18 months to 3 years; volatility and new capital types provide entry points rather than changing the thesis.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C03-X3|MT069-C03-X3]] — Although trades are conceived on 18-month to 3-year horizons, he will exit and even reverse a position within days when the evidence changes, so long horizons do not mean holding through contrary information.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C04-3|MT069-C04-3]] — Scaling: start with a small position that matters, add as evidence (ChatGPT, then macro-call analyst) builds; doubled twice.

## [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns]]
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C03-1|MT070-C03-1]] — Uses about 2x gross leverage in long-short portfolios, versus ~10x rumoured for Goldman Global Alpha, conservative especially when serving less sophisticated clients.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C05-X1|MT070-C05-X1]] — Governance of a fully systematic book: researchers write and run the programs, execution is done by separate teams, and humans step in only to correct program errors, not to override signals with discretionary views.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C08-3|MT070-C08-3]] — Volatility is controlled by a single leverage 'volume knob' (10, 20 or 30 vol), independent of strategy changes.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C09-2|MT070-C09-2]] — Trend following is not a reliable hedge: it hedged in 2008, 2001-02, but in a 1987-style crash or Feb 2018 managed futures funds were long and lost because trends were up.

## [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408]]
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C02-3|MT071-C02-3]] — Trend followers should not change a sound model during a poor period (the CTA winter), because poor results reflect an absence of trends rather than a model flaw.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C03-3|MT071-C03-3]] — Micro futures contracts make it practical for small systematic portfolios to trade diversified futures.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C05-3|MT071-C05-3]] — Systematic trend followers do not need forecasts; they follow trends as they develop.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C10-3|MT071-C10-3]] — Systematica argues the total portfolio approach beats strategic asset allocation because bonds no longer diversify equity risk.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C10-4|MT071-C10-4]] — Many hedge fund strategies and private equity carry hidden equity beta, so true diversifiers are scarce.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C10-5|MT071-C10-5]] — Equity market neutral diversifies but lacks convexity, so trend following is complementary through dynamic risk management.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C11-1|MT071-C11-1]] — Under the total portfolio approach, strategies are judged by their contribution to the whole portfolio rather than versus peers.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C14-2|MT071-C14-2]] — Practical implication: CTAs trading fast should reduce weight or penalize small-tick contracts, as they already penalize illiquid contracts.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C14-3|MT071-C14-3]] — Even if fast trend returns degraded, it can still add convexity, skew and crisis alpha (Man papers).
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C15-5|MT071-C15-5]] — Small contracts have unchanged per-contract fees, so same exposure costs more; a backtest must reflect that cost.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C17-1|MT071-C17-1]] — Passive execution misses breakouts; aggressive execution is costly.

## [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350]]
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C01-4|MT072-C01-4]] — Noisy macro data harm strategies built on high-frequency macro data, so such macro strategies face a tricky period.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C02-4|MT072-C02-4]] — Private credit offers less diversification than assumed because it holds the same sectors, industries and weaker credit ratings.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C11-1|MT072-C11-1]] — Weak signal strength makes trend models cut risk, and with equities near highs they add long risk again.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C13-1|MT072-C13-1]] — When allocators flock to discretionary macro, a non-predictive rules-based diversified approach is useful in unpredictable policy regimes.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C14-1|MT072-C14-1]] — Allocation processes should be robust to volatility and correlation assumptions because the future can differ from the past.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C14-2|MT072-C14-2]] — Build a portfolio for each macro scenario (stagflation, inflation) and weight by probability; this tilts away from financial assets toward real assets, commodities and pure diversifiers.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C16-4|MT072-C16-4]] — Choosing among several regime scenarios raises the risk of picking the wrong one, an argument for trend following.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C16-5|MT072-C16-5]] — Portfolio managers list known risks but have no answer for unforeseen risks; trend following covers risks nobody planned for.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C16-6|MT072-C16-6]] — The trend follower's role is regime-agnostic positioning, not crisis alpha.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C17-1|MT072-C17-1]] — Robust portfolios come from diversification across risk factors, not from strong forecasts.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C17-2|MT072-C17-2]] — True diversification means balancing across risk factors; equities, private equity and credit share the same tail risk.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C17-3|MT072-C17-3]] — Allocating to trend following based on a macro view works for about a year before something surprises, so do not link it to the macro outlook.

## [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362]]
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C03-3|MT073-C03-3]] — Judge trend following over long periods, not one to six months; it is painful like going down the stairs and up the elevator.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C04-5|MT073-C04-5]] — Traditional CTAs hold roughly constant risk allocations per outright market, but this cannot be done for spreads.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C05-1|MT073-C05-1]] — Spread volatility is itself volatile, so targeting constant risk needs huge leverage and wipes you out in a dislocation (left-skewed).
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C05-2|MT073-C05-2]] — Only start trading a spread after a dislocation lifts its volatility to a minimum level, then size it dynamically.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C06-4|MT073-C06-4]] — Spread volatility is residual volatility after 90-98% correlation between legs, so it is tiny and needs 50x leverage to matter.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C06-5|MT073-C06-5]] — Leveraging a 2% residual-vol spread is the LTCM trap (on/off the run Treasuries); trade only when the spread becomes volatile.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C07-1|MT073-C07-1]] — After a structural break (tariff, refinery), spread volatility rises from about 2% to about 10% of leg vol, making it tradeable without much leverage.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C07-3|MT073-C07-3]] — Trade transient factors only while their volatility is sufficient, exit when it fades.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C08-3|MT073-C08-3]] — Spread trend should be traded faster than outright trend but not too fast because costs bite.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C08-4|MT073-C08-4]] — Spreads cost more per unit of risk since both legs trade, so fast spread trend may be uneconomic.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C08-5|MT073-C08-5]] — Use curve spreads to choose which contract to trade: if momentum wants to buy and the back is cheap, buy the back (supply/demand driven) rather than the front (macro driven).
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C10-2|MT073-C10-2]] — Speculators cut positions when volatility rises, a dominant effect now (volatility-based risk targeting).
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C14-3|MT073-C14-3]] — An allocator estimates the equity beta of each manager's P&L to track hidden equity exposure.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C14-4|MT073-C14-4]] — Allocators cannot fit CTA positioning from P&L because CTAs change exposure too often, showing CTAs' lack of persistent correlation.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C16-5|MT073-C16-5]] — Including systematic strategies in the optimization automatically adjusts the allocation as assumptions change.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C17-3|MT073-C17-3]] — Treat CTA/systematic alpha as independent of the CMA allocation, using its positioning only to modulate small tactical changes.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C18-1|MT073-C18-1]] — Allocators already embed systematic strategies such as FX hedging and could embed trend as another tool.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C18-X1|MT073-C18-X1]] — An allocator with structural foreign-currency exposure (e.g. an Australian investor holding USD assets) should hedge it with a systematic rule; a van Hemert paper studies the best ways to hedge FX exposure, a simple model for embedding systematic strategies such as trend into allocation.

## [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149]]
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C03-2|MT074-C03-2]] — Original portable alpha products (GFC era) used extreme leverage and shared a common left tail, so simulation of uncorrelated streams looked good but live structures broke in a crisis.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C03-3|MT074-C03-3]] — Insufficient cash buffers forced portable alpha structures to unwind at the worst time, so cash-efficiency and margin buffers determine survival.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C04-2|MT074-C04-2]] — Portable alpha lets investors add CTAs without changing strategic asset allocation.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C04-3|MT074-C04-3]] — CTAs are margin-traded and so leave most capital unencumbered, enabling cash-efficient overlays.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C04-4|MT074-C04-4]] — In stress, CTAs reduce risk and move to cash (volatility targeting), a self-healing property that helps overlay structures survive shocks.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C04-5|MT074-C04-5]] — Per 100 of capital, about 15-25 can fund a diversified CTA and 15-20 can fund 100 notional of equity exposure, leaving ample cash for variation margin.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C05-4|MT074-C05-4]] — Viability of a portable alpha structure depends on the tail behavior of the alpha and beta together, not any alpha with any beta.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C06-2|MT074-C06-2]] — Equity beta is the best base for a CTA overlay, and equity sleeves or a CIO completion portfolio set the overlay size as a tracking error choice.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C07-1|MT074-C07-1]] — Pure hedging or tail-risk strategies bleed returns over time unless timed perfectly, so they suit only short-term mindsets.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C08-3|MT074-C08-3]] — Trend following lets investors own equities.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C08-4|MT074-C08-4]] — Making trend portfolios 50% single-stock equities weakens diversification since equities struggle most when diversification is needed; prefer classically diversified portfolios.
