- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C09-1|MT074-C09-1]] — CTAs are only almost antifragile: a sharp gap against a long position gives no protection, so a cash buffer is needed.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C09-2|MT074-C09-2]] — Equity market neutral keeps shared equity tail risk, while a CTA overlay gives greater tracking error but a mechanical cross-asset response when equities shock.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C10-2|MT074-C10-2]] — CTAs have no prior, fair-value view or geographic bias, so can follow whichever divergence emerges.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C11-2|MT074-C11-2]] — Strategic allocation silos optimize within asset classes while ignoring portfolio-wide interactions.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C11-3|MT074-C11-3]] — The total portfolio approach looks through to risk factors and allocates risk rather than buckets, used by larger endowments, SWFs and pensions.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C11-4|MT074-C11-4]] — Organizations using the total portfolio approach find CTAs more useful because CTAs are best judged at portfolio level.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C13-1|MT074-C13-1]] — CTA replication products do not replicate closely, and their correlation to CTA indices breaks down at critical times.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C13-2|MT074-C13-2]] — Replication products trade few markets and take manager risk, so they may not capture trend following properties.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C13-3|MT074-C13-3]] — A blow-up in a replicated strategy could harm the whole CTA industry's reputation.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C15-2|MT074-C15-2]] — Allocators with an existing portfolio want sleeves of a CTA program (commodities only, non-trend only) to complete the portfolio.

## [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293]]
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C08-6|MT076-C08-6]] — A tail risk with a clear time window (late May to mid June) exists, so hedge for it with options.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C10-2|MT076-C10-2]] — Option strategies are powerful for professionals but can hurt non-professionals because they cut both ways.

## [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations]]
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C02-4|MT077-C02-4]] — Before placing a trade wait 30 days; a real investment loses nothing, while a trade you can barely wait on is sensation-seeking, not investing.
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C08-2|MT077-C08-2]] — Invest for the average outcome, not for the crash; expecting a crash always means never being invested.
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C09-1|MT077-C09-1]] — In the GFC quants were overconfident about mortgage correlation, and many assumed housing prices would never fall in unison nationally.
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C09-2|MT077-C09-2]] — AIG's internal view that it could not lose on mortgage-backed insurance illustrates overconfidence plus lack of imagination (about $80 billion lost).
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C11-3|MT077-C11-3]] — In a bear market investors should harvest tax losses (avoiding wash sales) to offset gains; anchoring prevents it.

## [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment]]
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C01-1|MT078-C01-1]] — The real skill is making money with risk under control so a bad year does not do much damage; the goal is consistency slightly above the middle with bounce to the top in bad years.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C01-X1|MT078-C01-X1]] — Because most market years are good years, making money is easy most of the time; results in good years say little about skill, which shows only in how little is lost in bad years.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C04-5|MT078-C04-5]] — An opinion on areas one does not know well (China cheap, AI) is fine but should not be bet with client money.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C04-6|MT078-C04-6]] — Sound risk management is not risk avoidance; take risk that is aware, analyzable, diversifiable and highly paid.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C05-1|MT078-C05-1]] — Under any circumstance a variety of outcomes is possible, so allow for the vagaries of the future; excessive certainty is the enemy.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C05-4|MT078-C05-4]] — No separate risk department; the investment advocate owns risk, so nobody can count on others to limit risk.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C05-5|MT078-C05-5]] — Fixed income has capped upside, so the skill is avoiding losers (Graham and Dodd: negative art).
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C06-3|MT078-C06-3]] — Avoiding losers works for 8% fixed income targets but aspirational 15-20% targets also require finding winners.

## [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306]]
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C05-X2|MT079-C05-X2]] — Some trend followers drop a market when its volatility falls too low (e.g. a soft currency peg); the hosts later prefer volatility floors to removal.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C06-2|MT079-C06-2]] — Drop markets when volume or open interest fall below minimums, because illiquidity brings jump risk; avoid brand-new markets.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C06-4|MT079-C06-4]] — Use volatility floors and multiple lookbacks so that suppressed volatility (Swiss franc, short rates) does not lead to oversized positions.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C06-6|MT079-C06-6]] — A market that makes little money but pays when others lose still adds diversification value.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C07-2|MT079-C07-2]] — Volatility targeting increases size when volatility is artificially low (Swiss franc peg), which was the worst action when the peg broke.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C09-3|MT079-C09-3]] — Portfolio construction (diversification) is the one free lunch available.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C09-4|MT079-C09-4]] — Risk management belongs in the model's entry and exit rules, with a stop loss as a fail-safe protecting investor principal.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C10-2|MT079-C10-2]] — If stock-bond correlation turns positive, 60/40 volatility rises and bonds lose their diversification role, so risk should be monitored via the off-diagonal covariance terms.

## [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337]]
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C07-4|MT080-C07-4]] — Choose speed by client objective: the highest Sharpe favours slower trend, risk-mitigation clients care about conditional returns versus equities and want short-term reactivity.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C12-4|MT080-C12-4]] — Single-strategy replication carries large return dispersion because a manager's window can be best one year and worst the next.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C14-2|MT080-C14-2]] — With tracking error this large, 'replication' is misleading: an investor gets something that may be better or worse than the index.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C14-3|MT080-C14-3]] — Anyone wanting CTA beta must pick the method that gives the most beta-like exposure, since a single ETF can differ greatly from the industry.

## [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod]]
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C05-2|MT081-C05-2]] — This AI capex cycle is funded from cash flow, so if it fails the loss is an equity loss, not a leverage loss or systemic risk.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C10-2|MT081-C10-2]] — The financial crisis came from excessive bank leverage, a blown-up asset class (subprime) and systemically important banks owning it.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C11-2|MT081-C11-2]] — Greenspan admitted he was wrong that derivative markets would self-regulate.

## [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1]]
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C05-4|MT082-C05-4]] — Calculo trades only the most liquid metals, agriculturals, softs and energies, which also lets it scale.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C06-1|MT082-C06-1]] — Shorter-horizon traders are much more sensitive to market liquidity than slow trend followers, who do not need to trade daily.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C06-3|MT082-C06-3]] — Machine learning applied to risk management reduces volatility and gives a smoother return profile than plain trend following.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C07-1|MT082-C07-1]] — The model can be flat, partly long or partly short, with no mandatory allocation to any market or sector.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C07-2|MT082-C07-2]] — About 75 to 80% of trades are closed by machine learning exits and the rest by classical stops and trailing stops.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C07-4|MT082-C07-4]] — Capturing parts of trends and avoiding counter-moves gives a smoother ride but not the whole trend, as classical longer-term CTAs do; ML uses daily bars.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C09-1|MT082-C09-1]] — Machine learning exits are timed at the most liquid hours and are flags of possible correction, not firm predictions, so execution is done in liquid periods.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C09-2|MT082-C09-2]] — The long-only Bloomberg Commodity Index had a 77-month, 38% drawdown from 2015 to 2020 and a further 22% drop after 2022, so a long/short strategy with similar returns and lower volatility is preferable.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C09-3|MT082-C09-3]] — Commodity strategy returns tend to be positive in bad equity months, lowering portfolio volatility.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C10-6|MT082-C10-6]] — Calculo reports equity-like returns at two-thirds of stock index volatility since 2018, non-correlated and non-cyclical.

## [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394]]
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C03-2|MT083-C03-2]] — Energy and short fixed-income/rate positions profited from inflation worries and offset equity losses, showing the diversification value of trend.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C03-4|MT083-C03-4]] — If inflation returns or escalation continues, neither equities, bonds, gold (wobbling) nor private markets help; trend is a candidate defensive allocation.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C05-1|MT083-C05-1]] — Carry, value and similar strategies may do well in a mid-distribution equity unwind but can show horrible left tails in a full liquidity break.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C05-6|MT083-C05-6]] — Market-set weights matter when one sector makes a huge move (50% in energy): 10% vs 20% energy weight changes results greatly.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C06-1|MT083-C06-1]] — Against the S&P (down about 4.3% YTD), MSCI World and world government bonds, trend did well in March, answering critics who said there was no crisis alpha; the short-term traders index did best (+56 bp).
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C07-3|MT083-C07-3]] — Maximum crisis-Sharpe portfolio overweights gold, crude and dollar-vs-pound; maximum long-run-Sharpe portfolio overweights alternative markets (hydropower, iron ore, China commodities).
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C07-4|MT083-C07-4]] — Traditional markets give maximum cash efficiency, so for portable alpha a trend allocation needs little cash.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C08-4|MT083-C08-4]] — The crisis-alpha portfolio and cash-efficient portfolio are similar, so risk mitigators also get cash efficiency.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C09-2|MT083-C09-2]] — Crisis alpha needs a strategy to be unbiased, liquid and adaptable.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C10-3|MT083-C10-3]] — Trend uses little cash ($25 to $30 per $100 of exposure), leaving cash to hold the beta index.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C11-1|MT083-C11-1]] — Trend as portable alpha had better drawdown characteristics than equity-focused or hedge fund alpha.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C11-3|MT083-C11-3]] — Coincident alpha and beta drawdowns (COVID, GFC) could hurt portable alpha, so structures need due diligence.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C12-1|MT083-C12-1]] — With 10% margin on S&P futures, an 8 to 10% fall exhausts margin, so keep large cash buffers (e.g. $40 per $100 rather than $10) instead of investing the rest.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C12-2|MT083-C12-2]] — An illiquid alpha cannot meet margin calls, forcing closure of the beta, so use liquid alpha such as trend.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C12-3|MT083-C12-3]] — Trend manages to risk, so in a crisis its exposure and margin fall, reducing funding needs.

## [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377]]
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C09-3|MT084-C09-3]] — Financial systems always break down, so prepare (Bill White).
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C10-6|MT084-C10-6]] — Both runaway inflation and deflationary deleveraging are plausible tail outcomes, with the bond-equity correlation less stable and more positive.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C10-7|MT084-C10-7]] — Credit looks diversifying in up markets but correlates positively in equity declines, so labels such as 'alternative' mislead.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C11-2|MT084-C11-2]] — The proposed portfolio allocates risk 40% growth, 40% adaptive (trend, macro), 20% diversifiers, using return stacking and leverage and risk contribution rather than capital weights.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C11-3|MT084-C11-3]] — The adaptive portfolio's rolling equity beta swings from negative to above one, unlike the constant roughly 0.6 of 60/40.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C12-4|MT084-C12-4]] — The adaptive portfolio has about 8% tracking error to 60/40, versus CalPERS about 4% to its 75/25, with more flexibility than a total portfolio approach.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C13-2|MT084-C13-2]] — Private equity and venture capital are alternative growth assets, a private form of public equity risk, not diversifiers; the bar for diversifier is higher.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C14-1|MT084-C14-1]] — Markowitz logic: choose the best risk-adjusted blend first and then set risk with leverage or deleverage, not by shifting between equities and bonds.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C14-4|MT084-C14-4]] — A typical institutional portfolio with 10 to 12 buckets is 80% growth-linked, and over 90% in risk terms, so it is concentrated in growth and stable inflation.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C15-3|MT084-C15-3]] — Typical wealth portfolios are 50 to 60% equities, 20% fixed income and little gold or hedge funds, so they need rebalancing and leverage tools.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C15-4|MT084-C15-4]] — The adaptive portfolio beats 60/40 since 1990 mainly through smaller drawdowns, which compound from a higher base after recoveries.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C16-2|MT084-C16-2]] — With many people near retirement, smoother returns matter because a big drawdown near retirement is worst.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C16-3|MT084-C16-3]] — Think in risk terms, use futures, return stacking and portable alpha as building blocks, and combine adaptive strategies to overcome behavioural obstacles to holding them.

## [[MT085 - Inside the mind of a legendary investor - Stan Druckenmiller - Highlights - In Good Compan]]
- [[MT085 - Inside the mind of a legendary investor - Stan Druckenmiller - Highlights - In Good Compan#^MT085-C02-2|MT085-C02-2]] — Take a meaningful but not huge starting position, then do the research, sell if wrong and add if right.
- [[MT085 - Inside the mind of a legendary investor - Stan Druckenmiller - Highlights - In Good Compan#^MT085-C02-5|MT085-C02-5]] — When conviction is high, bet very big (Soros); his own 100% pound position was doubled to 200% by Soros.
- [[MT085 - Inside the mind of a legendary investor - Stan Druckenmiller - Highlights - In Good Compan#^MT085-C02-7|MT085-C02-7]] — Soros called the pound a rare one-way bet and doubled the position to 200% of the fund; asymmetric one-way trades justify far larger size.
- [[MT085 - Inside the mind of a legendary investor - Stan Druckenmiller - Highlights - In Good Compan#^MT085-C02-X1|MT085-C02-X1]] — The 1992 pound trade was sized at 100% partly because the pound was still only about half a percent away (caption unclear) from its peg level, so downside was tiny relative to the upside if the link broke; small, capped downside is what makes a one-way bet.

## [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318]]
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C06-4|MT086-C06-4]] — A dynamic long/short trend exposure to fixed income is a good diversifier in a world with higher cash rates and inflation risk, not just long bonds.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C07-3|MT086-C07-3]] — Trend following in bonds (long or short) yields strongly negative correlation to stocks even when static bonds correlate positively, so it fixes the 60/40 diversification loss.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C07-4|MT086-C07-4]] — Static bonds are a less reliable diversifier in higher rate/inflation environments, so 60/40 needs more long/short exposure to improve correlation properties.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C09-4|MT086-C09-4]] — Dispersion also came from asset-class risk allocation: managers with more risk in equities and commodities beat those with more in bonds and FX in 2024.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C13-5|MT086-C13-5]] — With higher cash rates, adding trend following to a 60/40 portfolio helps more because bonds lose diversification while trend keeps return and negative correlation.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C14-1|MT086-C14-1]] — Quantica's optimal mixes: in low cash-rate regimes 55/35/10 (stocks/bonds/trend) beats 60/40; in high cash-rate regimes 55/25/20.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C14-2|MT086-C14-2]] — Rule of thumb: if you cannot judge the rate regime, hold about 15% in trend as core allocation; 2-3% allocations are too small to help in a crisis.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C15-1|MT086-C15-1]] — Niels notes risk (leverage) had come out of trend portfolios in late 2024 because of changed correlations, making a lower-risk entry point even in a drawdown.

## [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352]]
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C05-3|MT087-C05-3]] — Trend followers keep their role as crisis hedge; investors stayed because no sustained crisis has occurred, with no outflows and rising inflows.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C06-3|MT087-C06-3]] — Trend portfolios are built from signals plus a risk model (vol and correlation estimates) and usually target or bound portfolio volatility so investors can scale them next to 60/40.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C06-4|MT087-C06-4]] — Idea: make the portfolio-level volatility target dynamic instead of constant, because realized volatility is a function of leverage, which depends on diversification and correlation.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C07-2|MT087-C07-2]] — Three risk-sizing schools: target volatility, target VaR with a risk budget, or fix initial risk and let VaR float; some managers' portfolio vol swung from 30 to 90 in a year, which Niels calls wild.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C08-1|MT087-C08-1]] — Because turning points are unpredictable, leverage going into them matters: twice the leverage means roughly double the loss.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C08-2|MT087-C08-2]] — If V-shapes become more frequent and signal-to-noise is low, lower volatility targets (less leverage) may be warranted.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C08-3|MT087-C08-3]] — SG Trend index realized volatility over the last 1-2 years was about 7-11%, below the historic 10-13%, hinting managers already run lower or more dynamic risk.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C08-4|MT087-C08-4]] — Motivation for dynamic vol targets: cut drawdowns and take more risk when trends are plentiful; open question is how managers decide the level.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C09-R1|MT087-C09-R1]] — Baltas accepts lagging peers in a strong year (15 versus 20) as the price of being under-levered to avoid a deeper loss in a bad year, which is the asymmetry that motivates dynamic de-leveraging.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C10-2|MT087-C10-2]] — Niels's firm has used dynamic risk budgeting for over 12 years and sees value in it, using volatility and correlations beyond price; critics call it contrary to pure trend following.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C11-1|MT087-C11-1]] — Mean-variance would put 100% in managed futures (higher Sharpe), but investors are judged against a benchmark, so career and tracking-error risk constrain the allocation.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C14-1|MT087-C14-1]] — Trend following should be a strategic allocation (like insurance), because timing it is not possible: past performance is a weak predictor of future returns.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C14-3|MT087-C14-3]] — Moderating risk through vol scaling is distinct from timing the allocation in and out: it manages the pain of losses at the margin.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C15-2|MT087-C15-2]] — Most trend investors did not change models after the 2025 V-shape since this is the known losing regime; its sign is understood, only the size is in question.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C15-X1|MT087-C15-X1]] — Treating trend as mandatory insurance suggests a fixed strategic allocation of around 10% that is never timed.

## [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive]]
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C01-1|MT089-C01-1]] — Graham began as a systematic CTA (1994) and added a discretionary macro business in 1998 to diversify alpha sources; a fed-watcher trader was the catalyst for its success.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C03-1|MT089-C03-1]] — Hedge funds were intended to diversify from market beta; macro was dominant in the first decade of the industry for that reason.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C06-1|MT089-C06-1]] — Graham has held a daily 9:30 risk meeting since 2007 reviewing P&L, positions, liquidity, counterparty risk and stress tests; hands-on risk management adds value over the long haul.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C06-2|MT089-C06-2]] — Diversify internal alpha sources (systematic momentum, discretionary macro, long-term quant equities) so returns are not reliant on momentum alone.

## [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next]]
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C01-1|MT090-C01-1]] — Graham's book is about 75% macro risk: rates, FX, equities, some commodities, with much in fixed income relative value, curve (steepeners) and breakeven/inflation trades.
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C01-2|MT090-C01-2]] — Conservative philosophy: success across many cycles; sometimes constructive for views, other times keeping risk close to home is best; be innovative and opportunistic but careful.
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C03-2|MT090-C03-2]] — In 2025 policy was fluid (tariffs, Supreme Court case), and Graham's traders did well being tactical with low risk levels.
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C06-3|MT090-C06-3]] — Rule: when there is no conviction, keep risk small; few aggressive traders survive, so run the book conservatively and accept bad cycles.

## [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast]]
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C01-2|MT092-C01-2]] — Graham combines 22-25 discretionary trading teams with many quant models to hold as many uncorrelated sources of alpha as possible.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C02-3|MT092-C02-3]] — A stock-bond correlation of 40% means bonds may not diversify an equity selloff, so broader diversification is needed.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C02-7|MT092-C02-7]] — Managers want opportunities but not ones accompanied by so much volatility and noise that they cannot be traded.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C04-4|MT092-C04-4]] — Zero term premium, record equity prices and a stock-bond correlation near 0.4 (normal zero or slightly negative) form a daunting combination for institutional investors.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C04-X1|MT092-C04-X1]] — In the early-1980s rate shock, par bonds fell to about 50 cents within two years and holders could only wait for maturity or sell at large losses, so recovery from a duration shock takes very long.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C05-1|MT092-C05-1]] — Multi-strategy hedge funds succeeded by building many uncorrelated alpha sources, conservative risk management, and consistent high-Sharpe returns.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C05-2|MT092-C05-2]] — Multistrats correlate with equity conditions, doing well in good equity markets and poorly in 2022.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C05-4|MT092-C05-4]] — In an equity correction macro is one of the few hedge fund styles that tends to perform, while equity long/short, credit and private equity do not.

## [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -]]
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C03-2|MT093-C03-2]] — The classic managed-futures case shows crisis episodes (GFC, 2022, 2000) and that adding 10% trend to a 60/40 portfolio improves mean, standard deviation, drawdown and Sharpe ratio.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C05-1|MT093-C05-1]] — Rob runs about 50-60% of his typical risk because clear trends are lacking, with long equities, FX, bonds and short energy and Bitcoin.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C05-5|MT093-C05-5]] — If discretionary views (Trump bad for bonds and equities) were right, a systematic long-bond portfolio would lose money, showing model and gut views can conflict.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C05-X2|MT093-C05-X2]] — Rob's trend system holds a modest but persistent long bond position (BTPs, US 30-year, Canadian 10-year) with risk about half that on equities, so a bond sell-off driven by Fed-independence or inflation fears would cost his systematic book money.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C06-2|MT093-C06-2]] — For cash-only (unlevered) portfolios where forecast-scaled positions exceed 100%, one option is to halve all positions so average exposure is 50% and maximum is 100%.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C06-3|MT093-C06-3]] — Halving works for high-volatility assets (e.g. EM stocks at 50% vol, giving a 25% target) but gives a risk target too low for liquid S&P 500 stocks.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C06-X1|MT093-C06-X1]] — In an unlevered account forecast scaling is asymmetric: weak forecasts can shrink positions but stronger-than-average ones cannot raise them, so the fraction of the forecast-strength information that is used falls.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C07-1|MT093-C07-1]] — Alternative: cap forecasts at the average (10) instead of twice average (20), so weak forecasts shrink positions and cash creeps back in.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C07-2|MT093-C07-2]] — Reallocating freed-up cash to other positions is theoretically optimal but usually not worth the complexity.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C08-1|MT093-C08-1]] — Do not currency-convert price series before trend following; trade the local-currency price and the FX rate as separate instruments.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C08-2|MT093-C08-2]] — Combining the price and FX rate into one series removes diversification and increases correlation with a separate currency trade.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C08-4|MT093-C08-4]] — With futures only margin is converted, so FX exposure is a fraction of notional and the currency can be traded separately.

## [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2]]
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C11-X1|MT094-C11-X1]] — The OpenAI round carries contingencies on whether the nonprofit-to-for-profit conversion completes, so the headline funding is not fully secured.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C17-X1|MT094-C17-X1]] — CoreWeave is highly levered and must delever, which is the main risk against the cash-flow case from contracted GPU revenue.

## [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369]]
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C02-3|MT095-C02-3]] — Gold behaves like a safe asset: uncorrelated with risk assets, better in crises, an inflation hedge and as liquid as Treasuries at times.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C05-1|MT095-C05-1]] — Safety may come from strategies that are adaptive, diversified and liquid (trend following) rather than from long-only safe assets.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C05-2|MT095-C05-2]] — Safe assets can be fickle like friends, so diversify across multiple safe assets and strategies.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C10-2|MT095-C10-2]] — First Brands' bankruptcy hit trade-finance funds (a UBS fund had 30% exposure) marketed as low-risk steady double-digit returns, showing packaging narratives can hide risk.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C10-3|MT095-C10-3]] — Trend following shows daily volatility but rarely any really bad surprises in 35 years.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C11-1|MT095-C11-1]] — Trade finance is intensive credit work requiring physical inventory checks (counting windshield wipers); you need many good loans to cover one bad loan.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C11-2|MT095-C11-2]] — Daily mark-to-market and margining in futures makes lending against collateral safer than opaque lending, though fraud like nickel and the salad-oil case happened in futures markets.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C11-X1|MT095-C11-X1]] — Lending against inventory carries a double-pledging risk: the same collateral may back several loans, so lenders must verify inventory physically (windshield wipers in First Brands' warehouses).
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C15-2|MT095-C15-2]] — Execution is short-term trading: cutting market impact costs by 1-2% can be a significant share of annual returns, and market-on-close still has impact.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C15-3|MT095-C15-3]] — Trading at the close, first 10 minutes or VWAP all have microstructure impact even for long-term trend followers.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C16-3|MT095-C16-3]] — Diversify across style, time horizon and markets because nothing works all the time.

## [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355]]
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C03-3|MT096-C03-3]] — Realized volatility of trend managers fell after the April 2025 spike because positions were scaled back and resized, which is the vol-targeting response to a volatility spike.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C06-3|MT096-C06-3]] — Trend-following losses are smaller than equity drawdowns, so the risk/reward profile is better managed than equities even in bad years.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C10-3|MT096-C10-3]] — The Capstone paper argues long-volatility strategies protect in sudden crashes (first responder, e.g. March 2020) and trend following protects in prolonged declines (second responder, e.g. 2022), so a blend is more robust.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C10-X1|MT096-C10-X1]] — Individual CTA managers have had worse drawdowns than the index, so investors holding only one or two managers experience the strategy's bad periods more severely than index statistics suggest; manager dispersion shapes perception and the case for diversifying across managers.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C11-2|MT096-C11-2]] — Family-office portfolios spread across 12 categories (developed/EM equity, private equity, real estate, high yield, EM debt) are mostly equity/growth beta that fails together in stress, with only about 4% in hedge funds and under 1% in commodities.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C11-X1|MT096-C11-X1]] — Niels is only lukewarm on the long-vol plus trend blend argued by Capstone and Alan, preferring a pure trend approach; a disagreement on whether adding long volatility improves a crisis-hedge portfolio.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C12-1|MT096-C12-1]] — A typical family-office allocation is a bet on favorable growth and stable inflation and lacks commodities and convexity, so it would struggle in stagflation.
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C12-X1|MT096-C12-X1]] — A hedge fund allocation only diversifies if it holds divergent strategies run at meaningful volatility; a small allocation to low-vol market-neutral or long-short equity funds adds little convexity in a crisis.

## [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS]]
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C01-1|MT097-C01-1]] — Seykota's approach shows that maximizing return requires pushing bet size and tolerating large heat, but overshooting the optimum risks ruin, so extreme sizing is not advisable for most traders.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C02-2|MT097-C02-2]] — Seykota followed a few sacrosanct general rules: cut losses short, let profits ride, diversify.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C02-3|MT097-C02-3]] — Seykota's equity swings are enormous, so no institution would invest in him despite the very high returns, since his aim was maximum profit rather than controlled risk.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C05-1|MT097-C05-1]] — There is an optimal bet size given a known edge per trade (Seykota and Drew wrote an article on heat distribution), but because edge is unstable over time it is only a rule of thumb, and the optimal size is bigger than most people would use.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C05-2|MT097-C05-2]] — Betting too large raises risk of ruin steeply, while betting too little makes risk of ruin near zero but gives low returns.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C05-3|MT097-C05-3]] — To make a lot of money one must bet bigger than seems comfortable, taking considerable heat, but not so big as to risk ruin.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C05-R1|MT097-C05-R1]] — Drew relays Seykota's answer on his biggest drawdown: he does not think he lost more than 50% in a day, i.e. extreme heat is part of his return.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C06-3|MT097-C06-3]] — Drawdown reduction rules (dialing risk back after losses) can keep a system underwater indefinitely, so size should instead scale with current equity.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C06-4|MT097-C06-4]] — Bet size should scale proportionally with equity (2x at 2 million), rising and falling, otherwise risk of ruin rises if you only scale up.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C07-1|MT097-C07-1]] — Sizing from closed-trade equity (after stops) is more stable than from open-trade equity, which includes large open profits and encourages over-risking.
- [[MT097 - Living With Ed Seykota - Open Interest - Ep.19 BONUS#^MT097-C07-2|MT097-C07-2]] — After a 50% drawdown bet size should be on the reduced equity base but not cut further, as extra de-risking gives too little risk to recover and distorts the statistics of the system.

## [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)]]
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C05-1|MT098-C05-1]] — Real economic investment (starting firms) is high risk with about 90% failure over ten years, so retail savers should treat their portfolio as prudent reallocation of savings across cash, bonds and stocks, not a get-rich-quick bet.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C06-1|MT098-C06-1]] — Asset allocation should be built around the timing of liabilities (monthly bills, car in 2 years, house in 5, retirement in 30) rather than optimizing return per unit of risk in a mean-variance framework.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C06-2|MT098-C06-2]] — A bond ladder (e.g. 10% of the allocation spread over one to ten year maturities and rolled annually) gives a near-certain short bucket for cash needs without regard to interest-rate forecasts.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C07-1|MT098-C07-1]] — In the All Duration Investing model every asset gets a calculated duration (stocks about 18 years, also gold, commodities, REITs) to map assets to time horizons; the duration estimates involve guesswork.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C10-1|MT098-C10-1]] — More active trading means higher taxes and fees and less control over outcomes, so day trading is a poor use of human capital compared with building valuable skills.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C14-2|MT098-C14-2]] — Bonds (and T-bills at 4%) provide nominal principal stability over defined horizons, not real returns, which is why TIPS are not a favored tool; use maturities within about 5 years to avoid large interest-rate risk.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C15-2|MT098-C15-2]] — In 2022 stocks fell 25% while bonds fell 11%, gold 10% and commodities rose, so a diversified mix buffered stock losses and cut behavioral stress.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C16-X1|MT098-C16-X1]] — Whether a diversifier works depends on the investor's base currency: in 2022 gold fell about 10% in dollars but rose in euros because the euro fell about 16%.

## [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308]]
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C05-2|MT099-C05-2]] — Trend followers operate by rules and treat a crash day as an ordinary day, unlike discretionary or other strategies that see frantic activity; trend gains of one early-year week exceed the loss of the August week.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C07-1|MT099-C07-1]] — Under dynamic risk allocation, bonds received less and less risk in 2024 because they were not trending, then regained direction in August.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C07-2|MT099-C07-2]] — Diversified portfolios were cushioned by grain downtrends and non-traditional commodity trends during July/August, which headlines focused on equities and yen missed.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C07-X1|MT099-C07-X1]] — A few-day shock is too short relative to medium/long trend look-back windows to flip equity or yen positions; it mainly causes deleveraging and weaker signal strength while most positions continue, so trend portfolios change little after brief crashes.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C08-1|MT099-C08-1]] — With dynamic vol scaling, risk allocation fell to about 10% in rates (from 25% across equities, rates, FX, commodities) because rates did not trend while equities and commodities trended.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C08-2|MT099-C08-2]] — Dynamic sizing has two layers: vol scaling per asset, and whether to force equal risk across asset classes (e.g. 25% each) or let risk flow to the trending class; the latter caused most dispersion between managers.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C09-1|MT099-C09-1]] — A balanced asset-class risk allocation would have lost less in August (equities caused 60% of the loss) but would have captured less of the equity trend before; it is a trade-off between forced diversification and conviction in signals.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C11-1|MT099-C11-1]] — Assessing strategies should focus on turning points and market rotations and how they preserve capital because one must survive drawdowns to see new highs.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C13-1|MT099-C13-1]] — Defensive trend designs (no long equities) were around 0% month-to-date, so design choices (equity caps, speed, class balance) should be matched to what the investor needs to defend, e.g. 60/40 equity and rates risk.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C14-X2|MT099-C14-X2]] — Trend followers should see themselves first as risk managers because they control risk taken but not returns the market offers; many managers have moved from stop losses with static sizing to VaR-based portfolio risk models, raising the worry that common VaR use synchronizes selling.
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C15-1|MT099-C15-1]] — Value at risk is only a threshold; use expected shortfall (conditional VaR) for the loss beyond it, and note that VaR is bounded by historical realizations (parametric or Monte Carlo variants).
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C15-3|MT099-C15-3]] — Correlations rise in a crisis, so back-tested diversification overstates protection.

## [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322]]
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C05-2|MT101-C05-2]] — Trend confidence falls if fundamentals disagree: slowing growth, tightening financial conditions or negative surprise index while yields trend up.
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C08-2|MT101-C08-2]] — Allocators are cautious on crypto-only trend programs because of operational issues (prime broker, no cross margining) and thin liquidity that caps capacity and raises costs beyond the top coins.
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C13-2|MT101-C13-2]] — Good position sizing and risk management matter as much as forecast skill: average forecasters survive with sizing, great forecasters with poor sizing still go bankrupt (55/45 accuracy is good).
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C17-X17|MT101-C17-X17]] — Large allocators may prefer trend replication or trend beta to get the average CTA return and avoid manager-selection risk, accepting that skill is not replicated.

## [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56]]
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C04-3|MT102-C04-3]] — Loeb rules: cut losers fast, let winners ride; they also sell half on a double and take profits.

## [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G]]
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C09-1|MT103-C09-1]] — Altimeter started 2023 about 95% net long and 2024 around 80% net long (longs minus shorts), reducing 'chips on the table' because the price of entry is higher.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C12-1|MT103-C12-1]] — Altimeter avoids derivative plays (power companies) in favour of bigger bets on the best idea (essentialism).

## [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368]]
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C11-1|MT104-C11-1]] — Replace stock with out-of-the-money calls (stock replacement): short the left tail and underweight the right? Buy the cheapest part of the distribution (112% strike calls, vol ~ avg 17).

## [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371]]
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C07-4|MT105-C07-4]] — The expectancy equation ignores sequence of events and so is an ergodic statistic that misleads in markets.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C08-1|MT105-C08-1]] — Wealth paths have a lower bound of zero (ruin) and compounding, making them non-ergodic; Gaussian-based tools (Markowitz, Sharpe, standard deviation, expectancy, risk of ruin) break down.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C09-1|MT105-C09-1]] — Low volatility is latent compressed risk, like a sponge, awaiting a tipping point; volatility is not risk, and standard volatility measures cannot define risk.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C09-X2|MT105-C09-X2]] — Volatility comes in directionless and directional forms, with or without cross-market correlation; this distinction (rather than a volatility level) is the signature of a live compress-and-expand fractal system.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C10-X1|MT105-C10-X1]] — Rich does not use volatility or other statistical risk measures to protect the portfolio because their assumptions come from a Gaussian world; protection comes from robust system design and sizing instead (the root of his disagreement with volatility-based sizing).
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C13-1|MT105-C13-1]] — Diversify trend systems by behavior (breakout, volatility expansion, smooth persistence, mean-reversion into trend) rather than correlation, which is fickle; use ensembles to be ready for any structural form.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C13-2|MT105-C13-2]] — Markets are non-ergodic with one path; ruin ends the game, so survival is the key measure of success including of a manager's track record.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C14-1|MT105-C14-1]] — Rich considers Kelly, expectancy and value-at-risk bad statistics because they derive from a Gaussian model.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C14-2|MT105-C14-2]] — Identical expectancy gives different outcomes depending on order of wins and losses; -50% then +50% leaves a permanent hole (sequence risk).
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C14-3|MT105-C14-3]] — Size new positions from closed balance (realized capital): it automatically reduces exposure in drawdowns and scales up only after confirmed gains; with positive skew, open equity sits above closed balance so sizing is more conservative.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C14-4|MT105-C14-4]] — Large position sizes can turn a 5% positive expectancy into ruin through compounding drag; very small positions can make a 2%-expectancy (30% win rate, 3:1) system compound well.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C14-X1|MT105-C14-X1]] — Closed-balance sizing aims at avoiding ruin rather than maximising return or targeting volatility; it embeds humility by sizing only from capital that has survived the realized path.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C15-1|MT105-C15-1]] — Niels argues the open equity is not illusory for liquid futures managers who can liquidate in 24 hours, so sizing from full equity is reasonable; closed-balance sizing also reduces volatility like dynamic sizing.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C15-2|MT105-C15-2]] — Closed-balance sizing depends on the inception date; a fund with new client inflows has investors at different entry points, leaving undefined risk for them.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C16-1|MT105-C16-1]] — The cutback rule is discretionary and also cuts lifting power until a new high; dynamic sizing may increase risk in drawdown, but long-term track records show no big difference in returns.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C17-1|MT105-C17-1]] — Constant position sizing in a big trend (gold, cocoa) may help, but constant sizing in all other positions can detract; both approaches have worked for decades.

## [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39]]
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C05-3|MT106-C05-3]] — Carver's vol-targeted system cuts risk to a third of target when forecasts are weak, so risk scales with signal strength.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C05-X2|MT106-C05-X2]] — The aggregate risk a vol-targeted system runs (set by average forecast strength, here about a third of long-term target) works as an own trend-strength barometer: low risk means weak or conflicting signals and a hard environment for trend followers.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C06-2|MT106-C06-2]] — Position sizing in vol-targeted trend following mechanically takes profit out of a metals bubble as volatility rises.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C07-6|MT106-C07-6]] — Costs are predictable while performance is noise, so give costs more weight in design than performance.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C09-1|MT106-C09-1]] — Marginal gains: cutting 50bp of slippage is a near-certain annual gain, whereas 50bp of expected return is random.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C10-3|MT106-C10-3]] — Using complex methods without understanding them is the main danger.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C13-2|MT106-C13-2]] — Optimise a CTA for crisis alpha only if it is a sleeve in a larger portfolio, otherwise maximise Sharpe.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C13-3|MT106-C13-3]] — Jointly optimising a portfolio cuts equity and bond weights in the CTA sleeve, adds ags and speeds it up.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C19-3|MT106-C19-3]] — Claimed cost savings of replication look overstated.

## [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333]]
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C04-2|MT107-C04-2]] — Risk should be managed by adapting to the current regime, not by an average volatility assumption.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C05-3|MT107-C05-3]] — Convexity equals positive skew: amplify gains when volatility is favorable and cap losses with risk management.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C06-1|MT107-C06-1]] — Convex process accelerates in favorable regimes and brakes (stops, small bets) in adverse regimes, rather than assuming constant conditions.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C06-2|MT107-C06-2]] — Volatility splits into adverse and beneficial; smoothing funds only limit adverse volatility and forgo upside.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C07-2|MT107-C07-2]] — Barbell: keep losses small and linear on one side and amplify opportunity on the other, forcing asymmetry.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C08-4|MT107-C08-4]] — Tail protection funds add options to long-only portfolios to cover unforeseen collapses.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C09-X1|MT107-C09-X1]] — Counter-view on convexity horizon: longer-term managers can argue that supplying convexity at short horizons (daily) costs too much bleed and does not matter for long-run results, so convexity should be defined by time frame (Quantica has a paper on this).
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C09-X2|MT107-C09-X2]] — Options are the most precise hedge for a traditional portfolio because they can be matched to the specific risk (correlation risk, market risk), whereas trend following is a less targeted, free-of-premium form of convexity.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C10-1|MT107-C10-1]] — Trend following in a multi-asset portfolio cushions events nobody imagined, so ask what you are doing about what you are not worried about.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C11-4|MT107-C11-4]] — VaR misses contagion because markets are connected complex adaptive systems.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C11-5|MT107-C11-5]] — Risk must be built into portfolio design (convexity), not only forecasted.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C12-3|MT107-C12-3]] — Risk should be defined as permanent loss of capital, not volatility.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C13-3|MT107-C13-3]] — Correlation-aware VaR, used properly, remains a decent risk measure, better than Sharpe.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C13-4|MT107-C13-4]] — Trend following is a barbell of small losses and let profits run, with very small bet sizes.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C13-5|MT107-C13-5]] — Risk each trade as a small fraction of closed-trade core capital, letting unrealised equity run.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C14-1|MT107-C14-1]] — Large open profits expose new investors to 50 percent drawdowns; allocate AUM on closed equity.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C14-X1|MT107-C14-X1]] — Rich's answer to the new-investor problem: allocate new AUM using closed equity, so open (unrealised) equity stays with existing investors and is not a new investor's problem; there are different models for this.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C15-1|MT107-C15-1]] — Take a small initial trade, add as it succeeds (anti-martingale) with an ensemble, for positive convexity.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C15-2|MT107-C15-2]] — Martingale doubling after losses is concave: small gains but catastrophic risk.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C15-X1|MT107-C15-X1]] — Rich's aim is 'infinite yield': an open-ended payoff from letting profits run so that one trend pays for all accumulated costs of small controlled losses, which are the price of staying in the game for the next unknown opportunity.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C16-1|MT107-C16-1]] — Small position size is the ultimate stop since stop orders can gap.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C16-2|MT107-C16-2]] — Survival through small bets keeps capital in play for compounding.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C16-X1|MT107-C16-X1]] — Diversification in trend following serves three purposes: lower portfolio correlation and risk through diversity, a wider opportunity set, and capturing outliers wherever they appear.

## [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12]]
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C03-2|MT108-C03-2]] — Build one sub-strategy per return driver (e.g. commodity trend, term-structure arbitrage, inventory) then combine in a risk-based portfolio, instead of one big model with filters.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C06-3|MT108-C06-3]] — Short volatility carry has catastrophic tail risk in spikes, so it must be hedged and managed.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C06-4|MT108-C06-4]] — Allocate across strategies by inverse realised volatility (equal risk), which gave the most stable overall return.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C06-6|MT108-C06-6]] — Futures are unfunded, so scale exposure to a 10 percent target volatility (realised 10.26 percent over 10 years).
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C06-X2|MT108-C06-X2]] — Combining many uncorrelated sub-strategies lowers portfolio volatility, and because futures are unfunded the exposure can be levered up to restore the 10% target volatility, so diversification can be converted into return.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C09-2|MT108-C09-2]] — About 700 round trips per million per month (roughly 8,000 per year) across 12 futures.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C09-4|MT108-C09-4]] — Vishal says performance is not highly sensitive to small cost changes; he lacks exact numbers.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C10-1|MT108-C10-1]] — Moritz's math: at about 6 dollars per contract and 8,000 round turns per million, costs are about 10.8 percent a year, a very high hurdle for short-term strategies.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C10-2|MT108-C10-2]] — Short-term systems can easily overtrade, so exposure and sensitivity must be controlled.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C10-3|MT108-C10-3]] — Intraday trading needs 24-hour automated infrastructure (FIX execution, alerts, monitoring desks in India and UK), an operational overhead.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C10-X1|MT108-C10-X1]] — Cost build-up for a non-HFT futures trader: about 0.75 broker commission, 0.50 exchange fee, 0.50 clearing fee, plus spread, slippage and market impact, which brings the all-in cost to roughly 5-6 dollars per contract.

## [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the]]
- [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the#^MT109-C06-X1|MT109-C06-X1]] — Risk management principle: compromise on details (project costs, technical specs) but set hard, pre-announced lines on fundamentals (human rights, sovereignty, borders); the 'never give an inch' philosophy is not refusal to compromise.

## [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C03-5|MT110-C03-5]] — In H1 2026 oil ran up at the start of the Iran conflict then reversed; the best CTAs rode the run-up and managed risk at the turn.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C04-3|MT110-C04-3]] — Trade a broad market set because the big winning market is unknown in advance.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C04-4|MT110-C04-4]] — Too many markets dilute exposure to the winners; hence a middle ground that also preserves liquidity.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C10-X4|MT110-C10-X4]] — Changes to what official data is collected and how may be motivated by making the data look better, which is a data-integrity risk for fundamental and economic-trend systems and a reason to trust prices more.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C11-4|MT110-C11-4]] — Combining a price-based and fundamental system with systematic weights lowers volatility and smooths returns.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C13-X1|MT110-C13-X1]] — In high-risk regimes tail-risk strategies are on the right side, but the host prefers trend following as the diversifier for a high-VIX environment.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C14-X1|MT110-C14-X1]] — Global macro, like managed futures, shows an insignificant sensitivity to the high-risk regime but with a negative coefficient, so it should be given less weight as a high-VIX diversifier.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C15-4|MT110-C15-4]] — A bundle of alternative strategies does not protect against high-risk regimes; pick managed futures.

## [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336]]
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C04-1|MT111-C04-1]] — OTC and off-exchange markets can be traded at size with some human execution and ISDA arrangements, so low exchange-visible liquidity is not a barrier to adding markets.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C06-1|MT111-C06-1]] — With uncorrelated markets, Sharpe scales with the square root of the number of markets (0.1 each -> 1.0 with 100), so adding independent price drivers raises long-run trend Sharpe.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C06-4|MT111-C06-4]] — To hedge a risk-asset portfolio, trade the same instruments held in it (equity indices, Treasuries), not unrelated markets like Scandinavian power.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C07-1|MT111-C07-1]] — Alternative markets are harder to access, not smaller, and are sized with the same liquidity rules as futures; smaller markets just get smaller position size.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C07-4|MT111-C07-4]] — Crypto has exit-liquidity risk: ether fell over 20% in one day on a weekend when futures could not trade, an issue to price into position sizing.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C08-2|MT111-C08-2]] — Risk from crypto is managed by diversifying across many futures markets and across coins; investor appetite, not opportunity, can limit allocation.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C10-1|MT111-C10-1]] — Moving-average crossover positions change smoothly and incrementally, so market impact and transaction costs are low.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C12-1|MT111-C12-1]] — Portable alpha: take equity beta at index weight and port the alpha to other sources (quant, multi-strategy, trend) away from pure equities to diversify alpha sources.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C12-3|MT111-C12-3]] — Portable alpha needs margin management: the alpha strategy must not draw down while beta draws down.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C14-1|MT111-C14-1]] — Trend strategies can be run at any volatility target (5, 10, 20) by scaling a dial; investor preferences differ and asset volatility does not matter.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C14-2|MT111-C14-2]] — High-volatility assets (Bitcoin about 100 vol) are handled by smaller position size under volatility-based sizing.

## [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C04-1|MT112-C04-1]] — Leverage-based products (lending against Bitcoin on the assumption it compounds at 50%) add fragility, since most system breaks involve leverage.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C06-X1|MT112-C06-X1]] — Whether low volatility helps a trend follower depends on sizing: a trend that starts in low volatility lets volatility-scaled traders put on a large position, while fixed-size traders gain nothing from it, so the vol-performance link differs by sizing method.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C07-1|MT112-C07-1]] — Volatility tends to rise around consolidation or reversal, so dynamic vol-based sizing reduces exposure just before give-back; long sustained trends can themselves occur in low vol.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C07-3|MT112-C07-3]] — Raising leverage to hold volatility fixed after volatility falls (12 to 6) is a recipe for disaster if volatility reverses; leverage is the enemy.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C07-4|MT112-C07-4]] — Experienced managers set a floor on volatility estimates and look at long-term vol so they never take unbounded positions when vol collapses (e.g. short-rate contracts at zero rates).
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C12-1|MT112-C12-1]] — Intraday stops can trigger on large intraday moves even when the close returns above the moving average, costing longer-term trend followers positions.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C12-3|MT112-C12-3]] — Execution matters most to short-term traders, but a long-term model's execution error persists for a long time.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C14-2|MT112-C14-2]] — Empirical definition: a safe asset has low or negative correlation when markets fall; a hedge asset differs; Treasuries and bills can become a fickle safe asset.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C15-2|MT112-C15-2]] — Berkshire Hathaway had three or four drawdowns over 50%; 20-25% corrections are not worth worrying about, judge strategies over 10-40 years, as with trend following.

## [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini]]
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C01-X1|MT113-C01-X1]] — Graham runs its discretionary multi-PM macro book concentrated, betting on its highest-conviction teams (about 15) rather than spreading across 50 to 150 teams, a portfolio-construction choice favouring conviction over breadth.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C04-2|MT113-C04-2]] — Bonds are held because their lower expected return buys a hedge; with a 400-500bp equity risk premium they only make sense with a low or negative correlation to stocks.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C05-1|MT113-C05-1]] — Without the degree of freedom from a known mean, proper long-horizon calculations raise equity volatility from about 17% to about 22%, so mean-variance should hold fewer equities.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C06-1|MT113-C06-1]] — Adding 20% macro to a 60/40 over the last 20 years lowers return slightly but lowers volatility more, lifting information ratio from 0.66 to 0.75 and cutting worst drawdown from -36% to -29%.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C06-2|MT113-C06-2]] — The March 2023 Treasury yield move was a 20 sigma event that hurt dynamic trading funds; Graham was little affected but is making risk systems more robust to such shocks.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C06-3|MT113-C06-3]] — Macro is a complement to 60/40: it holds no long-term secular views (already in 60/40) and adds value by being tactical, dynamic and quick to cut positions when the thesis fails.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C06-X1|MT113-C06-X1]] — The 20-year average benefit of adding macro to 60/40 understates the benefit in stress years such as 2022, when macro can add more than the average.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C07-5|MT113-C07-5]] — Discretionary macro value is the ability to change views and positions within a month rather than stick to a view.
