- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C07-X1|MT113-C07-X1]] — A fundamental short-equity view is held with humility: unknown market forces may keep equities supported, so risk management and a tactical hedge are used to limit the cost of being early or wrong.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C08-2|MT113-C08-2]] — Trend program adds value via robust portfolio construction (mean-variance, PCA to estimate covariance matrix fast to mitigate risk) on a traditional time-series momentum signal.

## [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311]]
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C02-2|MT114-C02-2]] — The SG Trend Index was in a 10.5% drawdown from its March high, about half its worst drawdown, so August 2024 was not catastrophic.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C04-1|MT114-C04-1]] — Short-term trend models are more at risk of being whipsawed by sharp three-day repricing and missing the mild recovery.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C06-3|MT114-C06-3]] — Since 2016 the replicator beat the CTA index by about 300bp a year at 90% correlation through fee and expense disintermediation and about 10bp annual trading costs.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C07-4|MT114-C07-4]] — Adding instruments does increase diversification, but unlike a stock picker's best ideas, managed futures is a series of small-edge bets (52-55% hit rate).
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C07-X1|MT114-C07-X1]] — Diversified trend models are used as risk-management tools, and in the recent period many idiosyncratic whipsaws across the instruments the 10-factor replicator does not hold explain why a simple replica can beat the more diversified CTA index in a given year.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C08-3|MT114-C08-3]] — Claims on optimal CTA allocation range from 25% to 40% (Man, social media), while Winton says other considerations matter.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C09-3|MT114-C09-3]] — Replication is not perfect, but to get exposure to the space with less manager risk, liquidity and a wrapper nothing better has been found.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C10-1|MT114-C10-1]] — Replicator risk exists alongside manager risk: the model can go out of sync or have a bug.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C16-1|MT114-C16-1]] — Replicator idiosyncratic risk versus the SG CTA Index is much less than single-manager risk, though not zero.

## [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014]]
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C06-X2|MT115-C06-X2]] — Forecasts, however well reasoned, are overtaken by new events, so something will change; that is the nature of trading and a reason to run risk control rather than rely on a single supply-demand scenario.

## [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo]]
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C02-3|MT116-C02-3]] — Pure trend following avoids any prediction or story and so is protected when narratives (e.g. Japan hike should lift yen) fail to show up in prices.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C08-1|MT116-C08-1]] — Changing trend design (speed, universe) in response to recent out- or under-performance is the wrong approach; discipline and trust in the process matter more.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C09-3|MT116-C09-3]] — Defensive strategies range from reactive, high-bias, expensive tail hedges to carry-generating but not downside-protecting ones, with trend following as the intermediate component (RMS framework of first and second responders).
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C12-1|MT116-C12-1]] — Linear scaling of position by signal magnitude improves on a binary long/short signal because weak signals get small positions.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C12-3|MT116-C12-3]] — Binary signals only win via an early start, but when a signal oscillates around zero they flip full positions and accrue large trading costs, so net of costs linear or sigmoid sizing is preferable.

## [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv]]
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C01-2|MT117-C01-2]] — Trend portfolios should include markets without screening out past underperformers, because diversification means unpredictable markets sometimes deliver the big trends.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C06-1|MT117-C06-1]] — A mean-variance optimizer would long the stronger and short the slightly weaker of two 95%-correlated markets and lever the tiny spread, creating a high-Sharpe-looking relative value trade that is fragile to the signal ordering reversing.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C06-2|MT117-C06-2]] — Relative value positions built from small signal differentials depend entirely on forecast persistence, create concentrated positions and are arguably no longer trend following.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C07-1|MT117-C07-1]] — CFM's agnostic risk parity allocates equal risk across all principal components of the asset correlation matrix, blending the market component (long both) with a small relative value spread component.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C07-X1|MT117-C07-X1]] — Concentrating on the dominant macro trend component has diminishing value, so adding diversifying components that need not agree with trend direction (agnostic risk parity's spread trades, or carry and reversion signals) gives a 'trend plus' strategy.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C08-1|MT117-C08-1]] — Grossing up the signal differential in a pure relative value trade has too much dependence on noise and concentration, so the middle ground or pure trend direction is preferable.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C08-2|MT117-C08-2]] — Any fixed weighting scheme (e.g. 60/40, equal risk across asset classes) carries an implied return forecast and a built-in value bet because it sells winners and buys losers to rebalance.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C08-3|MT117-C08-3]] — Inverse-volatility weights across asset classes imply equal expected Sharpe ratios across them.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C09-2|MT117-C09-2]] — Scaling positions by volatility alone leaves highly correlated clusters over-represented and loosely correlated markets under-represented; correct risk balance needs correlations, not just volatility.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C09-3|MT117-C09-3]] — Trend managers give every market the same opportunity, risking the same amount only when trend strength is the same, rather than forcing equal risk by sector.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C10-1|MT117-C10-1]] — Forcing fixed risk budgets (including risk parity) embeds an expectation of equal Sharpe ratios; dynamic budgets avoid that by not forcing allocation into non-trending markets.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C10-2|MT117-C10-2]] — Mathematically, a pure long-term return maximiser would hold close to 100% trend versus 60/40, but institutions' tracking error and career risk against the policy portfolio mean there is no single optimal allocation.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C14-1|MT117-C14-1]] — Timing the end of a crowded equity momentum rally is very hard, so options are limited to managing exposure.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C15-1|MT117-C15-1]] — Volatility-scaling equity momentum exposure cuts participation when its volatility rises, a prudent way to stay in the trade while reducing crash risk.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C15-4|MT117-C15-4]] — Trend followers with dynamic position sizing scale out when a market goes volatile, which naturally reduces exposure to crowded blow-off moves.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C15-5|MT117-C15-5]] — Cocoa positions were already reduced before the reversal because the volatility spike weakened the vol-normalised signal even as price kept rising.

## [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11]]
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C01-1|MT118-C01-1]] — Risk management should ensure that no scenario can put the fund out of business, by listing what could go wrong (carry reversal, taper tantrum, oil shock) and adding each to the risk system.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C02-2|MT118-C02-2]] — DCM deliberately avoids trend signals so its program is orthogonal to the CTA index and sits next to trend allocations in a broader portfolio.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C02-X1|MT118-C02-X1]] — Adding a trend-following component to a 25-model non-trend book would probably still raise risk-adjusted returns, even though correlation to trend followers would rise, because trend has a positive expected return and diversifies the models.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C03-X1|MT118-C03-X1]] — The fund concedes that trend following has a positive expected return and that an optimal blend with it exists, but sees choosing that blend as the allocator's job; the fund's own role is to be non-overlapping so the allocator is not doubling up on what they already hold.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C04-4|MT118-C04-4]] — The shock-reversal model is run market-neutral across equity indices to limit directional risk, while other models handle vol spikes with long-volatility positioning.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C06-X1|MT118-C06-X1]] — Relative value is built either as pair spreads (Brent vs WTI, soybean oil vs soybean meal) or as larger baskets (FX, grains) with zero or controlled net exposure.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C07-1|MT118-C07-1]] — Strictly market-neutral relative value (e.g. WTI vs Brent) forces overtrading or excessive leverage, so DCM allows slack and derives positions from term structure with a limited directional exposure.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C08-1|MT118-C08-1]] — The program never holds a net short VIX position, because its role is to provide diversification in drawdowns; it uses beta-adjusted VIX curve spreads and a fast model that goes long VIX when hedging demand rises.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C08-2|MT118-C08-2]] — Risk management should be proactive using stylised scenarios (e.g. Volmageddon shape: front VIX months hit most) injected into the optimiser as a loss constraint, rather than only scaling after realised volatility.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C08-X1|MT118-C08-X1]] — Hedging-role models (such as long-VIX shock models) can produce no returns for years and have false alarms, yet are kept because their function is protection in shocks, so models are judged by role in the portfolio, not stand-alone return.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C09-1|MT118-C09-1]] — Proactive risk management stress-tests current positioning against past scenarios such as Volmageddon, while reactive risk management adjusts positions after the fact.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C09-2|MT118-C09-2]] — On 6 Feb 2018 (Volmageddon) the DCM program, long the front of the VIX curve, still netted about +3 to +4% after giving some back the next day.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C09-3|MT118-C09-3]] — Volatility-based risk management naturally cuts exposure after large gains (as in March 2020), working like automatic profit-taking and protecting capital.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C10-2|MT118-C10-2]] — FX carry loses in equity risk-off shocks, so a regression-based risk-off factor is included in the risk system and neutralised in FX models.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C10-X1|MT118-C10-X1]] — Each of the 25 models neutralises certain factors locally (US dollar carry, yield-curve level), but there is as yet no portfolio-level systematic hedging overlay; building one with an overview of the whole portfolio is the next research step.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C11-2|MT118-C11-2]] — Execution is 98% automated with pre-trade previews and manual send but no discretionary modification, with a human monitoring the process.

## [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341]]
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C06-2|MT119-C06-2]] — Quant trend signals are discounted by noise, so when volatility or uncertainty rises the signal-to-noise ratio falls and positions shrink even if trends exist.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C08-1|MT119-C08-1]] — If the environment is truly unprecedented, expert judgement has no past analogues and one should load up on quant traders who let price action speak.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C10-2|MT119-C10-2]] — Raising trend exposure helps in high uncertainty because it is long/short with variable beta, diversified across currencies, commodities and non-US equities, and follows a disciplined rule to cut exposure when trends fall.

## [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C02-2|MT120-C02-2]] — Bucket-by-bucket optimisation misses total-portfolio effects; under a total portfolio approach, trend following's total-portfolio risk reduction (locally vs globally optimal) should raise its allocation.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C02-X2|MT120-C02-X2]] — Siloed line-item and bucket construction makes each piece look optimised but hides total-portfolio effects; trend following gives total-portfolio risk reduction that many other alternatives do not, which is why a total portfolio approach fits it.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C08-3|MT120-C08-3]] — Recent events should not change the model, but they raise questions about how volatility estimates and signals respond to sustained shocks.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C08-X1|MT120-C08-X1]] — Trend systems can usually ride through a one-day shock, whose impact is bounded, but a three-day consistent move is large; the trend follower's problem is balancing trend strength against volatility, and such a sustained move was not in the backtest.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C11-2|MT120-C11-2]] — Combining price trend and economic/macro strategies depends on investor mandate: risk-mitigation investors use trend for its stress behaviour and accept modest results in strong equity years.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C13-1|MT120-C13-1]] — Adding even some managed futures to stocks and bonds improves diversification more than expected because it is one of the few genuinely different return sources.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C14-1|MT120-C14-1]] — In a model of target-retirement cash flows, managed futures improve the worst-case (bottom 5%) outcomes caused by poor timing of allocations or withdrawals.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C14-2|MT120-C14-2]] — Because individuals experience one sample path, managed futures' reduction of maximum drawdown in equity-heavy portfolios helps avoid retiring into a crash like 2008 (market down 60%).

## [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P]]
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C03-2|MT121-C03-2]] — Good but low-return quant funds were made useful by adding leverage in a dedicated share class, so the allocator scaled return to target.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-3|MT121-C05-3]] — First-stage customization of managed accounts was simple dial turning such as a higher volatility target, a foreign-currency account or excluding a few markets, so that the manager fits the rest of the portfolio.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-4|MT121-C05-4]] — To limit equity exposure in a CTA, capping the overall portfolio beta is more effective than removing equity markets, because equity risk otherwise reappears through commodities and other markets.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C06-1|MT121-C06-1]] — Because constraints, optimization and risk overlay are the last stage of a quant process, any sensible cap can be built into the optimization and yield an optimal constrained portfolio rather than a suboptimal remnant.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C06-2|MT121-C06-2]] — Hybridizing strategies by netting their signals before portfolio construction gives an optimal combined book and the beta cap need not be zero but can be set to whatever fits the client portfolio.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C06-3|MT121-C06-3]] — A completion portfolio first identifies the gap in the client's existing portfolio and then designs the best-fit exposure, an outcome-oriented rather than strategy-selection approach.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C06-5|MT121-C06-5]] — Completion portfolios can combine the manager's alpha strategies with optimal baskets of assets and volatility exposure, tailored to the role needed in the client's portfolio.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C07-3|MT121-C07-3]] — A total fund view lets an allocator target portfolio-level holes with overlays, options and risk-mitigation strategies, whereas under SAA fixing them means taking money from one bucket and creates internal political pressure.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-2|MT121-C08-2]] — Strategic asset allocation takes long-term capital market assumptions, a return target and funded status volatility and outputs an asset mix, which works well with few asset classes and small internal teams.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-6|MT121-C08-6]] — In TPA all investment opportunities compete for capital on their contribution to the whole portfolio rather than filling asset-class buckets.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C09-1|MT121-C09-1]] — Under TPA a manager must have a proposition strong enough to improve risk-adjusted return of the whole portfolio, not just one asset class.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C09-2|MT121-C09-2]] — A risk-mitigation portfolio is defined by its role of cushioning the drawdown caused by the largest risk in the total portfolio, reducing the conditional beta to it and raising liquidity and dry powder dynamically.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C09-3|MT121-C09-3]] — The risk mitigation framework combines passive and active strategies, the active ones run by a handful of managers willing to structure strategies specifically for the allocator's portfolio.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C10-1|MT121-C10-1]] — Completion funds shift from manager selection to outcome selection: the best manager is the one whose output best fills the gap in the specific portfolio.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C10-2|MT121-C10-2]] — Customization must reflect governance and other non-portfolio constraints as well as portfolio ones when formulating the optimization problem.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C10-3|MT121-C10-3]] — Optimal completion design needs the client's actual portfolio data, although anonymized data or headline proxies can be used at earlier stages.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C11-2|MT121-C11-2]] — A small internal team has implemented risk mitigation trading in-house with pretty good results, showing it can be done with limited resources.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C11-6|MT121-C11-6]] — Excluding part of the investable universe is not reliably good for performance, which investors will have to grapple with.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C12-4|MT121-C12-4]] — Client restrictions such as no grains or no fossil-fuel energy are handled as constraints in the portfolio optimization rather than as ad hoc deletions.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C13-2|MT121-C13-2]] — Under a total fund approach ESG can be implemented by mapping it into the main macro risk factors and feeding them into portfolio optimization.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C13-3|MT121-C13-3]] — ESG-based frameworks can lead to a different portfolio than intended and do not guarantee better risk-adjusted returns.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C14-2|MT121-C14-2]] — If stocks and bonds correlate positively, a 60/40 core loses its natural offset and drops together in a crisis, so investors seek diversification and loss control through completion funds, not necessarily using bonds.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C14-7|MT121-C14-7]] — Risk parity managers with an inflation focus could be a good play for the inflationary period.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C14-X1|MT121-C14-X1]] — Investors facing correlated stocks and bonds say they are already maxed out on private markets and hold a less diversified core, so they turn to completion funds with a diversification, risk-mitigation and loss-control objective rather than a pure portfolio-efficiency objective.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C15-3|MT121-C15-3]] — Investors now look for conditional (down-market) correlation and downside diversification rather than average low correlation.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C15-4|MT121-C15-4]] — Investors want to offset equity downturns through lower conditional beta and downside capture and are willing to pay active fees only for esoteric strategies that deliver this, preferring cheaper implementation otherwise.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C15-X1|MT121-C15-X1]] — Inflation-linked bonds and gold were among the instruments that formed the foundation of the risk mitigation portfolio framework and are standard inflation hedges.

## [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C03-2|MT122-C03-2]] — Elliott is an absolute return fund whose clients (universities, hospitals, charities) need performance in seriously adverse markets, not an activist-only vehicle.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C03-3|MT122-C03-3]] — Losing trades usually come from a missed risk or from hedges with more tracking error than expected, not mere bad luck.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C03-5|MT122-C03-5]] — Hedging has become more sophisticated, with bespoke hedges per trade, though even these do not work out every time.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C03-6|MT122-C03-6]] — The worst trades are those where the risk is misunderstood and the position is put in the wrong category.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C04-1|MT122-C04-1]] — Buying a supposedly late-stage bankruptcy claim that was not actually late stage and whose business did not work caused a loss, showing the need to verify stage and risk category.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C04-3|MT122-C04-3]] — The linker fell about 30% as implied inflation dropped to -4.5% per year, and daily mark-to-market with leverage means a convergent trade with maturity six years out can still destroy capital and force sell-outs.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C04-4|MT122-C04-4]] — Leveraging a position whose essential characteristic is misunderstood produces devastating losses, so leverage should depend on correct risk understanding.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C04-X2|MT122-C04-X2]] — Daily mark-to-market makes holding a losing convergent trade a matter of skill: pressure to unwind was heavy and many were sold out, but Singer kept the position through the 2008 do-or-die period and notes it requires a different skill set from managers.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C05-1|MT122-C05-1]] — Concentrated conviction positions held on margin produced an 88% loss in the 1974 bear market, which shaped a no-large-loss philosophy.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C05-4|MT122-C05-4]] — Keeping capital intact lets an investor exploit rare dislocations such as the last weeks of 2008 when everything got cheap before collapsing.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C05-5|MT122-C05-5]] — Capital preservation works like a ratchet: gains are kept and never given back, so compounding relies on avoiding large losses.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C05-6|MT122-C05-6]] — Elliott uses no benchmark; the objective is not losing money first and return second.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C06-1|MT122-C06-1]] — Because markets cannot be predicted, risk aversion must be constant rather than switched on and off.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C07-1|MT122-C07-1]] — The Argentine case took 15 years because the sovereign never negotiated; the time was not patience but having no alternative since the position was already deeply underwater.

## [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C05-3|MT123-C05-3]] — Physically settled futures converge to the physical price, so with no physical available shorts face unbounded squeezes.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C05-7|MT123-C05-7]] — In an illiquid, volatile commodity size so that a 50% adverse move can be survived; being right on direction is not enough.

## [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C01-3|MT124-C01-3]] — In extreme volatility exchanges can cancel trades (the LME did with nickel) and financing and physical delivery constraints appear, adding execution and counterparty risk.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C02-X1|MT124-C02-X1]] — Andurand holds bullish oil positions for months to years but bearish positions generally for a few months up to about a year, an asymmetry in the holding period of his large-move trades.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C03-1|MT124-C03-1]] — Andurand never sells options; he is flat or long calls and puts, so risk is capped at premium.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C03-2|MT124-C03-2]] — When expecting a big move in a short period he expresses the view with long options rather than futures, getting more leverage with less risk.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C03-X1|MT124-C03-X1]] — Bullish views are expressed through Brent, WTI, heating oil or gasoline futures with a chosen contract month: the front month for a near-term tightness view, or a deferred month (for example December Brent) for a view further along the curve.

## [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C03-1|MT125-C03-1]] — Without early demand cuts Europe faces shortage before the end of winter because storage would run out.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C08-7|MT125-C08-7]] — When the market moves against a thesis for reasons not understood, cut positions to focus on survival rather than stubbornly defending the view.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C10-2|MT125-C10-2]] — Society can absorb a 10-15% energy cut but not 50% without social unrest and a financial crisis, so the policy target is moderate demand cuts plus new supply.

## [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338]]
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C13-1|MT126-C13-1]] — Even when the event is foreseen, market reaction can be counterintuitive (for example 10-year yields rose after the Fed cut).

## [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C02-2|MT127-C02-2]] — A 45 billion fund losing about two thirds in weeks shows that concentration and leverage on one idea are dangerous and diversification matters.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C03-2|MT127-C03-2]] — Position sizing must be set so that a single position (such as the yen) cannot hurt the portfolio too much when an intervention reverses it.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C03-3|MT127-C03-3]] — The yen intervention produced a roughly 10 standard deviation move, rare and hard to simulate in market data, though such events exist.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C03-X1|MT127-C03-X1]] — The 2015 Swiss franc depeg was a 20 to 30 standard deviation move, a precedent showing that policy-driven gaps far larger than the 10 sigma yen intervention move exist and are the main tail risk for trend followers holding trending positions.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C04-2|MT127-C04-2]] — A 2x levered ETF lost over 40% while the underlying stock round-tripped to flat, showing volatility decay in levered retail products.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C07-2|MT127-C07-2]] — Stay balanced: the AI trade could also be right for 10 years, so one cannot predict and portfolios should be built for either outcome.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C07-3|MT127-C07-3]] — A portfolio of equities, bonds, commodities and private assets appears diversified in normal times but may all fall together in a 30-40% S&P crash.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C07-4|MT127-C07-4]] — Private assets mark down slowly and return capital slowly, so they give only paper protection in a crash.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C08-4|MT127-C08-4]] — In a left-tail shock, trend following is zero or negatively correlated to equities while other asset class correlations rise, making it a covariance-matrix diversifier.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C11-X1|MT127-C11-X1]] — The portable alpha structure examined sells no equity exposure: part of a passive S&P holding is held synthetically through a future or swap, and the freed cash is used to buy trend (or another alpha) on top, so the investor keeps the beta and adds the alpha.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C12-1|MT127-C12-1]] — Portable alpha structures failed in 2008 due to insufficient liquidity and alpha-beta correlation; resilient designs must survive the GFC, the dotcom bust and COVID.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C13-1|MT127-C13-1]] — A futures- or swap-based beta needs unencumbered cash beyond margin, because a 10% fall exhausts 10% margin and counterparties call for more.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C13-2|MT127-C13-2]] — A 20% unencumbered cash buffer on S&P plus trend would have been fully drawn down in the GFC, dotcom and COVID, forcing beta sales.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C13-3|MT127-C13-3]] — Forced beta selling at the lows loses the rebound (about 30% in 2009) and produces large tracking error against the benchmark.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C13-4|MT127-C13-4]] — About 40% cash (10% margin plus 30% unencumbered) means a forced degearing only once in 100-200 years, per Monte Carlo and real data.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C14-1|MT127-C14-1]] — Most investors want the manager to bear operational and cash-flow complexity, so a larger buffer is preferred over callable capital.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C14-4|MT127-C14-4]] — Recommended compromise: rebalance only when the S&P and trend sleeves drift more than about 10% out of balance.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C14-X1|MT127-C14-X1]] — Some investors can accept a smaller cash buffer because they hold callable capital or a credit facility and can post margin at short notice, but most clients want the manager to carry the operational complexity.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C14-X2|MT127-C14-X2]] — Pure buy and hold replicates the index best but causes the equity and alpha mix to drift, and makes it hard to bring new investors in at NAV with the intended mix, so the authors prefer a tolerance-band rebalance.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C15-X2|MT127-C15-X2]] — Investors in portable alpha should ask providers up front what happens in a stress month (for example a 20% drawdown): how margin is met, whether beta is sold, and what the tracking error would be.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C16-2|MT127-C16-2]] — The Turtles estimated volatility N with a range-based exponential estimator (half-life about 14 days), consistent with Parkinson's efficient range estimator.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C16-3|MT127-C16-3]] — Turtle units equalise risk across markets (one unit of crude equals one unit of Treasuries in risk), i.e. volatility-based position sizing.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C17-1|MT127-C17-1]] — The Turtles' 2% equity per 2N stop corresponds to a fractional-Kelly size on the left of the optimum, giving up some growth for much lower ruin risk.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C17-2|MT127-C17-2]] — A stop at 2N equals a loss of 2% of equity per position.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C17-3|MT127-C17-3]] — Turtles managed correlation with position-size caps on correlated markets instead of covariance inversion.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C18-1|MT127-C18-1]] — Because managers run others' money, drawdown-control rules are needed; the Turtles cut equity 20% after a 10% loss, later formalised by Grossman and Zhou.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C18-2|MT127-C18-2]] — Turtle drawdown rule: after losing 10% reduce book equity used for sizing by 20%.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C18-3|MT127-C18-3]] — The Turtles' edge was risk management and scaling rather than the breakout entry.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C18-4|MT127-C18-4]] — Trend followers control risk, not returns: small losses, intelligent scaling and surviving to catch outsized moves.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C19-1|MT127-C19-1]] — A 50/50 portfolio holds roughly 85% of its risk in equities, so leverage on bonds improves risk balance and Sharpe versus a $100 capital constraint.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C19-2|MT127-C19-2]] — Leverage is a portfolio-construction tool that can raise Sharpe when assets are risk-balanced.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C19-3|MT127-C19-3]] — Choose a portable alpha that is uncorrelated in the left tail; liquidity-provision strategies (short vol, merger arbitrage, basis trade) look good normally but have big left tails.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C19-X1|MT127-C19-X1]] — The alpha layer in portable alpha should not add beta, because extra beta in the same direction as the 100% equity holding raises volatility and the left-tail loss; the alpha must be uncorrelated, especially in the tail.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C20-2|MT127-C20-2]] — Trend scales positions inversely with volatility, so it needs less margin in turning-point stress, unlike liquidity-provision strategies.

## [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386]]
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C02-1|MT128-C02-1]] — A CTA's position in an instrument is a function of trend strength (forecast) and the instrument's volatility, so rising volatility can reduce exposure even while the trend signal strengthens.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C02-3|MT128-C02-3]] — CTAs differ: some adjust position continuously for volatility changes, some size on volatility only at entry, which drives very different outcomes in parabolic moves.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C02-X1|MT128-C02-X1]] — CTAs differ in how position size follows the trend signal: some take binary full-long or full-short positions, while others scale continuously with forecast strength; binary systems get much less benefit from forecast scaling when a trend ends abruptly.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C03-1|MT128-C03-1]] — Rob bought silver in late Nov/early Dec as the forecast grew, then sold contracts on 31 Dec and the following Monday because volatility rose, so the position shrank into the crash while the trend signal stayed long.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C03-3|MT128-C03-3]] — Rob's silver trades (bought at $53 and $59, sold at $71 and $81) were profitable even though the price peaked at $110-120 before the crash, because the crash returned silver to levels of weeks earlier.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C03-4|MT128-C03-4]] — Without continuous vol adjustment, silver grew as a share of the portfolio during the run-up, so the one-day drop caused a very large loss for such CTAs.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C03-5|MT128-C03-5]] — One highly levered CTA without vol adjustment went from +72% to +27% on the month in one day on Freaky Friday.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C03-X1|MT128-C03-X1]] — A daily-rebalanced system sizes positions from the prior close, so on a gap day such as 30 Jan 2026 it cannot reduce exposure intraday; the loss is limited only because volatility-scaling had already cut the position in the days before.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C04-1|MT128-C04-1]] — If a volatility-based stop is raised as volatility rises, position size should be reduced simultaneously, otherwise risk increases.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C04-2|MT128-C04-2]] — A stop that does not widen with volatility risks being shaken out of a trend too early.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C04-X1|MT128-C04-X1]] — A trader using a hard stop based on the recent high (possibly vol-linked) could have been stopped out on the crash day itself, so whether a stop-loss rule helps in a parabolic reversal depends on its exact definition.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C04-X2|MT128-C04-X2]] — Even a volatility-widened stop would probably have been breached by a 25% one-day fall, so stops do not remove gap risk; the size of the position, not the stop, determines the loss.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C04-X4|MT128-C04-X4]] — A small account can use a broader liquidity filter than large CTAs and so holds instruments that big CTAs avoid or hold small, which gives it extra markets but also more exposure to thin-market liquidity gaps.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C05-2|MT128-C05-2]] — Judge liquidity over a one to two year history, not recent volume/open-interest spikes caused by tourists.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C05-4|MT128-C05-4]] — Position sizing by volatility and diversification limited Rob's loss to 3% of account on the silver crash day, versus much larger for concentrated or unscaled funds.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C05-5|MT128-C05-5]] — A 3% one-day loss was in line with other CTAs at similar annual vol.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C06-1|MT128-C06-1]] — Larger CTAs were reducing silver notional as volatility expanded, so they dampen rather than cause parabolic moves.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C06-2|MT128-C06-2]] — Hunt brothers silver 1979: leveraged buying with borrowed money, exchange margin hikes and a rise from $6 to $50 ended in catastrophe; pyramiding and leverage repeat across eras.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C11-1|MT128-C11-1]] — With vol-targeted instruments, portfolio risk depends only on correlations; diversification means you must scale up positions to hit the book risk target.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C11-2|MT128-C11-2]] — Compensating for diversification across instruments requires multiplying positions by about four to five; without it a 15% target gives about 3%.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C12-4|MT128-C12-4]] — When instruments are already vol-targeted, portfolio construction only needs correlations.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C14-4|MT128-C14-4]] — An unconstrained Sharpe-maximising optimisation puts about 30% in traditional markets and 70% in alternatives (about 5% in crypto), although large CTAs cannot do this because of capacity.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C15-1|MT128-C15-1]] — Optimising for periods when equities fell gives a different answer than the generic optimisation, because investors buy trend for tail protection.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C16-2|MT128-C16-2]] — Adding cash efficiency as a constraint shifts the optimum to about 70% traditional futures and 30% alternatives.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C16-4|MT128-C16-4]] — To maximise standalone long-term Sharpe, trade more (including alternative) markets; to get equity-crisis protection, choose managers focused on traditional liquid futures.

## [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321]]
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C10-2|MT129-C10-2]] — Large performance swings occur when the three big financial sectors move together, with or against the trend, even for diversified portfolios.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C11-2|MT129-C11-2]] — Buffer ETFs only protect the first 10-15% of decline, leave a large tail risk and cap upside, so they are not a substitute for bonds.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C11-3|MT129-C11-3]] — Bonds provide stability and convexity in a big equity sell-off; trend following or managed futures is the right alternative if rising yields hurt bonds.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C11-X1|MT129-C11-X1]] — Buffered or outcome-defined ETFs are structured products (like capital-guaranteed notes) built on options, wrapped as an ETF: partial upside participation plus either full or 'soft' downside protection.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C11-X2|MT129-C11-X2]] — Buffer ETF protection applies to a fixed outcome period, so the buffer and cap you actually get depend on the entry date within that period.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C12-1|MT129-C12-1]] — Shortening bond duration (like holding cash) gives income and stability but no convexity.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C12-X1|MT129-C12-X1]] — Trend following or managed futures is the preferred bond diversifier, but retail investors (even in UCITS form) often cannot access it easily, which is the practical reason people look at alternatives such as buffer ETFs.

## [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401]]
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C06-1|MT130-C06-1]] — Designers should look at aggregate flow into a premium and overlap across strategies, not each strategy in isolation, and monitor whether market depth can absorb the demand.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C09-3|MT130-C09-3]] — In 2022 a lower price allowed recovery, but in 2026 rolling out of the current contract crystallised the negative P&L.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C10-1|MT130-C10-1]] — Diversifying premia (other commodity strategies) helped investors absorb the curve carry drawdown, and the market reaction was calmer than in past years.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C10-4|MT130-C10-4]] — Implementation matters: beta-hedging the front, dynamically selecting the back or closing the spread in backwardation reduced losses, and some variants were positive year to date.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C12-2|MT130-C12-2]] — For defensiveness choose a core universe; for the best long-term risk-adjusted return go broader at some cost to defensiveness.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C13-X1|MT130-C13-X1]] — A manager who runs a strategy directly can earn (or lose) return through execution relative to a closing price, while a QIS provider guarantees the reference price, so the client of a QIS gets no execution benefit.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C14-1|MT130-C14-1]] — In QIS slippage versus index price stays with the bank, while in a CTA it flows directly to the client's P&L.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C14-2|MT130-C14-2]] — Execution should be market-specific because order books, settlement windows and liquidity concentration differ (for example Brazilian vs CME live cattle).
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C14-3|MT130-C14-3]] — Gresham reports near-nil slippage in small markets through patient execution research; without it, slippage would cost two or three percent per year.

## [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363]]
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C05-5|MT131-C05-5]] — A long-term trend signal combined with agile risk management can look like a short-term model.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C06-1|MT131-C06-1]] — CTA model research mostly changes risk management (risk level, net/gross exposure, signal diversification) rather than signal speed.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C07-2|MT131-C07-2]] — CTA research is moving towards protecting the portfolio and anticipating falling signal-to-noise, not choosing a 3, 4 or 5 month speed.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C07-X6|MT131-C07-X6]] — A trend signal is a measure of how trendy a market is and can be used, under dynamic risk scaling, to set relative risk across assets rather than only direction.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C08-1|MT131-C08-1]] — Trend following gives defensiveness in prolonged equity or macro drawdowns plus long-term returns, with decorrelation visible over long horizons rather than locally.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C08-4|MT131-C08-4]] — Diluting trend programs to target absolute return makes them less defensive in equity crises.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C09-1|MT131-C09-1]] — Trend following already carries equity beta, so add diversifiers such as beta-neutral carry or reversion rather than more beta.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C12-1|MT131-C12-1]] — Following Dave Dredge (Convex): portfolios built to look safe and near long-term average load up on correlated strategies that fall together; maximising long-term compounding requires different construction.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C12-2|MT131-C12-2]] — Race-car analogy: best 'brakes' (long volatility, trend following) let a portfolio run faster over the long run.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C12-3|MT131-C12-3]] — Including crisis-protective strategies allows higher equity allocation (e.g. 80/20 instead of 60/40).
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C13-2|MT131-C13-2]] — Losses compound asymmetrically (10% loss needs about 11% to recover), so short-term losses reduce long-term compounding.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C13-3|MT131-C13-3]] — Unlike single-period mean-variance, a compounding investor targets long-horizon return while minimising short-horizon risk.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C14-1|MT131-C14-1]] — Short-term drawdowns matter for how wealth accumulates over years, not just terminal wealth.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C14-2|MT131-C14-2]] — Pension and income funds have annual solvency checkpoints, making short-term risk mitigation a requirement alongside long-term growth.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C14-4|MT131-C14-4]] — Long volatility or trend exposure should be judged in portfolio context, like insurance with negative standalone expected return that pays when needed.

## [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323]]
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C03-2|MT132-C03-2]] — For a multi-million VIX trade, use VIX futures rather than ETPs like VXX: ETPs add market-maker edge and rebalancing costs, futures are margined and more capital efficient when rates are 5%.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C08-3|MT132-C08-3]] — Tailored trades use short time windows: if the expected decline does not show in 1.5-2 weeks, drop the trade.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C09-X4|MT132-C09-X4]] — Short or hedged positions are hardest to get right, but used only in narrow pre-defined windows they can add risk-adjusted return and raise the Sharpe ratio of a mostly long book.

## [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini]]
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C02-X1|MT133-C02-X1]] — Pablo reads the April 2024 Iran-Israel exchange as largely symbolic, giving a short-term truce, but a line was crossed (direct attacks from each country's territory) and small war games can go wrong; a single mass-casualty strike would have forced a much harder Israeli response.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C05-2|MT133-C05-2]] — The macro index is positively correlated to equities in good markets and cuts or goes short in bad ones (conditional/negative correlation), complementing equity-heavy portfolios.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C05-3|MT133-C05-3]] — In equity drawdowns macro gained: 1990 recession (MSCI -24%, macro +6.7%), 2000-02 (-46%, +13%), 2008 (-54%, positive), 2022 (-25%, macro +10%).
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C05-4|MT133-C05-4]] — Macro is not guaranteed to hedge: Q1 2020 COVID sell-off saw macro down about 2%, unlike equity puts.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C05-X1|MT133-C05-X1]] — A macro manager claims no long or short bias but skill at anticipating bad equity markets, going short in anticipation and during them, and shifting risk-on exposure by regime.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C05-X2|MT133-C05-X2]] — In the 2022 equity drawdown (-25%) the macro index returned about 10% on average while Graham's own macro program made more than double that.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C06-1|MT133-C06-1]] — Macro offers conditional correlation: low to negative correlation to equities and bonds when they perform badly, but not always low.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C06-2|MT133-C06-2]] — Adding 20% macro to a 60/40 (1990-2023) raised return 6.7 to 7.2%, cut vol 10.2 to 8.7%, lifted Sharpe 0.65 to 0.83, cut max drawdown 36% to 29% and cut equity beta 0.7 to 0.55.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C06-X1|MT133-C06-X1]] — Adding macro lowers portfolio volatility for two reasons: the macro index runs at lower volatility than equities and has almost zero correlation to the portfolio, so the Sharpe ratio rises (return up while volatility falls).

## [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD]]
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C02-3|MT134-C02-3]] — Faster volatility estimation handled the three-day Liberation Day shock better than stable vol sizing; in 2022 faster was also better.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C05-4|MT134-C05-4]] — Recent non-correlation of assets like crypto and gold may not hold in a market shock; only designed tail strategies are non-correlated by construction.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C07-1|MT134-C07-1]] — Distinguish strategies uncorrelated in bull markets (liquidity-driven, e.g. crypto, gold) from those uncorrelated in bear markets (convexity in stress, e.g. trend); credit is the classic false diversifier.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C11-1|MT134-C11-1]] — When there is no strong edge in selecting managers, diversify allocation across them.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C13-1|MT134-C13-1]] — Investors who just want long-term equity trend need not hold alternative-market CTAs.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C15-2|MT134-C15-2]] — Dispersion across fast/slow, alternative markets and liquid futures CTAs gives different alpha sources that can be combined into a more holdable portfolio.

## [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16]]
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C02-1|MT135-C02-1]] — Portable alpha / return stacking keeps core stock and bond beta and uses futures, swaps and capital efficiency to free capital for other alpha sources (trend, carry, merger arb).
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C04-1|MT135-C04-1]] — Small-size ETF trades in less liquid futures baskets face bid-ask spreads of 20-30 bp on average and wider in stress; large trades via institutional desks can be done near NAV, and holding long term smooths cost.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C05-1|MT135-C05-1]] — CFTC limits on front-month positions can be bypassed by trading back-month contracts, giving 10x or more capacity.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C05-X1|MT135-C05-X1]] — Any fund faces a capacity trade-off: more diverse bets raise expected performance, but liquidity limits what can be traded at size, so at some point the markets you can trade no longer matter to P&L; this holds for hedge funds, mutual funds and ETFs.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C12-3|MT135-C12-3]] — Per Quantica, a longer tail of markets raises Sharpe but diverts risk from markets tied to big macro factors, reducing crisis offset.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C13-2|MT135-C13-2]] — A high-vol trend strategy is a capital-efficient version of a low-vol one (50 vol with a fifth of capital equals 10 vol) and at the limit a portable alpha overlay.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C14-1|MT135-C14-1]] — Stack only low-correlation components such as equity plus trend, which diversify each other.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C14-2|MT135-C14-2]] — SEC Rule 18f-4 caps fund VaR at 20% or two times the benchmark, limiting how far stacking can be pushed.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C14-3|MT135-C14-3]] — Nightly rebalancing keeps the beta/trend ratio fixed after losses; trend collateral needs only 25-30% of NAV with 40-50% cash buffer, so ruin risk is negligible, as seen in the April 2025 drawdown.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C14-X1|MT135-C14-X1]] — Stack design: hold equity beta in a cash-funded ETF where that is efficient and top up with S&P futures, while keeping enough cash as collateral for futures strategies, so margin never forces a sale during a drawdown.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C16-2|MT135-C16-2]] — Stacked suite distinguishes diversifying strategies (trend, carry, merger) from diversifying assets (gold, Bitcoin) as overlays on equities and bonds.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C16-X1|MT135-C16-X1]] — A merger-arbitrage ETF avoids the borrow-fee and recall risk on the short hedge leg of stock-for-stock deals by hedging via swaps as an alternative to physical shorts; most deals are cash, so hedging is rarely needed.

## [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367]]
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C05-3|MT136-C05-3]] — Under CalPERS' total portfolio approach, assets are judged by contribution to total portfolio goals, which would favour diversifiers such as trend following that look unappealing on a standalone basis.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C09-1|MT136-C09-1]] — Hedging or pairing against replication loses at least 300 bps per year on average; for investable mutual fund or UCITS managed futures the gap is greater than 300 bps.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C09-X1|MT136-C09-X1]] — The replication ETF outperformed single-manager CTAs by roughly 600-800 bps at the 2022 peak, partly because it charges no incentive fee, then gave much of that back when markets reversed in 2023, so relative performance versus managers swings widely.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C10-2|MT136-C10-2]] — Replication outperforms when the extra non-core trades or the risk management overlays of single-manager CTAs backfire.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C10-4|MT136-C10-4]] — Managers with broader diversification including commodities mitigated losses from financial sector reversals.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C11-X2|MT136-C11-X2]] — CTA losses in 2024-25 have no single clear narrative (unlike stock pickers, who can state which thesis failed); they came from many small timing, rebalancing and model-speed costs adding up, which makes the shortfall hard to diagnose and fix.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C12-2|MT136-C12-2]] — Before adding a marginal market to a CTA portfolio, test whether a stress-day execution loss (e.g. 200 bps) would have justified inclusion; liquidity under stress should be a market-selection criterion.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C13-1|MT136-C13-1]] — A trend only has value if transaction costs allow capture (Copenhagen housing example): trendfollowing needs low costs.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C13-2|MT136-C13-2]] — Strategies like high-frequency trading earned huge returns on a few million dollars but alpha disappeared at 30-50 million, so capacity and implementation limit what backtests suggest.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C13-3|MT136-C13-3]] — Slippage measured on simple 70-market liquid portfolios may not hold when trading 300 instruments; quants once boasted of cutting slippage from 400 to 200 bps.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C13-4|MT136-C13-4]] — Allocators judge strategies by worst-day outcomes, e.g. private credit risk lies in defaults and markdowns, so execution and liquidity must be evaluated for stress scenarios.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C14-2|MT136-C14-2]] — Multistrategy hedge funds had a worst month of -6% in March 2020 (about -1% at 5x unleveraged equivalent) while delivering consistent returns.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C14-X1|MT136-C14-X1]] — Trading liquid exchange-listed futures is a structural advantage over off-exchange or illiquid instruments because exit costs and valuations stay transparent in stress, so liquidity should be stressed as a selection criterion.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C17-3|MT136-C17-3]] — Despite 5x leverage on $50bn and major regime shifts since 2020, no multistrat blew up or had a catastrophic drawdown, which suggests the pod structure with hedge fund format works.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C18-1|MT136-C18-1]] — Pod managers face harsh drawdown limits, a down 3% year can mean dismissal, which drives risk discipline and hunger.

## [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -]]
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C03-1|MT137-C03-1]] — Cheaper, more transparent managed futures ETFs outperformed many established CTA funds in 2025 and attracted inflows.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C04-1|MT137-C04-1]] — In 2025, differences between CTAs, ETFs and replicators were driven by exposure (how much equity, fixed income) and market universe, more than model type, so conclusions about which approach is better are dangerous.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C04-3|MT137-C04-3]] — Carver's dynamic risk sizing was below average, consistent with a neutral trend barometer (48); he was in a drawdown of just over 10% with biggest short in Bitcoin and net long equities.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C09-2|MT137-C09-2]] — Institutional-size slow trend systems should spread execution through the day to cut impact, and can run dynamic optimisation continuously to smooth trades, though reacting to big intraday moves can lose if prices bounce back.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C09-3|MT137-C09-3]] — Intraday trend trading has higher costs because more trades are needed and passive limit orders do not work when catching a short trend.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C11-1|MT137-C11-1]] — Three things kill systematic traders: excessive costs, excessive leverage and overfitting.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C12-1|MT137-C12-1]] — Choosing between static and dynamic sizing depends on objective: positive skew improves geometric return but hurts Sharpe, negative skew the opposite.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C12-2|MT137-C12-2]] — Continuous trading re-evaluates positions on volatility, trend strength and possibly account size, and fixed-vol-target systems are also affected by correlations.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C13-1|MT137-C13-1]] — Without volatility scaling a sudden jump (cocoa) is held at full size, giving outsized positive outlier returns and positive skew but lower Sharpe.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C13-2|MT137-C13-2]] — Theory shows that the skew gained from not scaling position size to volatility does not compensate for the Sharpe lost, giving lower CAGR than dynamic vol sizing.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C13-3|MT137-C13-3]] — A high-Sharpe, less skewed system with a bit more leverage can beat the geometric return of high-skew, high-vol CTAs.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C13-4|MT137-C13-4]] — Rebalancing frequency trades off tracking the optimal position against trading costs.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C14-1|MT137-C14-1]] — Daily rebalancing to a noisy volatility estimate creates churn and extra trading costs that exceed the benefit.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C14-2|MT137-C14-2]] — Buffering (trade only if far from optimum) or smoothing (moving average of target position, EWMA vol) allows frequent rebalancing without extra cost.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C14-3|MT137-C14-3]] — Volatility should be treated symmetrically for sizing: upside risk equals downside risk and a coin flip away from big gains turning into big losses.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C16-X2|MT137-C16-X2]] — Carver's system uses no stop losses and no ATR; it sizes by standard deviation of returns and handles risk through position sizing instead.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C19-1|MT137-C19-1]] — SAA box structure ignores correlations across boxes, e.g. a CTA correlated to Danish equities added to a Danish-equity-heavy portfolio.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C20-1|MT137-C20-1]] — CTAs do not fit in the hedge fund box or the tail-protection box because their correlation is different, they have no crisis guarantee but a positive Sharpe, so portfolio-level assessment helps them.

## [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025]]
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-4|MT138-C02-4]] — After DeepSeek, Nvidia's market cap fell by roughly two Mexicos in a day, showing how under-capitalised markets like Mexico and Brazil are in world indices and the case for taking geographic diversification seriously.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-X3|MT138-C02-X3]] — In a tricky, dislocated world a portfolio needs short positions and dynamic protection at certain points, rather than being structurally long.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C03-1|MT138-C03-1]] — Market-cap-guided investors are concentrated in the US; true diversification means markets with different growth, inflation, monetary policy and elections (Latin America, Asia).
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C05-1|MT138-C05-1]] — How much risk and where is the key question; take moderate risk, mostly outside the United States.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C07-1|MT138-C07-1]] — Many uncorrelated opportunities exist outside the benchmark holdings, so portfolios should ask what diversifies what they hold.

## [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,]]
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C05-1|MT139-C05-1]] — Julian Robertson and George Soros exited a position fully at market when conviction was lost instead of waiting for a better price, because waiting tends to lead to worse exits.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C06-1|MT139-C06-1]] — Shorts have unlimited loss so size must stay small, and shorts should be chosen where skew is heavily favourable (risk 10-15, make 75-80).
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C07-2|MT139-C07-2]] — Distinguish shorts with catalysts that fall 30-40% from broken business models heading to bankruptcy; conviction should stay high on the latter even at lower prices.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C12-3|MT139-C12-3]] — Net exposure goes up to 20-25% net short in equities; credit risk is about half and currency about a third of equity volatility.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C12-4|MT139-C12-4]] — At peak $15bn the fund diluted ideas; it will not return to that size because the markets' liquidity is worse than 10-15 years ago.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C16-1|MT139-C16-1]] — In 2019 two large positions on US regulatory decisions went wrong; positions were too big (about 10-15 of the portfolio) and illiquid, making reduction hard.

## [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests]]
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL.
