Now He Invests#^MT140-C05-2|MT140-C05-2]] — Sharpe assumes symmetric, normal returns; LTCM-type strategies (option selling, CDS premium: small gains, rare disasters) are negatively skewed and should not be judged by Sharpe.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C05-4|MT140-C05-4]] — Sharpe is the go-to metric for leveraged futures traders, but for unleveraged investors geometric return is more appropriate; maximising Sharpe maximises geometric return only under normality and free leverage.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C07-2|MT140-C07-2]] — Unrealistic backtest performance leads to excessive leverage (LTCM) and trading too fast with high costs, so a realistic backtest matters even if the final model is the same.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C08-2|MT140-C08-2]] — Diversifying across many trading rules beyond pure trend reduces exposure to trend decay; slow trading lets Carver ignore decay.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C08-9|MT140-C08-9]] — Short-horizon mean reversion exists but does not survive trading costs for Carver.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C11-2|MT140-C11-2]] — Formal proposals for new strategies needed review by head of risk and CIO with veto; this rigour was valuable though mistakes still happened.

## [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)]]
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C03-1|MT141-C03-1]] — For concentrated long-term investors the same stock views can be enhanced with leverage and options, so there are many ways to express one investment idea.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-3|MT141-C05-3]] — Once a view is formed choose the instrument with the best payoff: stocks, futures curve (forward curve may predict decline) or options, depending on what the market is pricing.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-4|MT141-C05-4]] — Before sizing a new idea check its correlation to the existing book: an apparently unique idea (e.g. in Australia) can be 98% R-squared with the S&P, i.e. just alternate beta.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-5|MT141-C05-5]] — Conviction should build as confidence rises (Druckenmiller: above the 50th percentile of confidence, grow the position) while avoiding analysis paralysis.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-6|MT141-C05-6]] — Concentration with high conviction means you may sit in cash until a fat pitch appears (Ted Williams: only swing at strikes).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-X2|MT141-C05-X2]] — Druckenmiller discarded the formal asset-allocation matrix for concentrated thematic bets (about 40% in oil stocks in the 1970s), i.e. concentration on high-conviction themes over formulaic diversification.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C09-1|MT141-C09-1]] — Liquid markets resemble cards and backgammon rather than chess: events (Israel, Taiwan drills) are dealt randomly and success comes from managing what arrives, unlike private equity where all moves are on the board.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C09-2|MT141-C09-2]] — Key Square runs a few large positions on big themes with a small team plus about 30 regional consultants rather than local offices.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C10-2|MT141-C10-2]] — Expressing a reflation view: the yen is the cleanest trade, with Japanese equities a derivative; avoid shorting JGBs (widowmaker) because JGB yields might fall before rising.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C10-8|MT141-C10-8]] — Build positions in thirds and size according to what the market offers, e.g. vol-implied asymmetry in options.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C11-1|MT141-C11-1]] — Add to winners when the year's P&L is good: position size is set by P&L cushion rather than only by conviction as in fundamental investing.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C11-2|MT141-C11-2]] — Risk-taking capacity follows chip stack: bet more when you have more capital in hand for the year (like poker or backgammon, Annie Duke thinking in bets).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C11-4|MT141-C11-4]] — Define risk as probability times severity, not volatility: e.g. China holds a small chance of a Russia-style day (equities down 95% in a day).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C11-5|MT141-C11-5]] — Liquidity rule: be concentrated, illiquid or leveraged but never all three; prefer liquid and use leverage, and be able to exit part of the portfolio in 24-72 hours.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C14-1|MT141-C14-1]] — Prepare ahead and assume anything can happen so you are ready when a large event arrives (be prepared to get lucky).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C14-X2|MT141-C14-X2]] — Druckenmiller's reported record of never a down year is the benchmark for macro risk control in this tradition.

## [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King]]
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C01-2|MT142-C01-2]] — Know your own risk tolerance and do not get out over your skis, so you are never forced to sell at the bottom or chase at the top.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C11-1|MT142-C11-1]] — Buffett's rules (don't lose money; don't forget rule one) and chip-stack logic: the risk you take depends on how big your stack is.

## [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299]]
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C04-4|MT143-C04-4]] — Even correct fundamental shorts can fail through takeover premiums or prolonged mispricing (Chanos story), so being right on fundamentals does not make a short work.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C07-1|MT143-C07-1]] — Phase into a 5% CTA allocation by thirds over three quarters (dollar cost averaging) to avoid entering at the worst point, as many investors did in 2022 before a bad 2023.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C08-2|MT143-C08-2]] — Managers with fast exits get out earlier on reversals but are likely to underperform in sustained trend years like 2022; the choice depends on loss tolerance vs long-term returns.

## [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312]]
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C01-2|MT144-C01-2]] — In a drawdown models find weak forecasts and many markets have low volatility, so a volatility-targeted trend system runs low risk.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C04-4|MT144-C04-4]] — A trend system reduces risk for three reasons: trends turn against you (continuous forecast or stop loss), volatility rises (for vol-targeted systems), and you are losing money.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C05-1|MT144-C05-1]] — Scale positions with account value: after a 10% loss cut positions by 10% (open or closed trade equity).
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C07-1|MT144-C07-1]] — If correlation to existing strategy is around 0.4, allocate 5-10% even if the backtested Sharpe falls, trusting diversification more than Sharpe.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C07-3|MT144-C07-3]] — Instrument and forecast diversification multipliers (IDM/FDM) rescale combined streams back to the target standard deviation: with 150-200 markets portfolio volatility falls from 25% to about 8% without them.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C09-1|MT144-C09-1]] — Separate position size (risk) from holding period (speed of trading) instead of tying both to stops.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C09-3|MT144-C09-3]] — A wider ATR stop means more risk per trade but fewer trades, so overall risk is similar or lower than a tight stop with 2% per trade, which trades often and has bigger volatility and drawdowns.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C09-X1|MT144-C09-X1]] — Trading speed should follow instrument cost: his retail-oriented book system uses a slow 8 ATR stop (about three-month holds) because the instruments are expensive to trade, whereas futures can be traded somewhat faster.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C15-2|MT144-C15-2]] — The ETF is just a legal wrapper, not a safety label; it can hold single shares triple-leveraged.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C16-1|MT144-C16-1]] — A 3x leveraged daily ETP on MicroStrategy (LMI3) lost about 82% while MicroStrategy rose over 100%, showing the compounding damage of leveraged ETFs.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C18-1|MT144-C18-1]] — With 30-50 million, pick around 10 CTA managers since they are correlated.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C19-2|MT144-C19-2]] — High correlation between CTAs does not prevent outliers; picking your own CTAs can miss an exploding manager, like missing Nvidia in a picked stock portfolio.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C20-2|MT144-C20-2]] — Carver's own implementation generates positions in about 250 futures (60 untradeable) and replicates them in a small account.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C22-1|MT144-C22-1]] — Understand what is inside any ETF; the new investable CTA index is more trustworthy than a triple-leveraged Bitcoin ETP.

## [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C05-6|MT145-C05-6]] — In 2022 pods were hedged by their momentum sleeves, so they needed less liquidity than in 2020.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C07-1|MT145-C07-1]] — Standard deviation misses risk: a stock rising 5% a day and one rising 1% a day both have zero volatility, so vol budgets are blind to some risk.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C07-2|MT145-C07-2]] — PMs size leverage from standard deviation and long-short correlation, so low vol plus high correlation allows 30x leverage.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C12-2|MT145-C12-2]] — The firm kept equal risk allocation across sectors (long-cycle factor exposure) rather than dropping sectors that did poorly in recent years, avoiding recency optimisation.

## [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358]]
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C05-1|MT146-C05-1]] — Trend followers using the same techniques have large performance dispersion because of model speed, risk allocation, markets, stops and risk-cutting, so drawdown shape matters.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C05-2|MT146-C05-2]] — Drawdown shape matters for risk management; Man Group research finds V-shaped recoveries have become more common after fast risk cutting, but may not persist.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C07-3|MT146-C07-3]] — Even among the 20 SG CTA index managers dispersion is large in almost every year, so allocators diversify across several CTAs.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C08-1|MT146-C08-1]] — There was a 30-point gap between best and worst funds in H1 2025, driven for example by avoiding equities (good in 2022) or currencies (good this year).
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C08-2|MT146-C08-2]] — Replicating a pool of funds reduces single-manager risk, with correlations around 90% versus mid-80s for individual managers.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C13-1|MT146-C13-1]] — Implicit trading costs (market impact) are far larger than assumed; the all-in implementation cost of a typical CTA is about 200bp, probably more.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C13-2|MT146-C13-2]] — Adding esoteric markets can add about 400bp of implicit costs, so a CTA must expect much higher trending in market 193 versus market 14 to net positive; he calls this a complexity crisis.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C13-X2|MT146-C13-X2]] — Counter-argument: CTAs weigh transaction costs and slippage before adding a market, have become implementation experts over 10-15 years, and futures have moved from exotic to mainstream, so costly markets would not be added unless they raise the risk-adjusted return.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C14-1|MT146-C14-1]] — Excluding a market assumes the chosen markets will beat it on a cost-adjusted basis; institutional allocators want diversification of equity risk and alternative markets like commodities may help in stress.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C14-X1|MT146-C14-X1]] — Institutions diversify equity risk and want risk-mitigating portfolios rather than crisis alpha per se, so the CTA role is to diversify the primary risk asset (equity) across regimes.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C15-2|MT146-C15-2]] — Implicit costs are roughly 3-5 times explicit costs for large players; he benchmarks every trade against arrival price (BestEx).
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C16-2|MT146-C16-2]] — UCITS rules allow some large CTAs to report zero implementation costs, which is wrong (true cost 80-250bp), per a TransTrend paper.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C17-1|MT146-C17-1]] — Trend following has a naturally low Sharpe, so implementation efficiency (trade in liquid instruments) matters greatly.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C19-1|MT146-C19-1]] — Managers pushed into complexity; five years on they should beat simple replication but have not, implying costs or fees exceed the benefit; multistrats at least are open about 1200bp fee loads for a 2 Sharpe.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C20-1|MT146-C20-1]] — The late-2010s 'long winter' may be partly due to industry costs, and another may come, when cost efficiency matters most.

## [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C05-3|MT148-C05-3]] — In a bear market, aggressive shorts get hurt in sharp rallies, so he waits for a fat pitch.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C05-X1|MT148-C05-X1]] — Signs of economic weakness at the front end made the short fixed-income position much less attractive than three to six months earlier, even though he is not comfortable owning bonds, so he cut the position size.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C05-X2|MT148-C05-X2]] — A multi-asset mandate lets him sit out any asset class that lacks a clear edge, here fixed income, instead of forcing exposure.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C08-1|MT148-C08-1]] — He runs concentrated positions (15-20% of assets, 200-300% in macro) and monitors them daily, because stale longs/shorts are the main source of trouble.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C08-2|MT148-C08-2]] — From Soros: sizing is 70-80% of returns, i.e. how much you make when right and lose when wrong, not whether you are right.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C09-3|MT148-C09-3]] — A stock idea is sized up when it fits a standing macro view (e.g. copper bullish for 3-5 years).
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C14-X3|MT148-C14-X3]] — The main risk to the long-energy trade is a worldwide recession causing demand destruction; he monitors for it and has not seen it yet.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C15-1|MT148-C15-1]] — With no historical analog he has more humility and keeps open-mindedness to something really bad.

## [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C04-2|MT149-C04-2]] — He keeps a 2008-style tail outcome in the risk matrix without forecasting it.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C05-4|MT149-C05-4]] — The best response to a hard landing depends on the policy reaction over the next six to nine months, so stay flexible.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C06-3|MT149-C06-3]] — He sizes large on high-conviction asymmetric trades and waits otherwise.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C07-2|MT149-C07-2]] — With no fat pitch, keep gross low (about 60%) and net near zero, preserve capital for post-hard-landing dispersion.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C07-3|MT149-C07-3]] — Treasuries are his usual recession hedge but at 3.5% with the Fed at 5% and inflation risk they are not a fat pitch, so the hedge is off the table.

## [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347]]
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C04-3|MT150-C04-3]] — In stagflation the long bond does not rally to offset equity declines because inflation is sticky, so bonds fail as a hedge.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C10-1|MT150-C10-1]] — 60/40 and private alternatives are all long assets exposed to the same discount-rate and liquidity risk; real diversification needs trend following or non-correlated strategies.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C16-1|MT150-C16-1]] — With fat tails everywhere and many policy variables now dynamic, one cannot predict the tail event but can plan for a wide leptokurtic distribution.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C16-3|MT150-C16-3]] — Diversification buys resilience, not return, and has not paid off while seven stocks or one index dominated; passive may revert to active.

## [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C06-4|MT151-C06-4]] — He prefers avoiding big drawdowns because the job is stressful, and is not focused on risk-weighted return measures.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C07-2|MT151-C07-2]] — Sell when the reason for the investment has gone, regardless of entry price; cost basis is irrelevant.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C07-5|MT151-C07-5]] — Concentration in the highest-conviction ideas is a main driver of return; he learned sizing from Soros.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C07-6|MT151-C07-6]] — Trading across five asset buckets (equities, bonds, currencies, commodities, credit) lets him sit out equities during bear markets and avoid down years, since bond and currency moves are largest then.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C07-X1|MT151-C07-X1]] — Experience and confidence in your ability to find new trades make it easy to clear the slate: positions held out of inertia, with conviction gone, should be sold so that capital goes to the best current ideas.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C08-2|MT151-C08-2]] — Soros's lesson: high batting average is less important than slugging; bet very large when conviction is high.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C08-6|MT151-C08-6]] — He first took a 20-25% position (invest then investigate), accepting a small expected loss from carry, to be on before the break.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C09-3|MT151-C09-3]] — Soros's view: a one-way bet with limited downside deserves leverage beyond the fund size (200%).
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C10-1|MT151-C10-1]] — Soros played the third to sixth innings of a move with great leverage, once uncertainty was lower, while Druckenmiller prefers the turn.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C10-2|MT151-C10-2]] — Payoff asymmetry matters more than hit rate: maximize gains when right and minimize losses when wrong.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C12-4|MT151-C12-4]] — He built a 350% 10-year-equivalent Treasury position in 2000 as information (earnings model, weak clients, inappropriate Fed tightening) kept confirming and the economy weakened, making 40% in Q4.

## [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview]]
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C03-2|MT152-C03-2]] — Risk-reward of the Fed pausing: if wrong, pausing costs little credibility, but hiking and shrinking the balance sheet by $50 billion a month with ECB not offsetting if the indicators are right costs five to ten times more.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C07-5|MT152-C07-5]] — Shorting stocks in bear markets is hard because of vicious rallies and squeezes.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C09-5|MT152-C09-5]] — Pair short credit with long Treasuries: credit shorts profit when the economy falls apart (the same scenario in which Treasuries rally), but a credit short alone loses if rates rise.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C09-X1|MT152-C09-X1]] — Do not hold longs only because they will fall less than the market; if the macro view is bearish, take real hedges or shorts instead, and hold longs only where the thesis stands on its own (here, growth names that benefit from lower rates).
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C10-4|MT152-C10-4]] — He prefers asymmetric one-way bets over binary two-sided outcomes.

## [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets]]
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C04-5|MT153-C04-5]] — He cannot hedge algo risk directly; he trusts instinct and technical analysis, becoming more cautious when risk-reward deteriorates.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C05-3|MT153-C05-3]] — His style: build a thesis others lack, put on a starter position, then add heavily when momentum confirms (slugging percentage over batting average, like Soros).
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C09-3|MT153-C09-3]] — Concentrated bets belong in liquid 24-hour markets (bonds, currencies), where you can change your mind; he did not trust himself to hold 30-40% in illiquid equity.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C09-X1|MT153-C09-X1]] — Most of a manager's return comes from two or three ideas even in a 40-position book, so concentration in the best ideas (Soros lesson: capital preservation plus a really big bet) drives performance rather than diversification.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C09-X2|MT153-C09-X2]] — Large illiquid equity bets (Buffett, Icahn style) require tolerating very long deep drawdowns (he cites Berkshire down 40% from 1998 to 2008, a figure as stated and unverified); a hedge fund facing redemptions cannot, so he confines big bets to liquid markets he can reverse.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C10-2|MT153-C10-2]] — In fixed-exchange-rate trades there is no price momentum to confirm, so he goes all in at once; the pound, Italy and Sweden followed the DM.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C10-3|MT153-C10-3]] — Normal sizing: start with about a third of the position, wait for price confirmation (technical signal), then go.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C11-4|MT153-C11-4]] — Size by recent performance: when down (cold) play small and test the rhythm; exception for rare high-conviction ideas (2000 Treasuries) after a fresh start.

## [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025]]
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C01-7|MT154-C01-7]] — After the easy 2024 (multiples rose, everything worked), 2025 is a different regime, so PMs need a higher bar and caution with data-dependent and tariff-exposed names.
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C02-4|MT154-C02-4]] — During losing streaks return to a consistent process, because good processes can still produce bad stretches (a baseball slump analogy).
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C05-2|MT154-C05-2]] — Preparation prevents many mistakes, and first drawdowns are normal scars to learn from rather than disasters.

## [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328]]
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C03-2|MT155-C03-2]] — Rather than predicting events, think in broad distributions across every vector and shift them for known structural pressures; this is a real edge.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C04-1|MT155-C04-1]] — In the chaotic tail regions uncertainty cannot be quantified, so positive convexity is the way to handle it; models that ignore uncertainty come undone when it hits.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C04-2|MT155-C04-2]] — Risk is volatility, correlation and estimation error; uncertainty is the higher-order remainder, so portfolio construction should allow for the unseen.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C07-3|MT155-C07-3]] — Classic trend following diversifies across parameter sets, markets and strategies to capture crisis alpha.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C07-4|MT155-C07-4]] — Many investors lack tail protection and uncorrelated diversifiers, a case for wider access to CTA exposure.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C09-1|MT155-C09-1]] — Managed futures is uncorrelated to stocks and bonds; this matters now that stock-bond correlation is positive, it does best when needed and has no gates or lock-ups.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C09-X1|MT155-C09-X1]] — Contrast with multi-strats: managed futures is liquid and daily-priced, with no gates or multi-year lock-ups, whereas a multi-strat's high costs (caption '800 or 1000 basis points a year') and long lock-ups mean you learn only after 5-10 years whether it was a good allocation.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C10-2|MT155-C10-2]] — Private equity, VC and real estate are not true alternatives because they are equity/duration beta; true alternatives are non-correlated derivative strategies.

## [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151]]
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C06-4|MT156-C06-4]] — As a fiduciary, systematic managers can avoid repression by moving away from long government bonds when valuations are poor, since repression transfers wealth from investors.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C08-1|MT156-C08-1]] — Systematic funds are engineered to deliver a known return profile; trend has a specific profile that a well risk-budgeted implementation delivers, though not in all environments.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C09-X2|MT156-C09-X2]] — Managed futures has a dual mandate, long-run return uncorrelated to equities and crisis alpha at times of stress, and the balance between them shapes signal speed and risk allocation across markets.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C10-3|MT156-C10-3]] — Use the law of active management: with weak per-bet skill, win through breadth and transfer coefficient.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C11-2|MT156-C11-2]] — Their trend ETF trades about 40+ liquid contracts across FX, rates, commodities and equities, chosen by testing lists for liquidity, diversification and capacity.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C11-3|MT156-C11-3]] — It is hard to forecast which environment (tails or middle) is next, so hold trend for diversification not for timing.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C12-5|MT156-C12-5]] — Defensive alpha is strong in very down markets and okay in up markets (long-put-like payoff) while being beta-neutral.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C12-6|MT156-C12-6]] — Trend has a smile and defensive alpha a different convex shape; combine uncorrelated payoff profiles.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C12-7|MT156-C12-7]] — Their multi-strategy blends uncorrelated profiles with fixed-income-like volatility as a bond complement, while the ETF version is a higher-octane equity substitute.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C13-X1|MT156-C13-X1]] — Combining several defensive strategies is motivated by different equity-drawdown types (short sharp sell-offs versus prolonged ones like 2022 or the dot-com unwind), since each strategy protects in a different kind of drawdown.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C14-1|MT156-C14-1]] — ETF wrappers constrain liquidity, capacity and leverage versus Cayman hedge fund vehicles; so quant strategies must be adapted.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C15-1|MT156-C15-1]] — Leverage limits designed for beta exposure misfit long-short neutral strategies.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C15-2|MT156-C15-2]] — Low-volatility contracts need huge notional per unit of risk, so contract choice weighs risk per notional too.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C17-X1|MT156-C17-X1]] — Small tilts around a concentrated market beta do not diversify enough; the answer is allocating a sizeable portion (the 20% of 50/30/20) to uncorrelated strategies.

## [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421]]
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C09-2|MT157-C09-2]] — In the GFC bonds only preserved capital, high yield fell 22%, REITs and commodities (-50%) failed as diversifiers, while the SG CTA index rose 11%.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C10-1|MT157-C10-1]] — Each crisis broke the previous diversifier playbook (2022: bonds -15-20%, REITs -25%, gold flat), while CTAs gained again with oil lifting GSCI, so crisis alpha is the strongest argument for the space.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C10-3|MT157-C10-3]] — Volatility strategies worked in Volmageddon 2018 and 2020 but not in 2022, because the volatility was a different kind; no crisis hedge is guaranteed.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C10-4|MT157-C10-4]] — Tactical trading controls drawdowns (commodities fell 80% peak to trough) because managed futures do not white-knuckle hold losing assets.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C10-5|MT157-C10-5]] — Switching among equity styles gives little statistical diversification, whereas bond, REIT and commodity categories have positive correlation to equities.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C11-2|MT157-C11-2]] — Low correlation to traditional assets is the strongest input for including CTAs in an efficient frontier, regardless of daily sign.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C11-4|MT157-C11-4]] — Max monthly drawdown of SG CTA is 16% vs US Agg 17%, Global Agg 24%, S&P 51%, Nasdaq 81%, REITs 69%, gold 42%, commodities 87%.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C12-1|MT157-C12-1]] — Tactical (trend) rules cut losers, so even if long commodities they would not stay long through an 87% decline: capital preservation.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C12-2|MT157-C12-2]] — Individual funds' max drawdowns are ~25% (13% to high 30s; high 40s including departed funds), so the 16% needs diversification across several funds.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C13-X1|MT157-C13-X1]] — Volatility and other crisis hedges are insurance (you pay a premium and hope), which is psychologically hard to hold, while CTAs protect in crises and also have a positive long-run Sharpe.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C21-2|MT157-C21-2]] — Use two complementary CTA funds so that at least one tells a good story each year, and judge on a 10-year horizon.

## [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304]]
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C02-2|MT158-C02-2]] — Volatility-scaled trend followers cut a market's position as its volatility rises, so they were not driving the cocoa surge even while it was a key winner.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C02-X1|MT158-C02-X1]] — Convergent trades such as capital-structure arbitrage carry event risk (an LBO announcement can put you on the wrong side with leverage), whereas trend following responds to prices and so avoids being wrong-footed by a single event.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C03-2|MT158-C03-2]] — The more asset classes and markets in a trend portfolio the better; crypto and China are offered as separate vehicles because some investors object to them in the core portfolio.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C03-X3|MT158-C03-X3]] — Bitcoin and Ether futures are not big liquid futures markets, so capacity is limited, which together with investor objections is why crypto is run in separate vehicles.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C06-2|MT158-C06-2]] — Bonds failed as an equity hedge in 2022 (worst year in about 200) while trend gained, so trend can substitute part of a bond allocation.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C06-3|MT158-C06-3]] — Investors increasingly consider trend alongside or in place of part of a bond allocation, as bonds no longer reliably rally when equities sell off.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C07-X1|MT158-C07-X1]] — Choose the trend mix by purpose: liquid-futures trend for a hedge opposite a beta book (crisis alpha), alternative markets for steady long-run return without crisis alpha, with the combination offering maximum diversification.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C09-1|MT158-C09-1]] — Trend portfolios target risk levels, so volatility (low, or 10-15%) is chosen by investor preference.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C10-1|MT158-C10-1]] — ESG exclusions of commodities cut low-correlation return sources, a tradeoff between returns and policy.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C11-1|MT158-C11-1]] — Allocations under 2% do not move the needle; the right trend weight depends on the metric (Sharpe, drawdown) and portfolio vol (7-8% BTOP vs 12-14% SG Trend), but most hold too little.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C11-X1|MT158-C11-X1]] — In 2022 traditional portfolios fell 15-20% while BTOP50 (about 15%) and SG Trend (10-20%) rose, so on a one-for-one basis a few percent allocation to trend is too small to offset a bad year for stocks and bonds.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C12-2|MT158-C12-2]] — Trend is vulnerable at macro reversals, so diversify with similar yield-put strategies such as long-short quality, which handles flight-to-quality episodes like SVB.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-1|MT158-C13-1]] — AHL's research: every market can trend, so diversify across many distinct price drivers rather than correlated contracts.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-2|MT158-C13-2]] — Portfolios target a fairly constant long-run volatility, a little above in trending environments and below when trends are scarce, unchanged over 13 years.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-3|MT158-C13-3]] — Volatility scaling needs a safeguard because as volatility approaches zero the position size goes to infinity.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-4|MT158-C13-4]] — Over the last 13 years the main determinant of performance has been diversification via many markets, especially alternative markets.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-X1|MT158-C13-X1]] — Niels's recollection: early European CTAs targeted a volatility level, whereas most managers now only have risk limits and accept volatility fluctuating; Graham replies that AHL still targets a fairly constant long-run volatility.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C15-3|MT158-C15-3]] — The 1-year rolling correlation of the 10-year Treasury with the S&P 500 hit its highest in 27 years, weakening bonds as diversifier.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C16-X1|MT158-C16-X1]] — Whether to restrict equity exposure inside trend depends on the objective: capping long equity or beta (not trading equities, no longs, or constraining beta) may soften sudden sell-offs but gives up long-run return, so choose by whether the aim is long-term performance, crisis alpha or bond replacement.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C17-1|MT158-C17-1]] — Reducing equity exposure is better done in the equity portfolio than by capping equities inside trend following.

## [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger]]
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C12-2|MT159-C12-2]] — Trade or export decisions are evaluated in isolation but combined actions can trigger butterfly effects and escalation, up to hot conflict.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C14-2|MT159-C14-2]] — When uncertainty rises, discount rates rise and multiples fall, so a manager cuts directional units of risk (Altimeter reduced exposure).
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C15-X1|MT159-C15-X1]] — For a high-flying single stock, government action (export bans, a possible customer audit of shipments to Singapore) is the dominant and least forecastable risk, e.g. $12B of annual China sales at stake.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C16-3|MT159-C16-3]] — Despite a 10% drawdown, exposure should stay in the bottom third of normal until clarity on tariffs arrives; buying the dip needs more certainty.

## [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301]]
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C03-1|MT160-C03-1]] — After trends reversed or weakened, a volatility-targeted trend system automatically runs at about half its long-term risk target because forecasts and positions shrink.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C04-X2|MT160-C04-X2]] — The Spitznagel result requires two pieces: a permanent systematic programme of far out-of-the-money puts (the crash date is unknowable) and an explicit rule to monetise the puts when volatility spikes (listener question relayed by Niels).
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C05-2|MT160-C05-2]] — Adding a negative-return asset that is negatively correlated with the portfolio raises the Sharpe ratio, which allows more leverage and hence a higher geometric return.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C05-X3|MT160-C05-X3]] — For a leveraged book the danger is a gap drawdown too fast to de-risk, which vol-targeting cannot prevent and only an option or similar hard floor does; this is the case for tail protection.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C06-2|MT160-C06-2]] — Unleveraged long-horizon investors (e.g. endowments) face no margin-call risk, so tail protection only reduces their return; it makes sense only for leveraged investors.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C06-3|MT160-C06-3]] — A tail-risk fund that buys puts only selectively (when cheap) may leave the investor unprotected at the moment of need, so claims of free insurance are suspect.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C07-1|MT160-C07-1]] — Paying about 2% per year for tail protection can be reasonable for a leveraged book because protection allows higher leverage; at 30% cost it does not.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C07-4|MT160-C07-4]] — Adding trend following to a levered risk-parity portfolio lowers measured risk, tempting investors to lever up; in a crisis where trend also loses, all assets fall together with large leverage.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C07-X1|MT160-C07-X1]] — A 50% drawdown happens less than once per decade, so paying 2% a year for protection accumulates to a large drag over ten years and the benefit must be compared with that.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C08-X1|MT160-C08-X1]] — Match the tool to the investor: leveraged investors should buy tail protection (guaranteed payoff in a crash), unleveraged investors should buy trend following (positive expected return); trend must not be used to justify extra leverage.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C12-1|MT160-C12-1]] — A systematic trader should make no system adjustment for elections, referendums or payrolls, because trading political macro requires predicting the outcome, its implications and the market reaction better than everyone else.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C13-X1|MT160-C13-X1]] — If you believe you can forecast an election, express that on a betting site rather than by overriding the trading system, keeping the view separate from the systematic book.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C16-1|MT160-C16-1]] — AI used in trading models without supervision and transparency raises liability and explainability issues, unlike transparent trend rules where the output is known.

## [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo]]
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C02-1|MT161-C02-1]] — A trade can be right on the long-run theme but wipe out a levered fund if long and short legs are the same bet and it reverses short term.

## [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield]]
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C06-X3|MT162-C06-X3]] — Covid showed that overleveraged businesses face bankruptcy in a sudden shock unless the government steps in; business can cease overnight, so leverage must be sized for events nobody forecasts.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C08-2|MT162-C08-2]] — A macro forecaster is right only about half the time; profits come from cutting losers quickly, keeping winners and making sure losses on losers are smaller than gains on winners.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C09-1|MT162-C09-1]] — Caxton staff typically close nearly all positions before holidays to avoid unmonitored risk, although Law himself keeps positions and follows markets (markets trade from Sunday 6pm to Friday late evening).
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C09-X1|MT162-C09-X1]] — He manages 24/7 market risk with an escalation filter: the phone is on do-not-disturb with only named callers allowed through, so it rings only about ten times a year when something material happens.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C09-X2|MT162-C09-X2]] — He does not flatten positions for holidays because missing the payoff of a theme developed over six months costs more than the stress of staying in; flattening trades risk reduction against missing slow-developing theme payoffs.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C12-2|MT162-C12-2]] — Crises always occur and arrive when unexpected, so businesses and investors should build resilience, and post-2008/Covid investors are more skittish.

## [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357]]
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C07-X1|MT163-C07-X1]] — Long-volatility bets on the inflation/bond thesis should be at least a year out, because the thesis could take several years to play out and timing is uncertain.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C13-X1|MT163-C13-X1]] — Paul Tudor Jones's reported ideal portfolio on a volatility-adjusted basis is gold, Bitcoin and equities, while his highest conviction is the curve steepener; Niels says he does not agree.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C16-X1|MT163-C16-X1]] — Even if gold and Bitcoin suit a structural inflationary period, a concentrated vol-adjusted allocation risks 50 to 75% drawdowns in months, which is a poor risk-reward compared with non-correlated strategies.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C17-1|MT163-C17-1]] — Hold 30-50% of the portfolio in non-correlated strategies rather than just assets, as in 1960s-style investing.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C17-X2|MT163-C17-X2]] — Niels questions the PTJ portfolio: gold and Bitcoin are not natural hedges against high interest rates, though they could rise if the whole system blows up or on crypto support.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C23-1|MT163-C23-1]] — Knowing two plausible scenarios lets the trader position for both (options) rather than commit to one forecast.

## [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359]]
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C01-X2|MT164-C01-X2]] — A cashless economy has a single-point-of-failure weakness: a payment-system outage in Denmark (and Spain) stopped card payments, so digital convenience trades against resilience.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C08-1|MT164-C08-1]] — Do-nothing: a fixed allocation beat trying to react to tariffs; a 'do nothing plus' strategy adds trend-following, holding rising assets and reducing falling ones.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C09-2|MT164-C09-2]] — Trend followers pay for liquidity, so illiquidity and crowding raise transaction costs that can be a significant share of return in weak years; controlling costs is the real battle.

## [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360]]
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C13-1|MT165-C13-1]] — Trend is held for non-correlation, so during an equity bull run (+20%) a diversifier need not make money; the 1-5% allocation should not drive worry, though boardroom cycles make it hard to hold.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C15-3|MT165-C15-3]] — AQR's 'Diversifiers Forever' analyses UK foundations (45% public equity, 20% private equity) back to 1972, adding trend as liquid diversifier to maintain real portfolio value while paying out about 5%.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C16-2|MT165-C16-2]] — A smoother return stream raises the average payout for a spending portfolio (endowment, retirement drawdown) because drawdowns during payouts erode capital; 10% allocation or 10% portable alpha improved return and Sharpe.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C17-1|MT165-C17-1]] — From 2000 to June 2025 Barclay long/short equity had a correlation of about 0.66 to a 60/40 portfolio, global macro 0.34, while the SG Trend Index was -0.38 and made money in most crises.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C17-2|MT165-C17-2]] — Adding 20% trend gives the highest portfolio Sharpe improvement versus 20% of any Barclay hedge fund strategy; excluding the 1970s and 1980-2009 bull market the results still hold.

## [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361]]
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C03-X1|MT166-C03-X1]] — Extreme, previously 'unimaginable' market moves (cocoa) do occur, so an investor in a fast-changing world should plan for tail outcomes rather than forecasts, which favours non-predictive rules-based strategies.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C08-1|MT166-C08-1]] — Crisis risk offset archetype: shorter-term trend systems or option overlays plus convex exposures (bonds in risk-off, commodities in inflation spikes); judged on crisis payoff first, smoothness second.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C11-X8|MT166-C11-X8]] — For a core diversifier extra market breadth can dilute the intended low-correlation profile to the host portfolio, and a crisis-offset manager should be more selective, focusing on markets that respond in equity drawdowns, so breadth is not universally good.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C13-1|MT166-C13-1]] — Volatility targeting cuts positions when volatility spikes mid-trend, clipping the largest winners; equal small ATR-normalised bets at entry left alone preserve convexity.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C13-2|MT166-C13-2]] — Vol targeting fits mandates with a volatility budget and low drawdown tolerance; static small bets suit strategies designed to catch home runs.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C13-X9|MT166-C13-X9]] — With static, unadjusted bet sizes, wide diversification is what keeps each position small enough that an adverse volatility move in any one market cannot hurt much, so breadth substitutes for volatility targeting as the risk control.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C14-1|MT166-C14-1]] — Most European CTAs have moved from pure vol targeting to risk targeting: no active resizing for vol changes, but a cap on overall portfolio risk.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C14-2|MT166-C14-2]] — Static small bets can balloon: one fund's annualised vol rose from about 35 to 95 as positions grew, so 'small bets' is no protection against portfolio risk surges.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C14-3|MT166-C14-3]] — Vol targeting can add risk as well as cut it: it reduces size in volatile spikes then re-increases when markets quiet, so it is a path-dependent resizing, not only a clip on winners.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C15-X10|MT166-C15-X10]] — Replicators keep long and short rules mirrored (same breakout, stop, trailing and risk parameters) to avoid systematic directional bias and stay aligned with the benchmark.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C15-X11|MT166-C15-X11]] — Crisis-offset trend programs deliberately favour the long side in government bonds and safe-haven currencies, with slower, looser exits on those longs and tighter stops on shorts in risk assets, to maximise convexity in equity drawdowns.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C16-3|MT166-C16-3]] — Buying VIX is an unreliable equity hedge because VIX decomposition varies; analogously short-term trend funds sold as risk mitigation were doubted.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C18-2|MT166-C18-2]] — Standard metrics (MAR, Sharpe, CAGR) hide survival risk from small universes; the lopsided distribution means most small portfolios underwhelm while a few are lucky.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C18-X15|MT166-C18-X15]] — The unlucky quarter of random 30-market subsets (25th percentile) would show around three years of red ink and be survival-threatening for a real manager, not because the process is broken but because too few markets left the fat-tail edge unharvested; only a few lucky subsets were extraordinary.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C20-1|MT166-C20-1]] — Scaling rule: when capital grows, first add new liquid markets, second increase position size, third add systems.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C20-2|MT166-C20-2]] — Diversification across trend systems saturates at about 10; ensembles deliberately decorrelate related markets such as Brent and crude.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C20-X16|MT166-C20-X16]] — Market breadth is capped by liquidity and counterparty or regulatory risk: Rich will not add off-exchange or less-regulated markets even to extend diversification.

## [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392]]
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C03-2|MT167-C03-2]] — Gas and oil have near-zero statistical correlation; oil complex is homogeneous, gas is the most volatile with a right-skewed tail, so clustering and vol scaling in portfolios matter.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C03-3|MT167-C03-3]] — Commodity index weights by production and liquidity ignore volatility, so risk exposure to volatile markets exceeds the notional weight.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C04-1|MT167-C04-1]] — Trend following with volatility scaling captured the oil move yet automatically reduced risk as volatility spiked.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C04-2|MT167-C04-2]] — Multi-strategy diversification in commodity systematics (trend, skew, value) paid off year to date as skew and value benefited from the petroleum rally.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C06-2|MT167-C06-2]] — Typical QIS trend books hold 10-40 commodities out of 20-60 markets, with commodities around a quarter of risk.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C07-1|MT167-C07-1]] — Philosophical divergence: treating energy, metals and ags as separate asset classes (seven in total) rather than one commodity class gives commodities a higher risk budget because intra-commodity correlation is lower than within equities.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C09-2|MT167-C09-2]] — Zero-DTE options could be used as cheap intraday protection for long-term trend positions without cutting the underlying exposure.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C11-3|MT167-C11-3]] — In a crisis, correlations go to extremes and principal components drive returns, so a core liquid universe maximises crisis alpha.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C12-3|MT167-C12-3]] — Cash-efficient trend portfolios (low margin per unit of risk) resemble the max-crisis-alpha core liquid universe, so objectives converge.

## [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393]]
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C04-1|MT168-C04-1]] — With oil trading a $20-25 daily range (Brent near 120 then 90), execution time of day materially changes P&L and makes tracking one's own model difficult; scenario analysis back to 1998 ranks March 2026 surprise volatility at the top.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C08-1|MT168-C08-1]] — In a crisis CTAs scale all positions (longs and shorts) down per market, which is costly when EM spreads widen to about 10 bp; discretionary managers instead close the liquid duration exposure.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C08-2|MT168-C08-2]] — When correlations spike assets become substitutes, so selling Treasury futures hedges the common first factor as effectively as shrinking the whole book.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C09-2|MT168-C09-2]] — Closing a long-short book in the stress cost about 5% of performance in alternative markets, so crisis de-risking is expensive.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C09-3|MT168-C09-3]] — Factor-based risk management: in crises the first (rates) factor dominates volatility, so manage factor risk directly while limiting specific risk per market.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C10-X4|MT168-C10-X4]] — Hedging the common first factor while limiting specific risk per market is a constrained optimisation problem that is probably not worth the effort for a liquid-market CTA, which can simply scale the whole book down.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C15-2|MT168-C15-2]] — Capacity limits risk per market so footprint stays unwindable; larger AUM pushes risk into the biggest markets.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C16-1|MT168-C16-1]] — CTA return is diversification times average market Sharpe; if markets are highly correlated, trading only big markets costs little, but if you select low-correlation markets, capacity pushes risk into big markets and cuts effective breadth.

## [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9]]
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C02-2|MT169-C02-2]] — Objective differs from managed futures: reduce equity risk and drawdown while keeping equity-like returns rather than minimise correlation to equities.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C04-3|MT169-C04-3]] — Systematic downside risk management gives trend following a lower-beta, lower-volatility portfolio that still outperforms the index on long-term compounding.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C05-2|MT169-C05-2]] — Correlation of the strategy to the S&P 500 is about 0.55, low versus long-only but not versus managed futures or gold.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C06-X2|MT169-C06-X2]] — Much of the value of trend following on equity indexes comes from cutting the left tail of catastrophic market declines; the incremental edge from single stocks lies in the small and mid-cap area that index futures portfolios under-represent.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C07-1|MT169-C07-1]] — Risk-weighted position sizing is capped at 100% funded; small/mid-cap volatility is high enough that the risk target is reached without leverage, and leveraging low-vol names is deemed a recipe for disaster.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C07-X3|MT169-C07-X3]] — With hundreds of risk-weighted stock positions whose volatility keeps moving, the portfolio is rebalanced continuously to hold the target risk level, with a hard cap preventing leverage above 100% funded.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C07-X4|MT169-C07-X4]] — Dividends and corporate-action cash flows are not reinvested stock by stock; they are added to the portfolio's cash and base trading level and spread proportionately across all positions, which simplifies operations in a 300-600 stock book.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C08-X5|MT169-C08-X5]] — Longboard declined to lend out its shares for extra income because the small yield did not compensate for counterparty and rehypothecation risk and added complexity; the stated principle is not to take risks you are not paid for proportionally.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C09-1|MT169-C09-1]] — Holding 300-600 names (about 20% of the tradable set) avoids missing the 4% winners; concentrated top-50 portfolios have wide lucky/unlucky dispersion, so breadth minimises dispersion and maximises geometric compounding.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C09-X7|MT169-C09-X7]] — Dilution argument: in a portfolio of hundreds of markets or stocks a single outlier winner has a smaller impact on returns than in a 40-50 name portfolio, so concentration can give more positive skew and 'bang for the buck' (Moritz's question, which Cole answers with luck-dispersion arguments).

## [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418]]
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C03-2|MT170-C03-2]] — Trend following should be sized to investor goals and ability to stay with it since the next regime cannot be chosen; it is a disciplined way to live with uncertainty.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C05-1|MT170-C05-1]] — Taking partial profits on winners converts open profit to redeployable equity and smooths drawdowns, but cuts exposure to rare exceptional trades and gives up positive skew, moving away from pure outlier hunting.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C08-1|MT170-C08-1]] — Response not prediction: participate broadly, keep initial exposure small, accept failed attempts, and use trailing exits (no fixed profit targets) because later stages of the path depend on decisions not yet made.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C12-1|MT170-C12-1]] — A self-feeding move need not end because it travelled far, yet need not continue either; hence many small positions, an initial stop, and a trailing exit rather than a prediction of how far a trend will go.

## [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397]]
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C11-2|MT171-C11-2]] — Be tactically active short-term while buying cheap long-dated convex protection, because longer-dated risk is inexpensive relative to underlying risk.

## [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep]]
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C02-X2|MT172-C02-X2]] — For a middle-of-the-road chart with no edge (Amazon rated 2 out of 3), express the view with options: a straddle, or puts if sceptical of the business and calls if it may rebound from the middle of its range, so risk is limited to the premium.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C04-3|MT172-C04-3]] — Leveraged ETFs reset daily, so multi-day returns slip from the stated multiple and fees accumulate; they are trading vehicles, not buy-and-hold.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C05-2|MT172-C05-2]] — Hedges for tech concentration should come from lagging sectors: healthcare, discretionary and financials on rolling six-month relative performance.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C07-X1|MT172-C07-X1]] — Tech unwinds tend to come in spurts that then rebound, so the aim given a concentrated index is to be prepared (know the exposure) rather than to predict a crash; Eisman states he is not predicting one either.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C12-1|MT172-C12-1]] — REITs diversified during the 2000 tech-bubble unwind and rose, so they are a potential diversifier if tech concentration unwinds.

## [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302]]
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C01-2|MT173-C01-2]] — With VIX near historical lows and rate vol moderating, options are cheap so adding hedges is attractive even though the environment is not anti-CTA.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C02-X2|MT173-C02-X2]] — Volatility targeting limits whipsaw losses: as cocoa volatility rose, trend programs had already cut its exposure, so after another big move it was only about the fifth-worst commodity contributor, costing roughly 20-30bp in a 15%-vol program.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C03-2|MT173-C03-2]] — Adding markets to a trend program has diminishing diversification benefit (about square-root-of-N) while newer, niche markets cost more to trade.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C04-1|MT173-C04-1]] — Backtests of 40, 50, 100 or 150 markets look similar if vol target is held constant, as removing markets means grossing up the rest; a sensible universe is about 50-100 markets, with 10 markets more cautious.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C05-2|MT173-C05-2]] — Capping equity exposure on the upside decorrelates from the beta portfolio and may preserve capital in downturns, though not necessarily maximising returns (a gut feeling).
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C06-1|MT173-C06-1]] — Liability-driven investors need a nominal return target so their volatility is implied by the required Sharpe ratio; target-date funds cut equity as retirement nears; mandates run at 5-15% vol.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C06-2|MT173-C06-2]] — A 5% vol trend allocation alongside a 60/40 does little; leverage can scale it but then financing and leverage constraints dominate the choice.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C06-X1|MT173-C06-X1]] — There is no universal safe volatility level: on the Markowitz frontier the right risk is set by the investor's risk aversion and, for asset owners, by the nominal return target they must meet, so Baltas declines to give a risk-of-ruin threshold.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C07-1|MT173-C07-1]] — A 10-15% vol is suitable for trend in isolation, and about 20% alongside a strategic allocation matches equity vol; a lot of trend programs run higher than 15%.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C07-2|MT173-C07-2]] — A 2x leveraged equity ETF would have been wiped out in the 1929-32 crash, so leverage beyond matching equity vol is too aggressive.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C07-X1|MT173-C07-X1]] — Rule of thumb that expected drawdown is about twice the volatility (a 10%-vol program should expect around a 20% drawdown); Baltas says trend returns are not IID and are positively skewed over the medium term so the rule is only approximate, and he checks whether historical drawdowns stay below roughly twice vol while disliking drawdown as a measure.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C09-X3|MT173-C09-X3]] — The short-only equity variant is motivated by not wanting to be long equities when a vol shock hits (the end of 2017 into the February 2018 VIX event), because the program would be long into the reversal before it can turn short.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C10-X1|MT173-C10-X1]] — The cost of equity constraints shows up in bull regimes: in the best quarters the constrained variants give up roughly 100-140bp (the numbers are garbled in the captions), which is the mirror of the small downside benefit and part of why the long-run return falls.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C11-X3|MT173-C11-X3]] — A way to cap equity beta properly: estimate each market's beta to equities, note that portfolio beta is the position-weighted sum, and optimise the portfolio so that sum is capped at zero or another level; an AHL working paper (about 2016-17) did this, though the published version dropped the analysis.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C14-1|MT173-C14-1]] — Allocating to CTAs systematically manages net equity exposure, giving crisis beta from timing and crisis alpha from multi-asset diversification.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C17-1|MT173-C17-1]] — Optimised carry portfolios constrained to zero beta to equities and Treasuries combine carry and trend with Sharpe near one, although rolling correlations to equities and bonds vary over time.

## [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410]]
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C06-2|MT174-C06-2]] — A trend follower delivers long-term returns and crisis alpha; if crisis alpha is the aim, use a small core universe because principal components (equity, duration) dominate in downturns, while for long-term performance add more diversifying markets.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C07-2|MT174-C07-2]] — A practical universe is 50-100 markets; going broader costs capacity or adds nothing because adding same-factor markets splits a fixed risk budget.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C08-1|MT174-C08-1]] — A defensive portfolio has three layers: option-based hedges as first responders (reactive, costly), trend following as second responder for multi-month crises, and a diversifying third bucket (e.g. alternative risk premia) that moderates carry cost.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C08-2|MT174-C08-2]] — The diversifying third bucket cannot be short gamma, because financing a hedge with downside risk turns the overlay into a relative-value trade rather than a defence.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C08-X1|MT174-C08-X1]] — Specification for the third defensive bucket: flat-to-negative correlation to equities and a payoff that is neither convex nor concave (for example alternative risk premia), so it can offset the bleed of the first two layers without adding downside risk.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C09-2|MT174-C09-2]] — In portable alpha, trend on top of equities should limit its long-equity participation to avoid doubling risk on the upside.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C09-X2|MT174-C09-X2]] — In portable alpha, the notional leverage from a trend overlay is less risky than it looks, because the added exposure has historically been negatively correlated to equities in longer equity crises, so it works as a hedge rather than extra risk.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C10-X1|MT174-C10-X1]] — Leverage itself is not the problem with stacking trend on a portfolio; the danger is added leverage on the asset the portfolio is already concentrated in, so for an equity-only portfolio moderate the long-equity risk of the overlay, while for equity-bond portfolios keep trend unconstrained.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C14-X1|MT174-C14-X1]] — Theme-driven clusters are transient, so a covariance matrix that captures them has to be updated dynamically to reflect how risk is distributed tomorrow, not just historical correlations.

## [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21]]
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C02-1|MT175-C02-1]] — Beyond a few hundred markets, adding another changes nothing because allocation to it is tiny and the effective degrees of freedom matter more than the count (2-, 5- and 10-year Treasuries are essentially one market).
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C02-X1|MT175-C02-X1]] — Judge diversification by fundamental independence (different regions, consumers, producers, logistics) rather than by correlations measured over short windows: South African sunflower seeds, US East Coast power and Chinese bitumen are different markets whatever a one- or two-month correlation says.
