- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C03-1|MT175-C03-1]] — Alternative commodity diversification requires capacity discipline: with billions, allocation concentrates in crude, gold and gas so niche markets matter little. Gresham sees capacity around 1-2bn and soft-closed at about 1bn (half closed during COVID's high commodity volatility).
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C03-2|MT175-C03-2]] — Trend investors need a horizon of several years, so leave headroom so existing clients can benefit from organic growth instead of closing after a 15-20% year.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C03-X1|MT175-C03-X1]] — Capacity in commodity trend is measured in units of dollar risk, so it expands when prices and volatility are high (during COVID paper capacity was 4-5bn); Gresham did not assume that level would persist, so it half-closed at about 1bn and sets top-line capacity around a couple of billion through the cycle.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C04-X1|MT175-C04-X1]] — Group markets into clusters by shared physical drivers (a 'furnace' cluster of iron ore, flat glass and coking coal), so correlation and risk are managed through real supply-chain linkages rather than only statistical estimates.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C06-3|MT175-C06-3]] — In markets with a price floor (California carbon auction floor) the short trend is capped, so the model must be overridden to prevent adding to shorts near the floor.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C06-X1|MT175-C06-X1]] — Long and short positions are treated symmetrically in futures, with real-world boundary exceptions: Gresham avoids the front of the oil curve (prices went negative in COVID) and overrides shorts near price floors.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C07-1|MT175-C07-1]] — Position limits are set so the largest position can be exited in about a week in stress; liquidity plus diversification determine top-line capacity, and new markets start with small weights until real fills confirm costs.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C08-1|MT175-C08-1]] — Chinese commodity futures are accessed through swaps with margin held by a third party to limit counterparty risk; all other markets are exchange-cleared futures.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C08-2|MT175-C08-2]] — Large CTAs lose roughly 0.7-1% a year to slippage, and splitting orders across the day benchmarks well against TWAP/VWAP but hides the autocorrelated cost.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C08-3|MT175-C08-3]] — At 15% annual risk (about 1% daily variance), trades account for only about 5% of daily variance and are uncorrelated across markets, so execution risk is a small fraction of portfolio risk.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C09-1|MT175-C09-1]] — Gresham gives the trading desk a risk budget (tracking error to model) and lets it work orders passively over several days with bounds, so the desk acts as an anti-correlated buffer and trend signals can be faster for the same cost.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C09-2|MT175-C09-2]] — Faster signals look better before costs, but patient passive execution (avoiding crossing the spread, which in freight can be 100bp) preserves them after costs.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C09-3|MT175-C09-3]] — Passive execution cut slippage close to zero over the first year, partly via invisible savings when the model changes its target before the order is done.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C10-1|MT175-C10-1]] — Passive waiting behaves as an anti-trend (mean-reverting) component, adding diversification so the strategy risk scale can be raised.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C10-2|MT175-C10-2]] — If the market moves away, the unfilled trade accumulates tracking variance until the limit forces a trade, so slippage variance rises while the average cost falls; market near 50/50 each day gives savings about half the time.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C10-3|MT175-C10-3]] — In April 2025 big propane moves, the passive method of cutting positions saved slippage versus an aggressive exit that would have hit a move-away-and-back.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C12-2|MT175-C12-2]] — Commodity trend protects a 60/40 portfolio in rising, high and low inflation periods, so adding even basic commodity trend improves results across inflation regimes (the 'Eras Tour' paper).

## [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313]]
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C10-1|MT176-C10-1]] — Fixed income was a muted risk contributor for many months and has returned to being the largest exposure in trend portfolios, so risk allocation shifts with volatility and signal strength.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C12-3|MT176-C12-3]] — In a deleveraging or degrossing event bonds rally (TLT about +10% since July), supporting the diversifier role, but inflation-driven episodes like 2022 can make bonds and trend followers fall together.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C13-1|MT176-C13-1]] — Trend following has roughly zero correlation to equities, not negative, so periods when both lose are normal and do not make it an unreliable diversifier.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C14-2|MT176-C14-2]] — Volatility drags compound returns at portfolio level, but a high-volatility component can be beneficial if it is uncorrelated with the rest of the portfolio.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C14-3|MT176-C14-3]] — An uncorrelated high-volatility bet can add value with periodic total losses if the portfolio is rebalanced to the other assets and the bet is repeated.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C15-1|MT176-C15-1]] — Using a 25% volatility alternative instead of a 10% one lets investors allocate a smaller weight and add bonds, giving a more diversified portfolio with a better Sharpe ratio and return per unit of risk.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C15-2|MT176-C15-2]] — High-volatility futures funds give cheap, available leverage priced near the overnight rate rather than at a bank margin spread of 2-4%, so they are more capital efficient than borrowing to lever a 60/40 portfolio.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C17-2|MT176-C17-2]] — UCITS VaR limits cap leverage, so UCITS CTA products run around 15-16% volatility versus 20%+ in non-regulated versions.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C18-1|MT176-C18-1]] — Combining traditional assets with a non-correlated return stream like trend following gives a safer, higher-returning portfolio; the speaker says no white paper contradicts this.

## [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139]]
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C01-2|MT177-C01-2]] — Winton runs a diversified CTA that is 75% trend following and 25% other systematic macro strategies, alongside pure trend and quant multi-strategy products.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C05-2|MT177-C05-2]] — Adding non-trend signals smooths drawdowns but reduces crisis effectiveness, so type and weight of added signals matter; Winton chooses 75% trend as the level that keeps the trend profile with some drift.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C07-3|MT177-C07-3]] — The trend/non-trend mix depends on the opportunity set but is fixed so investors know what they receive.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C11-2|MT177-C11-2]] — Parameters are mostly universal across markets but adjusted for transaction cost, market impact, spreads and commissions.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C14-1|MT177-C14-1]] — Added markets should be truly diversifying (Chinese futures, regional gas, power, freight, marine fuel) rather than equity sector ETFs or developed-market swaps correlated with existing exposure.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C14-2|MT177-C14-2]] — Many OTC-traded alternative markets can be cleared on exchange, reducing counterparty risk close to major futures levels.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C15-2|MT177-C15-2]] — Trend allocation size and form (pure, capped beta) depend on the investor's drawdown tolerance and process, so there is no fixed rule; customised programs often vary alternative-market or China exposure rather than trend share.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C16-1|MT177-C16-1]] — Risk is not volatility: in 2017 the VIX was at record lows while valuations were rich, and estimating loss risk is harder than estimating volatility, so use many risk measures besides volatility.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C16-2|MT177-C16-2]] — Risk management by replaying current positions through all historical environments (worst day or week) and asking whether the loss is acceptable, e.g. the 1990 Gulf War oil jump of 30%.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C17-2|MT177-C17-2]] — Leverage varies naturally with signal strength and correlations, and risk measures decide whether to hold it inside a band.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C17-3|MT177-C17-3]] — Stylised stress test of correlation flips: before the 2013 taper tantrum trend was long bonds and long stocks with negative correlation, and a positive correlation scenario looked unacceptable, so risk was cut.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C18-1|MT177-C18-1]] — Winton ran lower volatility ahead of the 2013 taper tantrum because of the correlation scenario, which helped that year.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C18-2|MT177-C18-2]] — Capping equity long exposure in a trend program reduces long-term performance in proportion to the cap, helps crisis profile, and is offered as a bespoke option; neither is objectively better.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C18-3|MT177-C18-3]] — Using multiple trend managers cuts single-manager risk but loses fee netting; the investor chooses the trade-off.

## [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403]]
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C07-1|MT178-C07-1]] — Makita's risk mitigation framework treats growth as the main driver of institutional risk and uses first responders (tail risk, long volatility) for sudden 10-15% drops, second responders (trend) for quarters-to-years dislocations, and diversifiers for uncorrelated return.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C07-X1|MT178-C07-X1]] — CTAs are second responders, not designed to profit from sharp weekly sell-offs, so investors who judge them on such episodes are disappointed unnecessarily; tail-risk and long-volatility first responders are the right tool for sudden 10-15% drops.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C08-2|MT178-C08-2]] — Return dispersion across CTAs is large (about 80% in 2022; one manager lost over 30% while the asset class gained 20%), so a diversified multi-manager approach with equal risk allocation and volatility targeting gives more consistent crisis alpha.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C08-3|MT178-C08-3]] — Managed accounts let investors scale a high-Sharpe multi-manager CTA portfolio to a desired volatility, unlike funds of hedge funds that cannot scale returns up.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C10-3|MT178-C10-3]] — Volatility targeting at portfolio level diversifies bets across time, further improving performance beyond equal risk allocation across managers.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C10-R1|MT178-C10-R1]] — Molyboga believes diversification is the only free lunch in finance and the best principle to use.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C16-X1|MT178-C16-X1]] — Allocators worried about crises, inflation or geopolitical risk should hold diversified trend with meaningful commodity trend exposure, because commodity trend is the sleeve that helps most in geopolitical environments.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C17-2|MT178-C17-2]] — The benefit of short-term trend is very sensitive to transaction costs and market impact and disappears without investment in execution infrastructure (colocation, specialised algos).
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C17-X1|MT178-C17-X1]] — A practical target for diversifying manager risk is six to ten trend managers instead of the usual one or two, which captures crisis alpha more consistently.

## [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19]]
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C03-2|MT179-C03-2]] — Proper diversification across many futures markets requires more capital than an individual typically has, which motivated starting a fund.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C10-1|MT179-C10-1]] — A trading system has three separable parts (timing, money management, portfolio selection); entries came from opposite-hedger timing, exits from money-management rules, and the system was not always in the market.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C10-2|MT179-C10-2]] — Positions were sized by volatility so that a trailing stop is not triggered by random price noise (ATR-like), giving the market enough room but not too much.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C12-2|MT179-C12-2]] — There was no maximum portfolio heat rule; large open equity did not trigger cutting positions, and the computer helps follow trades during long drawdowns though psychological pain remains.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C13-1|MT179-C13-1]] — Portfolio selection matters more than timing or money management; the same system on cocoa versus crude oil gives very different outcomes, so portfolio construction deserves the most attention.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C14-X2|MT179-C14-X2]] — A portfolio should include a feedback mechanism that checks whether it still delivers its stated goal (such as smoother equity growth), not only a static basket of markets.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C15-1|MT179-C15-1]] — If the portfolio goal is smooth equity growth, choose orthogonal markets; 25 interest-rate futures give almost no smoothing, so weights by sector and sub-sector should reflect correlations.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C15-2|MT179-C15-2]] — Weights are set top-down by sectors and sub-sectors using orthogonality and slowly updated if correlations change.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C19-1|MT179-C19-1]] — Capping fund size keeps access to small markets; individual trades lose 70% of the time, but adding canola to the 10-year note helps the portfolio much more than adding the five-year note.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C21-X1|MT179-C21-X1]] — Blowing up is the number one reason a long-lived commodity trading firm disappears, followed by ageing or death of the principals; Tactical closed instead because two key employees retired in 2023.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C22-1|MT179-C22-1]] — Liquid futures strategies can return capital within about 30 days, so key-person risk is less costly than for illiquid strategies; Tactical closed in 2023 when two key employees retired.

## [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298]]
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C02-2|MT180-C02-2]] — Short fixed income worked as a hedge against the April equity drawdown within the trend portfolio even though it was noisy.
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C04-3|MT180-C04-3]] — Managed futures should be a strategic allocation rebalanced to target rather than timed; if adding at all, add during drawdowns since managed futures tend to recover on average.
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C08-1|MT180-C08-1]] — The goal is to move investors from bad to okay: even a modest managed futures allocation via ETF improves diversification versus none; ETFs use fewer, liquid markets (copper, bonds, maybe no cocoa).

## [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351]]
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C02-X1|MT181-C02-X1]] — Deep drawdowns are an inherent feature of trend-following models (including for the longest-established managers), so they are expected to recur and the process is simply to wait for the next high-water mark.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C03-1|MT181-C03-1]] — Large trend managers cut target volatility from about 25% to 10-12% in the early 2000s to attract institutional assets, using correlation reduction and vol targeting, which smoothed returns at the cost of the two-way volatility of the classic style.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C05-2|MT181-C05-2]] — Very large managers must tilt risk to liquid financial futures rather than commodities, losing diversification, which may explain why big established firms had their worst drawdowns.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C05-3|MT181-C05-3]] — Diversify with a system ensemble: about 10 structurally uncorrelated trend systems across 60-70 markets (500-600 return streams) rather than just adding correlated markets, because market correlations change quickly with politics.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C05-4|MT181-C05-4]] — Trading 70-80 less correlated markets may be a better bet than hundreds of eventually highly correlated markets.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C05-X1|MT181-C05-X1]] — Combining several structurally different types of trend following is an underappreciated form of diversification, because most managers run only one or two types and rely on multiple time frames instead.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C06-1|MT181-C06-1]] — Repeated reversals forced much more dynamic risk budgeting in spring 2025, with big swings in total CTA risk rather than the usual gradual change.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C08-1|MT181-C08-1]] — Fixed per-contract commissions hurt small contracts: $5 each way on a nano costs far more relative to notional than on a standard contract at the same number of round turns; percentage fees scaled to lot size fix this.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C12-2|MT181-C12-2]] — Roughly $200,000 is the minimum capital for a diversified retail trend program using micro/mini/nano futures, with enough skin in the game for discipline.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C12-3|MT181-C12-3]] — Trap one: with small capital, over-diversifying across 100 markets makes it impossible to size equal risk per bet, leaving hidden correlation and risk of ruin.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C13-1|MT181-C13-1]] — At $200k run about 10 uncorrelated markets with 5 ensemble systems and ATR-based equal-risk sizing, then widen to about 30 markets at $400k and 60 markets at $1 million, adding standard contracts.

## [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398]]
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C15-1|MT182-C15-1]] — Multi-strategy platforms sell bundling hundreds of orthogonal pods with faster risk management, the same bundle-versus-component choice investors face.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C15-X1|MT182-C15-X1]] — Bundled products (multi-strat, structured packages) can be good or bad, and the investor often cannot tell which until afterwards (the bundled bad loans of 2008 being the cautionary example), so bundling needs ex-ante scrutiny.

## [[MT183 - The Volatility Apocalypse - Systematic Investor 303]]
- [[MT183 - The Volatility Apocalypse - Systematic Investor 303#^MT183-C05-X1|MT183-C05-X1]] — Structured-product vol supply is unlevered and spread across many institutions, unlike concentrated vehicles such as XIV, so when the regime breaks it will not be the cause, although unwinds can still cause pain as with Chinese snowball contracts.
- [[MT183 - The Volatility Apocalypse - Systematic Investor 303#^MT183-C17-1|MT183-C17-1]] — Hold long-dated gold calls and trade around them: sell short-dated strangles after big moves to fund decay, and buy calendar call spreads; gold's bull trend should not be hit by an equity wobble and would gain from higher equity vol.

## [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)]]
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C05-1|MT184-C05-1]] — A tactical nuclear weapon would not reverse Russia's strategic situation because Ukraine out-mobilizes it, and Russian conscription is not going well.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C12-2|MT184-C12-2]] — Cyber deterrence resembles mutual assured destruction only in theory: cyber capabilities are less predictable than nuclear weapons and untested until used.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C13-1|MT184-C13-1]] — Russia's large cyberattack on Ukraine largely failed because Ukraine improved cyber resilience with Western and Microsoft help; defense in cyber is hard because attackers need to succeed once, so firms need zero-trust, compartmentalized security and real-time threat sharing.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C15-2|MT184-C15-2]] — Private-equity investors stress-tested portfolio exposure to Russia and Ukraine after 2014 and 2020 and put business-continuity plans in place, which paid off when the invasion came.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C15-X1|MT184-C15-X1]] — Geopolitical exposure runs through indirect channels (customers and remote IT workers) even without direct investment, so exposure mapping must go beyond holdings to revenue and workforce dependence.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C15-X2|MT184-C15-X2]] — Business-continuity and contingency spending looks like a cost to cut until a shock hits; the Ukraine invasion showed it was insurance with high value, so such spending should be protected.

## [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -]]
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C02-X1|MT185-C02-X1]] — Very short-term trading is only viable when transaction costs are near zero: the speaker moved from trend following to shorter-term trading because his partner's account paid no commissions, and the firm's short-term time frame harks back to zero-commission days.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C05-X1|MT185-C05-X1]] — Uncertainty has grown, especially in the last few years, so outlier moves catch portfolios more easily and do more damage, although the basic concepts remain valid and there is room for new ones.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C09-5|MT185-C09-5]] — Execution is a main source of edge: execution quality has improved by multiples in 20-25 years and slippage is at the best level in the firm's history.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C11-1|MT185-C11-1]] — Capacity is assessed by net dollars per contract against execution cost; if the margin is thin there is a problem, and this matters far more for very short-term than 5-10 day holds.

## [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382]]
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C05-1|MT186-C05-1]] — Since the location of the next outlier trend is unknowable, wide diversification ensures the portfolio is positioned to catch it and pay for losses elsewhere.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C05-4|MT186-C05-4]] — In an unpredictable world a non-predictive strategy such as trend following is a sensible portfolio component.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C05-X1|MT186-C05-X1]] — An event shock (the April 2025 Liberation Day tariff announcement) produced the largest historical drawdown for an outlier-hunting trend programme, and the recovery came from a persistent trend in one sector (metals), which is why broad sector diversification is needed to recoup shock losses.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C07-X1|MT186-C07-X1]] — Diversification should be applied across model types as well as across lookback time frames and markets, because no single lookback or model is reliably best year to year.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C08-1|MT186-C08-1]] — Trading to a historical average risk level is suboptimal; one should vary exposure with the conditions in front of you, like braking into corners and accelerating on straights.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C08-2|MT186-C08-2]] — Sizing positions on backtest-average volatility leaves the portfolio exposed when volatility spikes beyond what the backtest contained.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C08-5|MT186-C08-5]] — Stop-losses, position limits and drawdown rules should be defined in advance and engage without debate, rather than being decided when losses occur.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C10-1|MT186-C10-1]] — Permanently conservative sizing is an always-on brake that removes both downside and upside; variable braking (full exposure when structure supports it) is better.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C11-3|MT186-C11-3]] — Tightly fitted models embed stationarity assumptions and become fragile when conditions shift.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C13-2|MT186-C13-2]] — Accumulated calibration assumptions (moving-average length, vol target, patterns) make a system work only in the historical band and fail abruptly with hidden negative skew.
- [[MT186 - Trading the Fragmented World of 2026 - Systematic Investor - Ep.382#^MT186-C14-2|MT186-C14-2]] — Smoothing the equity curve by optimisation or sizing reduces the positive asymmetry that makes trend following profitable; costs are deferred and statistical.

## [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414]]
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C01-1|MT187-C01-1]] — A fund losing 67% in one month is a reminder that leverage can wipe out capital; such episodes are 'rumblings' of fragile markets.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C02-2|MT187-C02-2]] — Trend followers or shorts in single stocks face gap risk of order 177% in a day (Moderna), much more than normal futures, so stock trading needs explicit tail-risk limits.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C13-1|MT187-C13-1]] — When building a portfolio of managers, diversify by structural differences (markets traded, speed) rather than by style labels, because the future path is unknown.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C13-X1|MT187-C13-X1]] — Trend managers tend to stay with the style they started with, but programmes that evolve to blend several methodologies (provided each model is good and remains pure trend) can be stronger than a single style, which is a form of diversification inside one programme.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C14-1|MT187-C14-1]] — Run American, European and time-series momentum models as internal pods, aggregate signals before execution to cut transaction costs while tracking each pod's performance.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C14-2|MT187-C14-2]] — Blending styles in one fund nets performance fees across models, unlike paying separate fees to several managers.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C14-X1|MT187-C14-X1]] — For long-term trend following in exchange-traded futures, transaction costs are low and not the main hurdle, so the cost saving from netting pod signals before execution is modest and the fee-netting benefit matters more.
- [[MT187 - Treasury Markets not as “Safe” as You Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.414#^MT187-C16-3|MT187-C16-3]] — Niederhoffer blew up in the late 1990s (and again later) despite empirical rigour, showing tail and leverage risk; he recovered by improving the process.

## [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376]]
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C04-1|MT188-C04-1]] — A 10-market replication portfolio is more concentrated than diversified CTAs, so it carries idiosyncratic risk (more short yen helped; more short euro hurt in 2025).
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C05-1|MT188-C05-1]] — After Liberation Day secondary and esoteric markets were more volatile than major ones, so portfolios with many exotic markets were whipsawed more.
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C09-2|MT188-C09-2]] — Trading 10 liquid futures contracts less often cuts implementation costs, giving about 350bp annual outperformance over a decade versus funds trading hundreds of contracts.
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C09-3|MT188-C09-3]] — Implementation costs of a CTA strategy (~300bp) are real costs even if not paid to the manager, and should be compared apples to apples.
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C10-1|MT188-C10-1]] — CTAs with short-term models, vol controls and many instruments can turn over notional about 20 times a year, so implementation cost per added instrument (70 to 190 markets) matters.
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C17-1|MT188-C17-1]] — Alpha/beta separation: hold a cheap core replication/beta allocation and then take more single-manager or commodity risk (e.g. AQR, which had a train wreck in prior six years then a strong 5).
- [[MT188 - Trend ETFs, CTA Replication, and the Fall of 60-40 - Systematic Investor - Ep.376#^MT188-C17-X1|MT188-C17-X1]] — Because allocators cannot yet get the core trend signal efficiently, they bolt together several managers to average out differences, ending up with an index-like blend of 'beta adjacent' strategies that is inefficient compared with owning the core directly and adding satellites.

## [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C04-1|MT189-C04-1]] — CTAs actively allocate risk across markets (not static allocation), which made 2026 returns resilient and systematic trading more palatable.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C05-1|MT189-C05-1]] — Being long crude oil offset the war shock to long equities and long gold; stability has been the new up in 2026.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C05-2|MT189-C05-2]] — Mutual fund CTA index correlated highly with main index but underperformed by about 200bp a year due to structuring costs and untradeable markets.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C06-1|MT189-C06-1]] — Mutual fund CTAs underperformed hedge-fund CTAs by 400-500bp in 2022 due to 40 Act vol controls, but the gap has largely disappeared as clean low-fee funds replaced old products.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C08-2|MT189-C08-2]] — Long-only commodities had an 80% max drawdown over 25 years versus below 20% for CTAs, which is why commodities get a small allocation.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C08-3|MT189-C08-3]] — CTA losses are small compared with levered discretionary funds (Situational Awareness, a roughly $45bn AI fund at 4:1 leverage that fell 67% in July).
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C08-4|MT189-C08-4]] — Collateralised borrowing forces unwinds in drawdowns, whereas liquid futures with notional leverage do not; no CTA has suffered a Situational-Awareness-type loss.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C09-1|MT189-C09-1]] — A 5x leveraged version of a good CTA lost about 90% when a normal 20% drawdown occurred, showing leverage destroys capital.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C09-X1|MT189-C09-X1]] — Notional leverage in listed futures is built into margin and is not borrowed money, so equity-style leverage fears are mistranslated; CTAs size risk to the investor's chosen level, control it tightly and can exit at will, unlike concentrated, borrowed leverage.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C10-2|MT189-C10-2]] — Overengineering capital efficiency (portable alpha, more leverage) does not make strategies better and may be a risk.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C11-2|MT189-C11-2]] — Managed accounts and portable alpha let investors overlay CTA exposure on top of 100% equities using futures margin, without selling equities.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C11-X3|MT189-C11-X3]] — Disagreeing with the over-engineering concern, Roble argues that the logic of trend following is to capture trends in as many places as possible, so adding markets (efficiently, using futures leverage) is consistent with the philosophy rather than needless complexity.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C20-1|MT189-C20-1]] — Allocate to two to four structurally different trend managers rather than one.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C21-1|MT189-C21-1]] — Short-term models sold after 2022 as protection at inflection points act like vol controls or stop-losses, reducing drawdowns at a true economic cost of lost returns.
- [[MT189 - Trend Following Is Becoming Something Bigger Than Crisis Alpha - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT189-C21-R1|MT189-C21-R1]] — Beer voices the investor's reconsideration: for an all-weather solution with enough returns, one may have to accept more volatility instead of paying for protection at small inflection points.

## [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409]]
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C02-2|MT190-C02-2]] — Retail access to shorting via futures is dangerous because short losses are unlimited (takeover risk).
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C03-1|MT190-C03-1]] — A Sins Capital ranking placed managed-futures CTAs 15th of 17 on blow-up risk, vol-targeted diversified macro second lowest and short-vol/short-gamma the worst.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C03-2|MT190-C03-2]] — CTAs and vol-driven macro are defensive with upside bias in a blow-up, but not instantly protected, unlike short volatility which is dangerous.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C03-3|MT190-C03-3]] — IBM fell about 25% in a day (from 332 to 213 in six weeks), showing single-stock gap risk.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C03-X1|MT190-C03-X1]] — Long-only equity exposure is not low blow-up risk: a long-only Nasdaq position fell about 85% in the 2000-2002 bust, so a low blow-up ranking for long-only equity is questionable.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C06-2|MT190-C06-2]] — Trading the front contract captures most risk, with lowest cost and highest liquidity; most of the industry uses the front contract history as signal.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C07-1|MT190-C07-1]] — The front contract explains 90-99% of risk in financial futures but less in commodities; trade deferred contracts only to capture different risk factors.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C07-X1|MT190-C07-X1]] — Where adjacent contracts differ materially in volatility (natural gas summer versus winter), a trend manager can compute volatility for the specific contract or adjust position size at the roll, so risk stays constant across the roll; for most markets using the rolled front series is still valid and there is no single correct answer.
- [[MT190 - Trend Following Is Changing Faster Than Ever - Systematic Investor - Ep.409#^MT190-C17-1|MT190-C17-1]] — Equity trend diversification layers: index, industry (avoid double counting, e.g. semiconductors vs S&P), Barra-style factors, narrative; construction and normalisation are hard, but the result can be nearly uncorrelated to index trend.

## [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422]]
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C07-1|MT191-C07-1]] — A Danish public pension fund adopted a leveraged risk-parity-type strategy near the low in interest rates and suffered massive losses as rates rose.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C08-X1|MT191-C08-X1]] — Frequency matters: a systematic trend strategy shorts bonds only when data show opportunity and drops the position when it ends, so it carries no standing view, whereas a discretionary person whose job is to be short bonds permanently faces a far harder task.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C09-1|MT191-C09-1]] — Crisis alpha (profit during market stress) requires three traits: unbiased, opportunistic and liquid; today's stresses are inflation and geopolitical risk, not the same as past ones.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C09-2|MT191-C09-2]] — The risk-mitigation framework classes assets as first responders (react instantly to a shock), second responders (trend, reacts over weeks to months) and diversifiers (robust), and the portfolio needs each for geopolitical and inflation threats.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C10-3|MT191-C10-3]] — The risk-mitigation question should be asked against inflation as the threat, not only equity drawdowns, because portfolios hold many bonds and equities can be hurt by a big enough inflation shock.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C10-X1|MT191-C10-X1]] — The AQR inflation paper's practical lesson, as Niels reads it, is to build structural protection against inflation (diversified responders) ahead of time instead of forecasting inflation, which is why investors find it hard to act.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C12-3|MT191-C12-3]] — Commodities have the inflation correlation wanted but, unlike bonds, no positive carry and high volatility, so they need active management to avoid extreme outcomes.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C12-4|MT191-C12-4]] — Commodity ETFs can perform poorly because of roll yield costs and their construction, so implementation needs expert care.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C13-4|MT191-C13-4]] — Institutions that hold a trend allocation for long use rebalancing (take profit into equities, add after drawdowns), which may do as well as or better than the index.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C16-2|MT191-C16-2]] — Combining equities with trend in one portable-alpha product suits investors who need equity-like returns and diversification but cannot hold trend separately.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C16-3|MT191-C16-3]] — Buying the dip or rebalancing trend against equities works reasonably well, showing cohesive portfolio characteristics.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C17-1|MT191-C17-1]] — A portable equity-plus-trend product lags equities sometimes when equities are up but is still up, and trend limits losses when equities fall.
- [[MT191 - Trend Following Rewards Investors Who Wait - Systematic Investor - Ep.422#^MT191-C17-2|MT191-C17-2]] — Portable alpha could pair bonds with trend, not just equities with trend.

## [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C04-2|MT192-C04-2]] — A paper on adding negative correlation shows that for a long-term allocation an asset with lower Sharpe but low or negative correlation to the existing portfolio can add more risk-adjusted return than a higher-Sharpe asset (transcript says 'higher correlation', probably a slip).
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C04-3|MT192-C04-3]] — A negatively correlated long-term strategy improves the portfolio's risk-adjusted return even if it is flat or loses money, because it lowers overall portfolio volatility.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C05-1|MT192-C05-1]] — High-volatility negatively correlated strategies are capital efficient: even small allocations make a meaningful hedging impact.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C05-2|MT192-C05-2]] — In the paper's two-year example, a high-vol manager with about -30 percent equity correlation but lower standalone returns added more Sharpe to an equity portfolio than a multistrat with 12 percent correlation and stronger returns.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C06-1|MT192-C06-1]] — Fast trend programs run about 1 to 2 billion and face capacity limits because of trading churn; option-based defensive strategies also have capacity limits.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C06-4|MT192-C06-4]] — A 50/50 mix of the S&P 500 and a Dunn trend program is near all-time highs, with a return close to the sum rather than the average of the two parts.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C06-X1|MT192-C06-X1]] — When improving an equity portfolio, investors typically screen candidates on standalone Sharpe, but they should look for the asset that de-risks the portfolio, for example one with negative correlation at the right time, even if its own return looks modest.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C07-1|MT192-C07-1]] — Trend following is zero correlated to the traded assets by construction because long and short are symmetric, unlike a defensive strategy whose negative correlation may not persist.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C07-2|MT192-C07-2]] — A tail-protect strategy only works if the puts are bought continuously; skipping them to save money removes protection at the crisis.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C07-3|MT192-C07-3]] — Because trend is symmetric and zero correlated, it can be overlaid on any long-only exposure (Bitcoin, gold, equities, bonds) and stays additive.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C08-3|MT192-C08-3]] — From 1990 to 2025 a 60/40 equity/CTA mix lifted Sharpe from 0.49 for the S&P 500 to 0.64; gold gave 0.56.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C08-6|MT192-C08-6]] — A 60 percent equity portfolio with the 40 percent split equally into gold, Treasuries and CTAs gave the best Sharpe, 0.72.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C09-1|MT192-C09-1]] — With regimes changing, diversifying across several liquid risk-mitigating strategies makes sense and can be implemented with ETFs.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C09-6|MT192-C09-6]] — Even assuming a forward Sharpe of 0.7 for 60/40 and 0.3 for CTA, uncorrelated assets imply close to 30 percent of portfolio risk in trend.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C11-4|MT192-C11-4]] — A CTA adds value by raising Sharpe in each market slightly through execution, nonlinear filters, fast/slow switching, cost reduction and risk management.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C12-3|MT192-C12-3]] — Trend following produced large gains in 2008 while other assets lost, an early demonstration of crisis alpha.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C14-1|MT192-C14-1]] — With similar raw Sharpe and zero correlation, combining trend with 60/40 improves overall and bear-market Sharpe and the efficient frontier is easy to find.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C15-1|MT192-C15-1]] — Volatility estimation and risk scaling was the major innovation in trend following.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C15-3|MT192-C15-3]] — Tilt between fast and slow signals for portfolio diversification, not standalone alpha.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C15-4|MT192-C15-4]] — Signals that currently hedge the rest of the trend book (negative correlation) deserve more risk, which many CTAs do not do.
- [[MT192 - Trend Following Still Works — So Why Do Allocators Ignore It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT192-C16-1|MT192-C16-1]] — With oil switching rapidly, overweight fast momentum in that market because it diversifies a slow-trend book, not because of higher alpha.

## [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297]]
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C09-4|MT193-C09-4]] — Blending trend with quant macro should diversify, but allocators must check how much hidden trend exposure each program has and watch concentration risk.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C10-1|MT193-C10-1]] — Quant macro relies on relationships between markets and economic data that can break, as in Covid when markets moved ahead of fundamentals; relative-value books also concentrate exposures.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C12-2|MT193-C12-2]] — A trend overlay gives equity exposure with a mechanical rule to cut it when the trend turns, solving the problem of when to leave an expensive equity bull market.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C13-3|MT193-C13-3]] — Combining equally weighted trend (Dunn) and S&P 500 gives almost the sum, not the average, of the two returns because of compounding and diversification.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C14-1|MT193-C14-1]] — Trend following lets investors hold equities because it hedges when equity trends turn (Virginia Retirement System lesson).
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C15-3|MT193-C15-3]] — Multi-manager success comes from combining uncorrelated strategies with leverage; old fund-of-funds had no leverage, so they needed high Sharpe at 3 to 4 percent vol.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C15-X2|MT193-C15-X2]] — The usual explanations for multi-manager outperformance are that platforms attract and pay talent better and cut managers quickly through risk management; Hurst's paper stresses instead the structural construction of leveraged, uncorrelated strategies.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C16-1|MT193-C16-1]] — Twenty strategies with Sharpe 0.7 and correlation of 0 to 0.1 produce a very high Sharpe once leveraged.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C16-3|MT193-C16-3]] — When correlations rise, platform volatility rises and capital must be pulled from managers to hit target volatility.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C16-4|MT193-C16-4]] — Tail correlation, not average correlation, determines multi-manager risk.
- [[MT193 - Trend Following as a Return Enhancement Overlay - Systematic Investor 297#^MT193-C16-8|MT193-C16-8]] — Platforms actively offset common pod exposures (e.g. many pods long Apple) to control factor concentration.

## [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344]]
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C05-X2|MT194-C05-X2]] — On multi-sigma days it feels like things are in slow motion and emotions push for action, so a rules-based process (watch the data, follow the process) is what tends to work in extreme environments; she responds to drawdowns by rerunning the data rather than reacting.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C09-1|MT194-C09-1]] — Crisis alpha is a search for strategies that may do well in different crises, not a guarantee, and different crises need different tools.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C09-2|MT194-C09-2]] — The Makita first/second/third responder framework: build a portfolio with attributes that catch different phases of a crisis rather than one tool.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C09-3|MT194-C09-3]] — Chaos or crisis alpha can come from volatility strategies or even cash.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C09-4|MT194-C09-4]] — Long US Treasuries, a classic first responder, also failed in April 2025, showing classifications give no guarantee.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C10-3|MT194-C10-3]] — Managers reduce dispersion risk by aggregating several trend measures and adjusting for changing volatility, though shocks remain unpredictable.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C11-4|MT194-C11-4]] — Simple single-parameter implementations create more return dispersion, so robust managers diversify parameters and methods.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C11-5|MT194-C11-5]] — Static versus dynamic position sizing makes a big difference in short crisis periods; dynamic worked in the last two or three years but is painful at turns.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C12-2|MT194-C12-2]] — Adding trend to a full portfolio can improve upside and reduce downside, motivating return-stacking products (Standpoint, Cory Hoffstein).
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C13-2|MT194-C13-2]] — Shorter-term trend has lower Sharpe recently but better diversification, which helps when added to equities.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C13-4|MT194-C13-4]] — Combining equities and trend changes the objective: it becomes a portfolio construction and rebalancing problem rather than standalone best Sharpe.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C13-X1|MT194-C13-X1]] — In a stacked portfolio of equities plus trend, the two are not independent: when trend is itself fully long equities the combination doubles equity exposure, so the pair needs joint risk management and rebalancing rather than simple addition.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C14-1|MT194-C14-1]] — Instead of naive portable alpha stacking, risk-manage equities and trend jointly.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C16-3|MT194-C16-3]] — When stocks and bonds are driven by inflation or rates they are positively correlated, so being long both is more risk and correlation is needed to see where the risk is.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C16-4|MT194-C16-4]] — Correlation between US assets and equities was positive early in the April 2025 selloff and then flipped, showing correlation is volatile.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C17-2|MT194-C17-2]] — A risk model with a long-term negative stock-bond correlation underestimates risk when the correlation turns positive.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C17-3|MT194-C17-3]] — Some CTAs ignore correlation and use entries and stops, which still exposes them but protects with stops.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C17-5|MT194-C17-5]] — Position sizing by volatility balances risk across assets (10 percent vol currencies vs 40 percent commodities means 4:1), like risk parity.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C18-1|MT194-C18-1]] — When bond volatility doubled in 2022 volatility sizing forced trend to rethink risk per asset.
- [[MT194 - Trend Following during Trump Tariff Turmoil! - Systematic Investor 344#^MT194-C18-4|MT194-C18-4]] — The practical vol-estimation question is how much to include shocks and jumps, as in the nickel event.

## [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326]]
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C06-1|MT195-C06-1]] — Replication is useful for managed futures exposure but is better combined with bottom-up implementation and risk management.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C09-1|MT195-C09-1]] — Quantica's data: volatility scaling gives higher Sharpe, while static sizing gives higher return at higher volatility and lower Sharpe, so both camps are right.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C10-1|MT195-C10-1]] — Choose between capturing the most return from rare trends with reversal vulnerability or a smoother, more risk-managed ride.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C11-2|MT195-C11-2]] — For risk-mitigation seekers static sizing offers more long-vol correlation, aggressive sizing gives better risk-adjusted return; a mix of both may suit investors.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C11-X1|MT195-C11-X1]] — A discretionary macro manager concentrates risk in a high-conviction idea by levering up, whereas quant trend programs spread risk statistically, so concentration is a philosophical split between discretionary and systematic styles.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C12-X1|MT195-C12-X1]] — Pre-crisis portable alpha mostly paired equity beta with another equity-type alpha strategy, which shared the same risk and failed in 2008; today's version pairs equity beta with managed futures that are diversifying.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C13-1|MT195-C13-1]] — In portable alpha, alpha is only relative to the risks removed; investors assume the alpha is segregated from risk when it is not.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C13-2|MT195-C13-2]] — Managed futures can be long equities, so a portable alpha stack has correlation risk and adds to losses in a recession or drawdown.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C13-4|MT195-C13-4]] — Equities plus trend gives a smoother global portfolio while retaining equity exposure.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C13-X1|MT195-C13-X1]] — Combining equities with trend improves Sharpe, which suggests a trend program could hold a constant 100 percent long S&P future and modulate around it; this is effectively the same as portable alpha, leaving packaging as the practical difference.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C14-5|MT195-C14-5]] — Pensions solved classification by creating a risk-mitigation bucket with a specific mandate for trend and long vol.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C15-3|MT195-C15-3]] — To blend trend with equities, managers dynamically size trend, cap equity beta in the trend book or accept independence.
- [[MT195 - Trend Following in 2025- New Challenges, New Strategies! - Systematic Investor 326#^MT195-C15-4|MT195-C15-4]] — Portable alpha has months when equities and trend both lose, producing very tough months.

## [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346]]
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C04-3|MT196-C04-3]] — The largest trend followers lost from about 1% to 10% in April 2025, leaving year-to-date results between slightly up and down 15-18%.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C04-6|MT196-C04-6]] — April 2025 outcomes may differ materially between hard-stop trend systems and continuous-signal systems.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C05-3|MT196-C05-3]] — A stop-based trend follower who is stopped out cannot re-enter until a new signal, so can miss the bounce, while a continuous-signal approach keeps participating.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C06-2|MT196-C06-2]] — A tight stop gets triggered near the lows, booking a loss and leaving a flat position, producing a sharp drop then flat P&L curve.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C06-3|MT196-C06-3]] — Variable position sizing that adjusts positions daily to risk automatically cut Rob's risk to about a third of normal (less in equities) as the trend became unclear, so his P&L dropped then went sideways.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C06-4|MT196-C06-4]] — Variable position sizing makes return distributions closer to normal with fewer extreme outliers, while stops make outliers (both big winners and big losers) more likely.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C06-X1|MT196-C06-X1]] — Dispersion among trend followers in a V-shaped reversal has two separate drivers that should not be conflated: signal speed, and the position-capture method (hard or wide stops versus continuously risk-adjusted sizing).
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C07-1|MT196-C07-1]] — Stop-based systems give larger outliers; the upside outliers are desired for positive skew but downside outliers occur when closing at the wrong moment or when an oversized position (e.g. cocoa) moves against you.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C07-3|MT196-C07-3]] — Funds that can make 100%+ in good years can lose 30-60% in bad years; whether that is Kelly-optimal matters less than whether investors can stomach it.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C07-X1|MT196-C07-X1]] — Early April 2025 data suggested most trend managers' risk-management systems worked as designed, so a down month for those who followed their process is within the normal range and not evidence that the strategy has broken.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C09-2|MT196-C09-2]] — Rob's worst year in his own trading was about -15%, a roughly one-in-10-year event; the 2024-25 tax year drawdown was driven by a few short sharp legs (about 12% over 3 weeks tied to the yen), not by a steady bleed.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C10-2|MT196-C10-2]] — Over the year to end-March/April 2025 the SG CTA index was about -10% versus Rob at about -14.7% on much higher volatility, so vol-adjusted his performance was acceptable.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C12-X1|MT196-C12-X1]] — Rob's answer to trend underperformance is to keep trend (about 50-60% of his portfolio) and add non-trend systems he is confident in for diversification, rather than abandoning trend or tinkering with it.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C19-1|MT196-C19-1]] — The one quadruple-leveraged S&P 500 ETF was down 52% year to date versus S&P down about 4.5%, because daily reset compounding makes adverse moves devastating.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C19-2|MT196-C19-2]] — Leveraged ETFs carry high expense ratios (about 295bp) and are toxic as holding instruments; multiples of 2-4x on already risky assets exceed Kelly-criterion sizing.
- [[MT196 - Trend Following in Crisis- - Systematic Investor 346#^MT196-C19-X1|MT196-C19-X1]] — Leveraged ETFs stack fixed 2x-4x multiples (long and inverse) on already very volatile assets such as Bitcoin, semiconductors, South Korea and single stocks, the opposite of volatility-scaled sizing; with daily reset they compound the leverage and lose heavily in volatile, choppy markets.

## [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314]]
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C01-2|MT197-C01-2]] — Financial engineering can create the perception of liquidity for inherently illiquid assets, which is a risk.
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C07-4|MT197-C07-4]] — True diversification (about 100 markets in a trend portfolio rather than 100 stocks) helps against the unknown and unknowable.
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C07-X1|MT197-C07-X1]] — Under the KUU framework, the more of the world one judges to be unknown or unknowable (and managers unable to know more than the market), the more sensible passive indexing is; Niels counters that the real lesson is true diversification (about 100 markets), which works for both active and passive.
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C08-1|MT197-C08-1]] — Long-only is not enough when stock-bond correlation is positive; trend followers who go long and short across asset classes handle the unknown best.
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C10-1|MT197-C10-1]] — Following the model is usually better than overriding it in a one-off situation, though a mechanism for override exists.
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C15-2|MT197-C15-2]] — Systematic managers backtest over long history, so a plan exists for equity declines (sell/short) even if the manager has not lived through a bear market.
- [[MT197 - Trend Following over the next few years - Systematic Investor 314#^MT197-C17-1|MT197-C17-1]] — Replicators carry manager risk too: they are small firms whose models could be flawed.

## [[MT198 - Trend Following to Super Determinism - Systematic Investor 342]]
- [[MT198 - Trend Following to Super Determinism - Systematic Investor 342#^MT198-C14-1|MT198-C14-1]] — Trend followers diversify widely rather than specialise, because the seed of a trend emerges from what looks like noise and signals identify emergent structure; noise is just complexity we cannot resolve.
- [[MT198 - Trend Following to Super Determinism - Systematic Investor 342#^MT198-C15-1|MT198-C15-1]] — An embedded trader changes the system by trading (larger funds move prices more), so one must be humble and follow a rules-based process rather than predict or control.

## [[MT199 - Trend Following too complicated- - Systematic Investor 296]]
- [[MT199 - Trend Following too complicated- - Systematic Investor 296#^MT199-C12-2|MT199-C12-2]] — VUCA (volatility, uncertainty, complexity, ambiguity) is a framework for risk; current equity volatility is low and bond volatility, though high, has declined, making it a lower-risk environment surprisingly.
- [[MT199 - Trend Following too complicated- - Systematic Investor 296#^MT199-C14-2|MT199-C14-2]] — Matching VUCA to design: diversify for volatility, stop losses for uncertainty, simple models with added factors for complexity, ensembles for ambiguity (e.g. mixing long- and short-term trend).

## [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316]]
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C04-2|MT200-C04-2]] — From Q2 2024 the reversal of the short fixed-income trade, plus correlation-based risk management, reduced equity exposure over the summer before the August reversal.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C04-X1|MT200-C04-X1]] — Correlation-aware risk management cut equity risk in May-June while the short bond trade was reversing (two positively correlated longs), but as rates exposure was reduced approaching August the freed risk budget went back into equities just before the August reversal, so a risk-budget rule can add to the position that is about to reverse.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C06-2|MT200-C06-2]] — Choice of speed is a trade-off: slower means less reactivity (missing turning points), faster means more false positives and higher costs, and going faster requires strong cost control.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C06-X1|MT200-C06-X1]] — In the very fast August 2024 reversal, trend models cut exposure mainly through volatility scaling (volatility expansion), not through the trend signal changing within those few days.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C07-1|MT200-C07-1]] — Static position sizing would have helped in the August 2024 V shape because exposure would not be reduced into the trough, but this is data mining and not a lesson.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C07-2|MT200-C07-2]] — Longer lookbacks give more performance in meaningful, long equity crises; short-lived dips are too quick for trend following, which is the price of long-term compound growth, and overthinking models to remove bad periods ends badly.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C07-3|MT200-C07-3]] — Short moves of a day, two days or two weeks are not trend following's domain; combining options protection with trend following is possible but more expensive.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C07-X1|MT200-C07-X1]] — August 2024 was not a test of trend following's convexity (the 'smile' scatter of trend returns against equity returns): the downturn was too short-lived and the month ended positive, so convexity would only have paid if the move had been contagious and a fast model had reacted.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C09-3|MT200-C09-3]] — A 60/40 portfolio carries about 90% equity risk and 10% bond risk, and in down markets risk rises without compensation, while the stock-bond correlation turned positive in 2022; hence scale exposure as a function of realised volatility.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C09-4|MT200-C09-4]] — Reducing exposure in a vol spike implicitly plays a trend; early trend followers used static sizing, but technology allows dynamic sizing.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C10-2|MT200-C10-2]] — Volatility clusters and mean reverts, so over long horizons static and dynamic exposure converge, but scaling by volatility matters locally around turning points; spikes have shown negative association with subsequent returns.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C11-1|MT200-C11-1]] — In a risk parity setup correlation only matters for top-level leverage under a vol target; when stocks and bonds correlate positively (2022) volatility overshoots, so incorporate correlation if it is not too noisy.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C11-2|MT200-C11-2]] — The paper moves from nominal allocation to a vol-targeted risk allocation with a top-level leverage adjustment, reducing vol of vol.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C12-1|MT200-C12-1]] — Three stages: nominal allocation, static long-term risk allocation, then active risk allocation using realised volatilities and correlations, which performs best, similar to risk parity.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C15-1|MT200-C15-1]] — Mean-variance analysis would put about 70% in trend following because its historical Sharpe is higher than a stock-bond portfolio; minimising downside risk gives about 52% (1999-2019 data), both too high for institutions.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C15-2|MT200-C15-2]] — Sizing must balance benchmark-relative active risk (tracking error to the policy portfolio) against benefits, since institutions are judged against a benchmark.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C16-1|MT200-C16-1]] — Assess scenario analysis for increments of trend allocation (2%, 5%, 10%, 15%) with tracking error against benchmark; larger sizes improve tail, Sharpe and downside but carry career risk, and the horizon (1 vs 10 years) changes the value.
- [[MT200 - Trend Following- How much to Allocate- - Systematic Investor 316#^MT200-C16-R1|MT200-C16-R1]] — Baltas says a trend allocation should not be too small because the impact would be tiny, but the larger it becomes the more conscious institutions must be about tracking error to their benchmark.

## [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8]]
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C04-3|MT201-C04-3]] — Position sizing choices that differ across managers include constant versus flexible risk targeting and whether sizing uses only signal direction or the signal value.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C05-5|MT201-C05-5]] — Removing short signals would improve a backtest during 30 years of bull markets in bonds and equities but would destroy the crisis alpha that trend following is expected to deliver.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C06-1|MT201-C06-1]] — Volatility target is an annualised standard deviation goal met on average over the long run; the portfolio is not re-levered on days with few signals.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C06-2|MT201-C06-2]] — Each position has a risk budget or stop limit to control drawdown, complementing the symmetric standard deviation target with an asymmetric drawdown control.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C06-3|MT201-C06-3]] — When a market makes a huge move (cocoa) and the exit level lags far below price, positions are cut so one market does not dominate; limits are deliberately broad so ordinary moves do not trigger cuts.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C07-1|MT201-C07-1]] — More markets give more chances to catch outlier moves, so SMN began with about 70 markets and kept widening the universe.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C07-2|MT201-C07-2]] — Synthetic markets built as baskets or spreads of existing markets create return streams that look different from the underlying and add diversification.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C07-6|MT201-C07-6]] — Alternative (illiquid, regional) markets move on local factors rather than the global risk-on/risk-off regime, so they correlate little with a classic futures portfolio.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C08-4|MT201-C08-4]] — Trends in alternative markets occur at different times from classic-market trends, lowering correlation and improving diversification.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C08-6|MT201-C08-6]] — Slippage assumed for the smallest markets (nonfat dry milk, cheese) is roughly four to five times that of classic futures.
- [[MT201 - Trend Following...Austrian Style - Open Interest 8#^MT201-C09-2|MT201-C09-2]] — The first synthetic markets (2009) were risk-parity baskets of an equity index and a government bond, for example S&P versus 10-year note.
